66 Chapter 5 Classical Iterative Methods

It is easy to see that  $ I - D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2} $ symmetric and similar to  $ B_J $. Then the eigenvalues of  $ B_J $ are real.

Now, we first suppose that the Jacobi method converges, then  $ \rho(B_J) < 1 $ by Theorem 5.2. The absolute value of the eigenvalues of  $ I - D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2} $ is less than 1, i.e., the eigenvalues of  $ D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2} $ lies on (0, 2). Thus A is positive definite. On the other hand, the eigenvalues of  $ 2I - D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2} $ are positive, so the matrix  $ 2I - D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2} $ is positive definite. Since  $ D^{-1/2}(2D - A)D^{-1/2} = 2I - D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2} $, we know that 2D - A is positive definite too. Conversely, since  $ D^{-1/2}(I - B_J)D^{-1/2} = D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2} $ and A is positive definite, it follows that the eigenvalues of  $ I - B_J $ are positive, i.e., the eigenvalues of  $ B_J $ are less than 1. Because 2D - A is positive definite and  $ D^{-1/2}(2D - A)D^{-1/2} = D^{1/2}(I + B_J)D^{-1/2} $, we can deduce that the eigenvalues of  $ I + B_J $ are positive, i.e., the eigenvalues of  $ B_J $ are greater than -1. Thus  $ \rho(B_J) < 1 $. By Theorem 5.2 again, the Jacobi method converges.

Theorem 5.6 Let A be a symmetric positive definite matrix. Then the Gauss-Seidel method converges for any initial approximation  $ \mathbf{x}_0 $.

Proof Let  $ \lambda $ be an eigenvalue of the iteration matrix  $ B_{GS} $ and u the corresponding eigenvector. Then

 $ (D - L)^{-1}U\mathbf{u} = \lambda\mathbf{u} $.

Since A is symmetric positive definite, we have  $ U = L^T $ and  $ \lambda(D - L)\mathbf{u} = L^T\mathbf{u} $.

Therefore,  $ \lambda\mathbf{u}^*(D - L)\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{u}^*L^T\mathbf{u} $.

Let  $ \mathbf{u}^*D\mathbf{u} = \delta $,  $ \mathbf{u}^*L\mathbf{u} = \alpha + i\beta $.

5.2 Convergence analysis

67

We then have

 $ \mathbf{u}^*L^T\mathbf{u} = (L\mathbf{u})^*\mathbf{u} = \overline{\mathbf{u}^*L\mathbf{u}} = \alpha - \mathbf{i}\beta $.

Thus

 $ \lambda[\delta - (\alpha + \mathbf{i}\beta)] = \alpha - \mathbf{i}\beta $.

Taking the modulus of both sides, we have

 $ |\lambda|^2 = \frac{\alpha^2 + \beta^2}{(\delta - \alpha)^2 + \beta^2} $.

On the other hand,

 $ 0 < \mathbf{u}^*A\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{u}^*(D - L - L^T)\mathbf{u} = \delta - 2\alpha $.

Hence,

 $ (\delta - \alpha)^2 + \beta^2 = \delta^2 + \alpha^2 + \beta^2 - 2\delta\alpha $

 $ = \delta(\delta - 2\alpha) + \alpha^2 + \beta^2 $

 $ > \alpha^2 + \beta^2 $.

So we get  $ |\lambda| < 1 $. By Theorem 5.2, the Gauss-Seidel method converges.

Definition 5.2 A matrix A is called irreducible if there is no permutation matrix P such that

 $ PAP^T = \begin{bmatrix} A_{11} & A_{12} \\ 0 & A_{22} \end{bmatrix} $,

where  $ A_{11} $ and  $ A_{22} $ are square matrices.

We have the following lemma [85].

Lemma 5.1 If a matrix A is strictly diagonally dominant; or irreducible and weakly diagonally dominant, then A is nonsingular.

Furthermore,

Theorem 5.7 We have

(i) If A is strictly diagonally dominant, then both the Jacobi and the Gauss-Seidel methods converge. In fact,

 $ \|B_{GS}\|_{\infty} \leqslant \|B_J\|_{\infty} < 1 $.

(ii) If A is irreducible and weakly diagonally dominant, then both the Jacobi and the Gauss-Seidel methods converge. Moreover,

 $ \rho(B_{GS}) < \rho(B_J) < 1 $.

For the proof of this theorem, we refer to [30,85].

68 Chapter 5 Classical Iterative Methods

5.3 Convergence rate

We consider a linear stationary iteration method,

 $ \mathbf{x}_{k+1} = M\mathbf{x}_k + \mathbf{g} $, \quad  $ k = 0, 1, \ldots $,

where  $ M $ is an  $ n $-by- $ n $ matrix. If  $ I - M $ is nonsingular, then there exists a unique solution of

 $ (I - M)\mathbf{x}_* = \mathbf{g} $.

The error vectors  $ \mathbf{y}_k $ are defined as  $ \mathbf{y}_k = \mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_* $ for  $ k = 0, 1, \ldots $, and then

 $ \mathbf{y}_k = M\mathbf{y}_{k-1} = \cdots = M^k\mathbf{y}_0 $.

Using matrix and vector norms, we have

 $ \|\mathbf{y}_k\| \leq \|M^k\|\| \mathbf{y}_0\| $

with the equality possible for each  $ k $ for some vector  $ \mathbf{y}_0 $. Thus, if  $ \mathbf{y}_0 $ is not the zero vector, then  $ \|M^k\| $ gives us a sharp upper-bound estimate for the ratio  $ \|\mathbf{y}_k\| / \|\mathbf{y}_0\| $. Since the initial vector  $ \mathbf{y}_0 $ is unknown in practical problems,  $ \|M^k\| $ serves as a measurement of comparison of different iterative methods.

Definition 5.3 Let  $ M $ be an  $ n $-by- $ n $ iteration matrix. If  $ \|M^k\| < 1 $ for some positive integer  $ k $, then

 $ R_k(M) \equiv -\ln[(\|M^k\|)^{1/k}] = -\frac{\ln \|M^k\|}{k} $

is called the average rate of convergence for  $ k $ iterations of  $ M $.

In terms of actual computations, the significance of the average rate of convergence  $ R_k(M) $ is given as follows. Clearly, the quantity

 $ \sigma = \left( \frac{\|\mathbf{y}_k\|}{\|\mathbf{y}_0\|} \right)^{1/k} $

is the average reduction factor per iteration for the norm of error. If  $ \|M^k\| < 1 $, then by Definition 5.3, we have

 $ \sigma \leq (\|M^k\|)^{1/k} = e^{-R_k(M)}, $

where  $ e $ is the base of the natural logarithm.

If  $ M $ is symmetric (or Hermitian, or normal, i.e.,  $ MM^* = M^*M $), by using the spectral radius of the iteration matrix  $ M $, we then have

 $ \|M^k\|_2 = [\rho(M)]^k $,

5.4 SOR method

69

and thus,

 $ R_k(M) = -\ln \rho(M) $,

which is independent of k.

Next we consider the asymptotic convergence rate

 $ R_\infty(M) \equiv \lim_{k \to \infty} R_k(M) $.

Theorem 5.8 We have

 $ R_\infty(M) = -\ln \rho(M) $.

Proof We only need to prove that

 $ \lim_{k \to \infty} \|M^k\|^{1/k} = \rho(M) $.

Since

 $ [\rho(M)]^k = \rho(M^k) \leqslant \|M^k\| $,

we have

 $ \rho(M) \leqslant \|M^k\|^{1/k} $.

On the other hand, for any  $ \epsilon > 0 $, consider the matrix

 $ B_\epsilon = \frac{1}{\rho(M) + \epsilon} M $.

It is obvious that  $ \rho(B_\epsilon) < 1 $ and then  $ \lim_{k \to \infty} B_\epsilon^k = 0 $. Hence, there exists a natural number  $ K $, for  $ k \geq K $, we have

 $ \|B_\epsilon^k\| \leq 1 $,

i.e.,

 $ \|M^k\| \leq [\rho(M) + \epsilon]^k $.

Thus,

 $ \rho(M) \leq \|M^k\|^{1/k} \leq \rho(M) + \epsilon $,

which means

 $ \lim_{k \to \infty} \|M^k\|^{1/k} = \rho(M) $. □

5.4 SOR method

The Gauss-Seidel method is very slow when  $ \rho(B_{GS}) $ is close to unity. However, the convergence rate of the Gauss-Seidel iteration, in certain cases, can be improved by introducing a parameter  $ \omega $, known as the relaxation parameter. This method is called the successive overrelaxation (SOR) method.

70 Chapter 5 Classical Iterative Methods

5.4.1 Iterative form

Let  $  A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $ and  $  A = D - L - U  $ defined as in Subsection 5.1.1. Consider the solution of  $  Ax = b  $ again. The motivation of the SOR method is to improve the Gauss-Seidel iteration by taking an appropriately weighted average of the  $  x_i^{(k)}  $ and  $  x_i^{(k+1)}  $ yielding the following algorithm

 $$  x_i^{(k+1)} = (1 - \omega) x_i^{(k)} + \omega \left( \sum_{j=1}^{i-1} c_{ij} x_j^{(k+1)} + \sum_{j=i+1}^{n} c_{ij} x_j^{(k)} + g_i \right)  $$ 

(5.8)

Here  $  D^{-1}(L + U) = [c_{ij}]  $,  $  \mathbf{x}_k = \left[ x_1^{(k)}, x_2^{(k)}, \ldots, x_n^{(k)} \right]^T  $, and  $  \mathbf{g} = D^{-1}\mathbf{b} = [g_1, g_2, \ldots, g_n]^T  $. In matrix form, we have

 $$ 

\mathbf{x}_{k+1} = L_{\omega} \mathbf{x}_k + \omega (D - \omega L)^{-1} \mathbf{b}

 $$ 

where

 $  L_{\omega} = (D - \omega L)^{-1} [(1 - \omega) D + \omega U]  $

is called the iteration matrix of the SOR method and  $  \omega  $ is the relaxation parameter. We have three cases depending on the values of  $  \omega  $:

(1) if  $  \omega = 1  $, (5.8) is equivalent to the Gauss-Seidel method;

(2) if  $  \omega < 1  $, (5.8) is called underrelaxation;

(3) if  $  \omega > 1  $, (5.8) is called overrelaxation.

5.4.2 Convergence criteria

It is natural to ask for which range of  $  \omega  $ the SOR iteration converges. To this end, we first prove the following important result [30,85].

Theorem 5.9 The SOR iteration cannot converge for any initial approximation if  $  \omega  $ lies outside the interval  $ (0,2) $.

Proof Recall that the SOR iteration matrix  $  L_\omega  $ is given by

 $  L_\omega = (D - \omega L)^{-1} [(1 - \omega) D + \omega U]  $

where  $  A = [a_{ij}] = D - L - U  $.

The matrix  $  (D - \omega L)^{-1}  $ is a lower triangular matrix with  $  1/a_{ii}  $ ( $  i = 1,2,\ldots,n  $) as diagonal entries, and the matrix

 $ (1 - \omega) D + \omega U $

5.4 SOR method 71

is an upper triangular matrix with  $ (1-\omega)a_{ii} $ ( $ i=1,2,\ldots,n $) as diagonal entries. Therefore,

 $ \det(L_\omega)=(1-\omega)^n $

Since the determinant of a matrix is equal to the product of its eigenvalues, we conclude that

 $ \rho(L_\omega)\geq|1-\omega| $

where  $ \rho(L_\omega) $ is the spectral radius of  $ L_\omega $

By Theorem 5.2, the spectral radius of the iteration matrix should be less than 1 for convergence. We then conclude that  $ 0<\omega<2 $ is required for the convergence of the SOR method. □

Theorem 5.10 If  $ A $ is symmetric positive definite, then

 $ \rho(L_\omega)<1 $

for all  $ 0<\omega<2 $

Proof Let  $ \lambda $ be any eigenvalue of the SOR iteration matrix

 $ L_\omega=(D-\omega L)^{-1}[(1-\omega)D+\omega L^T] $

and  $ \mathbf{x} $ be the corresponding eigenvector. We have

 $ [(1-\omega)D+\omega L^T]\mathbf{x}=\lambda(D-\omega L)\mathbf{x} $

or

 $ \mathbf{x}^*([1-\omega)D+\omega L^T]\mathbf{x}=\lambda\mathbf{x}^*(D-\omega L)\mathbf{x} $

Let

 $ \mathbf{x}^*D\mathbf{x}=\delta $

 $ \mathbf{x}^*L\mathbf{x}=\alpha+\mathbf{i}\beta $

Therefore,  $ \mathbf{x}^*L^T\mathbf{x}=\alpha-\mathbf{i}\beta $ and then

 $ (1-\omega)\delta+\omega(\alpha-\mathbf{i}\beta)=\lambda[\delta-\omega(\alpha+\mathbf{i}\beta)] $

Taking modulus of both sides, we obtain

 $ \left|\lambda\right|^2=\frac{[(1-\omega)\delta+\omega\alpha]^2+\omega^2\beta^2}{(\delta-\omega\alpha)^2+\omega^2\beta^2} $

(5.9)

Note that

 $$ [(1-\omega)\delta+\omega\alpha]^2+\omega^2\beta^2-(\delta-\omega\alpha)^2-\omega^2\beta^2=[\delta-\omega(\delta-\alpha)]^2-(\delta-\omega\alpha)^2 $$ 

 $$ =\omega\delta(\delta-2\alpha)(\omega-2) $$ 

72

Chapter 5 Classical Iterative Methods

Since  $  A  $ is symmetric positive definite, we have

 $ \delta > 0, \quad \delta - 2\alpha > 0 $.

Therefore, if  $  0 < \omega < 2  $, we have

 $ [(1-\omega)\delta + \omega\alpha]^{2} + \omega^{2}\beta^{2} < (\delta - \omega\alpha)^{2} + \omega^{2}\beta^{2}. $ (5.10)

Thus, for  $  0 < \omega < 2  $, we obtain by (5.9) and (5.10),

 $  |\lambda|^{2} < 1  $,

i.e., the SOR method converges. □

5.4.3 Optimal  $ \omega $ in SOR iteration

For further comparison of the Jacobi, Gauss-Seidel and SOR methods, we impose another condition on matrices. This condition allows us to compute  $ \rho(B_{GS}) $ and  $ \rho(L_{\omega}) $ explicitly in terms of  $ \rho(B_{J}) $.

Definition 5.4  $  A  $ matrix  $  M  $ has property  $  A  $ if there exists a permutation  $  P  $ such that

 $  PMP^{T} = \begin{bmatrix} D_{11} & M_{12} \\ M_{21} & D_{22} \end{bmatrix}  $,

where  $  D_{11}  $ and  $  D_{22}  $ are diagonal matrices.

If  $  M  $ has property  $  A  $, then we can write

 $  PMP^{T} = \widehat{D} - \widehat{L} - \widehat{U}  $,

where

 $ \widehat{D} = \begin{bmatrix} D_{11} & 0 \\ 0 & D_{22} \end{bmatrix}, \quad \widehat{L} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ -M_{21} & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad \widehat{U} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -M_{12} \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}. $

Let

 $ \widehat{B_{J}}(\alpha) \equiv \alpha\widehat{D}^{-1}\widehat{L} + \frac{1}{\alpha}\widehat{D}^{-1}\widehat{U} $.

We have

Theorem 5.11 The eigenvalues of  $ \widehat{B_{J}}(\alpha) $ are independent of  $ \alpha $.

Proof Just note that the matrix

 $ \widehat{B_{J}}(\alpha) = -\begin{bmatrix} 0 & \frac{1}{\alpha}D_{11}^{-1}M_{12} \\ \alpha D_{22}^{-1}M_{21} & 0 \end{bmatrix} $

is similar to the matrix

 $ \begin{bmatrix} I & 0 \\ 0 & \alpha I \end{bmatrix}^{-1}\widehat{B_{J}}(\alpha) \begin{bmatrix} I & 0 \\ 0 & \alpha I \end{bmatrix} = -\begin{bmatrix} 0 & D_{11}^{-1}M_{12} \\ D_{22}^{-1}M_{21} & 0 \end{bmatrix} = \widehat{B_{J}}(1). $

5.4 SOR method 73

Definition 5.5 Let  $ M = D - L - U $ and

 $ B_J(\alpha) = \alpha D^{-1} L + \frac{1}{\alpha} D^{-1} U $.

If the eigenvalues of  $ B_J(\alpha) $ are independent of  $ \alpha $, then M is called consistent ordering (or said to be consistently ordered).

Note that  $ B_J(1) = B_J $ is the Jacobi iteration matrix. From Theorem 5.11, we know that if M has property A, then  $ PMP^T $ is consistently ordered where P is a permutation matrix such that

 $ PMP^T = \begin{bmatrix} D_{11} & M_{12} \\ M_{21} & D_{22} \end{bmatrix} $

with  $ D_{11} $ and  $ D_{22} $ being diagonal. It is not true that consistent ordering implies property A.

Example 5.1 Any block tridiagonal matrix

 $$ 

\begin{bmatrix}

D_1 & A_1 \\

B_1 & \ddots & \ddots \\

\ddots & \ddots & A_{n-1} \\

B_{n-1} & D_n

\end{bmatrix}

 $$ 

is consistently ordered when  $ D_i $ are diagonal.

The following theorem gives a relation between the eigenvalues of  $ B_J $ and the eigenvalues of  $ L_\omega $ [30,85].

Theorem 5.12 If A is consistently ordered and  $ \omega \neq 0 $, then

(i) The eigenvalues of  $ B_J $ appear in  $ \pm $ pairs.

(ii) If  $ \mu $ is an eigenvalue of  $ B_J $ and  $ \lambda $ satisfies

 $ (\lambda + \omega - 1)^2 = \lambda \omega^2 \mu^2 $, (5.11) then  $ \lambda $ is an eigenvalue of  $ L_\omega $.

(iii) If  $ \lambda \neq 0 $ is an eigenvalue of  $ L_\omega $ and  $ \mu $ satisfies (5.11), then  $ \mu $ is an eigenvalue of  $ B_J $.

Corollary 5.1 If A is consistently ordered, then

 $ \rho(B_{GS}) = [\rho(B_J)]^2 $.

This means that the convergence rate of the Gauss-Seidel method is twice as fast as that of the Jacobi method.

74

Chapter 5 Classical Iterative Methods

Proof The choice of \omega = 1 for the SOR method is equivalent to the Gauss-Seidel method. Therefore, by (5.11), we have \lambda^2 = \lambda \mu^2 and then

\lambda = \mu^2. □

To get the most benefit from overrelaxation, we would like to find an optimal \omega, denoted by \omega_{opt}, minimizing \rho(L_\omega). We have the following theorem [30,85].

Theorem 5.13 Suppose that A is consistently ordered and B_J has real eigenvalues with \mu = \rho(B_J) < 1. Then

\omega_{opt} = \frac{2}{1 + \sqrt{1 - \mu^2}},

\rho(L_{\omega_{opt}}) = \frac{\mu^2}{(1 + \sqrt{1 - \mu^2})^2},

and

\omega - 1,

\rho(L_\omega) = \begin{cases} \omega - 1, & \text{if } \omega_opt \leq \omega < 2, \\ 1 - \omega + \frac{1}{2} \omega^2 \mu^2 + \omega \mu \sqrt{1 - \omega + \frac{1}{4} \omega^2 \mu^2}, & \omega_opt \leq \omega < 2, \\ 0 < \omega \leq \omega_opt, \end{cases}

For more convergence analysis of classical iterative methods, one can refer to [14,74].

Chapter 6

Krylov Subspace Methods

In this chapter, we introduce a class of iterative methods called Krylov subspace methods. Among Krylov subspace methods developed for large sparse problems, we mainly study two methods: the conjugate gradient (CG) method and the generalized minimum residual (GMRES) method. The CG method proposed by Hesten and Stiefel in 1952 is one of the best known iterative methods for solving symmetric positive definite linear systems [37]. The GMRES method was proposed by Saad and Schultz in 1986 for solving nonsymmetric linear systems [71]. As usual, let us begin our discussion from the steepest descent method.

6.1 Steepest descent method

We consider the linear system

 $ Ax = b $

where  $ A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ is a symmetric positive definite matrix,  $ \mathbf{x} = [x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n]^T \in \mathbb{R}^n $ is an unknown vector, and  $ \mathbf{b} = [b_1, b_2, \ldots, b_n]^T \in \mathbb{R}^n $ is a known vector. We define the following quadratic function

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}) \equiv \mathbf{x}^T A \mathbf{x} - 2 \mathbf{b}^T \mathbf{x} $. (6.1)

Theorem 6.1 Let  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ be a symmetric positive definite matrix. Then finding the solution of  $ Ax = b $ is equivalent to finding the minimum of function (6.1).

Proof Note that

 $ \frac{\partial \phi}{\partial x_i} = 2 (a_{i1} x_1 + \cdots + a_{in} x_n) - 2 b_i, \quad i = 1, 2, \ldots, n $.

We therefore have

 $ \text{grad } \phi(\mathbf{x}) = 2 (Ax - b) = -2 \mathbf{r} $. (6.2)

where  $ \text{grad } \phi(\mathbf{x}) $ denotes the gradient of  $ \phi(\mathbf{x}) $ and  $ \mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x} $. If  $ \phi(\mathbf{x}) $ reaches its minimum at a point  $ \mathbf{x}_* $, then

 $ \text{grad } \phi(\mathbf{x}_*) = 0 $.

6.1 Steepest descent method

77

i.e.,  $ Ax_* = b $ which means that  $ \mathbf{x}_* $ is the solution of the system.

Conversely, if  $ \mathbf{x}_* $ is the solution of the system, then for any vector  $ \mathbf{y} $, we have

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_* + \mathbf{y}) = (\mathbf{x}_* + \mathbf{y})^T A(\mathbf{x}_* + \mathbf{y}) - 2b^T(\mathbf{x}_* + \mathbf{y}) $

 $ = \mathbf{x}_*^T A \mathbf{x}_* - 2b^T \mathbf{x}_* + \mathbf{y}^T A \mathbf{y} = \phi(\mathbf{x}_*) + \mathbf{y}^T A \mathbf{y} $.

Since  $ A $ is symmetric positive definite, we have  $ \mathbf{y}^T A \mathbf{y} \geq 0 $. Hence,

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_* + \mathbf{y}) \geq \phi(\mathbf{x}_*) $,

i.e.,  $ \phi(\mathbf{x}) $ reaches its minimum at the point  $ \mathbf{x}_* $. □

How to find the minimum of (6.1)? Usually, for any given initial vector  $ \mathbf{x}_0 $, we choose a direction  $ \mathbf{p}_0 $, and then we try to find a point

 $ \mathbf{x}_1 = \mathbf{x}_0 + \alpha_0 \mathbf{p}_0 $

on the line  $ \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_0 + \alpha \mathbf{p}_0 $ such that

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_1) = \phi(\mathbf{x}_0 + \alpha_0 \mathbf{p}_0) \leq \phi(\mathbf{x}_0 + \alpha \mathbf{p}_0) $.

It means that along this line,  $ \phi(\mathbf{x}) $ reaches its minimum at point  $ \mathbf{x}_1 $. Afterwards, starting from  $ \mathbf{x}_1 $, we choose another direction  $ \mathbf{p}_1 $, and then we try to find a point

 $ \mathbf{x}_2 = \mathbf{x}_1 + \alpha_1 \mathbf{p}_1 $

on the line  $ \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_1 + \alpha \mathbf{p}_1 $ such that

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_2) = \phi(\mathbf{x}_1 + \alpha_1 \mathbf{p}_1) \leq \phi(\mathbf{x}_1 + \alpha \mathbf{p}_1) $,

i.e., along this line,  $ \phi(\mathbf{x}) $ reaches its minimum at point  $ \mathbf{x}_2 $. Step by step, we have

 $ \mathbf{p}_0, \mathbf{p}_1, \ldots $, and  $ \alpha_0, \alpha_1, \ldots $,

where  $ \{\mathbf{p}_k\} $ are line search directions and  $ \{\alpha_k\} $ are step sizes. In general, at a point  $ \mathbf{x}_k $, we choose a direction  $ \mathbf{p}_k $ and then determine a step size  $ \alpha_k $ along the line  $ \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_k + \alpha \mathbf{p}_k $ such that

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k) \leq \phi(\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha \mathbf{p}_k) $.

We then obtain  $ \mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k $. We remark that different ways for choosing line search directions and step sizes give different algorithms for solving (6.1).

78

Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

In the following, we first consider how to determine a step size  $ \alpha_k $. Starting from a point  $ \mathbf{x}_k $ along a direction  $ \mathbf{p}_k $, we want to find a step size  $ \alpha_k $ on the line  $ \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_k + \alpha \mathbf{p}_k $ such that

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k) \leq \phi(\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha \mathbf{p}_k) $.

Let

 $ f(\alpha) = \phi(\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha \mathbf{p}_k) $

 $ = (\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha \mathbf{p}_k)^T A(\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha \mathbf{p}_k) - 2 \mathbf{b}^T (\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha \mathbf{p}_k) $

 $ = \alpha^2 \mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k - 2 \alpha \mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{p}_k + \phi(\mathbf{x}_k) $,

where  $ \mathbf{r}_k = \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x}_k $. We have

 $ \frac{df}{d\alpha} = 2 \alpha \mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k - 2 \mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{p}_k = 0 $.

Therefore,

 $ \alpha_k = \frac{\mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{p}_k}{\mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k} $. (6.3)

Once we get  $ \alpha_k $, we can compute

 $ \mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k $.

Is  $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_{k+1}) \leq \phi(\mathbf{x}_k) $? We consider

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_{k+1}) - \phi(\mathbf{x}_k) = \phi(\mathbf{x}_k + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k) - \phi(\mathbf{x}_k) $

 $ = \alpha_k^2 \mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k - 2 \alpha_k \mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{p}_k $

 $ = -\frac{(\mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{p}_k)^2}{\mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k} \leq 0 $.

If  $ \mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{p}_k \neq 0 $, then  $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_{k+1}) < \phi(\mathbf{x}_k) $.

Now we consider how to choose a direction  $ \mathbf{p}_k $. It is well-known that the steepest descent direction of  $ \phi(\mathbf{x}) $ is the negative direction of the gradient, i.e.,  $ \mathbf{p}_k = \mathbf{r}_k $ by (6.2). Thus we have the steepest descent method. In order to discuss the convergence rate of the method, we introduce the following lemma first.

Lemma 6.1 Let  $ 0 < \lambda_1 \leq \cdots \leq \lambda_n $ be the eigenvalues of a symmetric positive definite matrix  $ A $ and  $ P(t) $ be a real polynomial of t. Then

 $ \|\mathbf{P}(A) \mathbf{x}\|_{A} \leq \max_{1 \leq j \leq n} |\mathbf{P}(\lambda_j)| \cdot \|\mathbf{x}\|_{A} $,  $ \mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n $,

where  $ \|\mathbf{x}\|_{A} \equiv \sqrt{\mathbf{x}^T A \mathbf{x}} $ is the energy norm.

6.1 Steepest descent method 79

Proof Let  $ \mathbf{y}_1, \mathbf{y}_2, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_n $ be the eigenvectors of  $ A $ corresponding to the eigenvalues  $ \lambda_1, \lambda_2, \ldots, \lambda_n $, accordingly. Suppose that  $ \mathbf{y}_1, \mathbf{y}_2, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_n $ also form an orthonormal basis of  $ \mathbb{R}^n $. Therefore, for any  $ \mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n $, we have  $ \mathbf{x} = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \beta_i \mathbf{y}_i $ and furthermore,

 $ \mathbf{x}^T P(A) A P(A) \mathbf{x} = \left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \beta_i P(\lambda_i) \mathbf{y}_i \right)^T A \left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \beta_i P(\lambda_i) \mathbf{y}_i \right)^T $

 $$ = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \lambda_i \beta_i^2 P^2 (\lambda_i) \leq \max_{1 \leq j \leq n} P^2 (\lambda_j) \sum_{i=1}^{n} \lambda_i \beta_i^2  $$ 

 $ = \max_{1 \leq j \leq n} P^2 (\lambda_j) \mathbf{x}^T A \mathbf{x} $.

Then

 $ \| P(A) \mathbf{x} \|_A \leq \max_{1 \leq j \leq n} | P(\lambda_j) | \cdot \| \mathbf{x} \|_A \cdot $ □

For the steepest descent method, we have the following convergence theorem.

Theorem 6.2 Let  $ 0 < \lambda_1 \leqslant \cdots \leqslant \lambda_n $ be the eigenvalues of a symmetric positive definite matrix  $ A $. Then the sequence  $ \{\mathbf{x}_k\} $ produced by the steepest descent method satisfies

 $ \|\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_* \|_A \leqslant \left( \frac{\lambda_n - \lambda_1}{\lambda_n + \lambda_1} \right)^k \| \mathbf{x}_0 - \mathbf{x}_* \|_A $,

where  $ \mathbf{x}_* $ is the exact solution of  $ A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b} $.

Proof The  $ \mathbf{x}_k $ produced by the steepest descent method satisfies

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_k) \leqslant \phi(\mathbf{x}_{k-1} + \alpha \mathbf{r}_{k-1}), \quad \alpha \in \mathbb{R} $.

By noting that

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}) + \mathbf{x}_*^T A \mathbf{x}_* = (\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_*)^T A (\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_*) $,

we have

 $ \left( \mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*\right)^T A (\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*) \leqslant (\mathbf{x}_{k-1} + \alpha \mathbf{r}_{k-1} - \mathbf{x}_*)^T A (\mathbf{x}_{k-1} + \alpha \mathbf{r}_{k-1} - \mathbf{x}_*) $

 $$ = \left[ (I - \alpha A) (\mathbf{x}_{k-1} - \mathbf{x}_*) \right]^T A \left[ (I - \alpha A) (\mathbf{x}_{k-1} - \mathbf{x}_*) \right], (6.4) $

for any  $ \alpha \in \mathbb{R} $. Let  $ P_\alpha(t) = 1 - \alpha t $. By using Lemma 6.1, we have from (6.4),

 $ \|\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*\|_A \leqslant \| P_\alpha(A) (\mathbf{x}_{k-1} - \mathbf{x}_*) \|_A $

 $ \leqslant \max_{1 \leqslant j \leqslant n} | P_\alpha(\lambda_j) | \cdot \|\mathbf{x}_{k-1} - \mathbf{x}_*\|_A,\quad (6.5) $

80 Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

for any  $ \alpha \in \mathbb{R} $. By using properties of the Chebyshev approximation [70], we have

 $ \min_{\alpha} \max_{\lambda_1 \leq t \leq \lambda_n} |1 - \alpha t| = \frac{\lambda_n - \lambda_1}{\lambda_n + \lambda_1} $. (6.6)

Substituting (6.6) into (6.5), we obtain

 $ \|\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*\|_A \leq \frac{\lambda_n - \lambda_1}{\lambda_n + \lambda_1} \|\mathbf{x}_{k-1} - \mathbf{x}_*\|_A $.

Thus,

 $ \|\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*\|_A \leq \left( \frac{\lambda_n - \lambda_1}{\lambda_n + \lambda_1} \right)^k \|\mathbf{x}_0 - \mathbf{x}_*\|_A $.  $ \square $

6.2 Conjugate gradient method

In this section, we introduce the conjugate gradient (CG) method which is one of the most important Krylov subspace methods.

6.2.1 Conjugate gradient method

The basic idea of the CG method is given as follows. For a given initial vector  $ \mathbf{x}_0 $, in the first step, we still choose the direction of negative gradient, i.e.,  $ \mathbf{p}_0 = \mathbf{r}_0 $. Then we have

 $ \alpha_0 = \frac{\mathbf{r}_0^T \mathbf{p}_0}{\mathbf{p}_0^T A \mathbf{p}_0}, \quad \mathbf{x}_1 = \mathbf{x}_0 + \alpha_0 \mathbf{p}_0, \quad \mathbf{r}_1 = \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x}_1 $.

Afterwards, at the  $ (k+1) $th step ( $ k \geq 1 $), we want to choose a direction  $ \mathbf{p}_k $ on the plane

 $ \pi_2 = \{\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_k + \xi \mathbf{r}_k + \eta \mathbf{p}_{k-1} : \xi, \eta \in \mathbb{R}\} $

such that  $ \phi $ decreases most rapidly. Consider  $ \phi $ on  $ \pi_2 $:

 $ \psi(\xi, \eta) = \phi(\mathbf{x}_k + \xi \mathbf{r}_k + \eta \mathbf{p}_{k-1}) $

\=(\mathbf{x}_k + \xi \mathbf{r}_k + \eta \mathbf{p}_{k-1})^T A(\mathbf{x}_k + \xi \mathbf{r}_k + \eta \mathbf{p}_{k-1}) $

 $ -2 \mathbf{b}^T (\mathbf{x}_k + \xi \mathbf{r}_k + \eta \mathbf{p}_{k-1}) $.

By directly computing, we have

 $ \frac{\partial \psi}{\partial \xi} = 2 (\xi \mathbf{r}_k^T A \mathbf{r}_k + \eta \mathbf{r}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1} - \mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{r}_k) $,

 $ \frac{\partial \psi}{\partial \eta} = 2 (\xi \mathbf{r}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1} + \eta \mathbf{p}_{k-1}^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1}) $,

6.2 Conjugate gradient method 81

where we use  $ \mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{p}_{k-1} = 0 $ (see Theorem 6.3 later). Let

 $ \frac{\partial \psi}{\partial \xi} = \frac{\partial \psi}{\partial \eta} = 0 $,

then we find a unique minimum point

 $ \tilde{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{x}_k + \xi_0 \mathbf{r}_k + \eta_0 \mathbf{p}_{k-1} $

of  $ \phi $ on the plane  $ \pi_2 $, where  $ \xi_0 $ and  $ \eta_0 $ satisfy:

 $ \xi_0 \mathbf{r}_k^T A \mathbf{r}_k + \eta_0 \mathbf{r}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1} = \mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{r}_k $, (6.7)

 $ \xi_0 \mathbf{r}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1} + \eta_0 \mathbf{p}_{k-1}^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1} = 0 $.

Note that from (6.7), if  $ \mathbf{r}_k \neq 0 $ then  $ \xi_0 \neq 0 $. We therefore can choose

 $ \mathbf{p}_k = \frac{1}{\xi_0} (\tilde{\mathbf{x}} - \mathbf{x}_k) = \mathbf{r}_k + \frac{\eta_0}{\xi_0} \mathbf{p}_{k-1} $

as a new direction which is the optimal direction for minimizing  $ \phi $ on the plane  $ \pi_2 $.

Let  $ \beta_{k-1} = \frac{\eta_0}{\xi_0} $. Then by using the second equation in (6.7), we have

 $ \beta_{k-1} = -\frac{\mathbf{r}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1}}{\mathbf{p}_{k-1}^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1}} $.

Note that  $ \mathbf{p}_k $ satisfies  $ \mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1} = 0 $ (see Theorem 6.3 later), i.e.,  $ \mathbf{p}_k $ and  $ \mathbf{p}_{k-1} $ are mutually A-conjugate.

Once we get  $ \mathbf{p}_k $, we can determine  $ \alpha_k $ by using (6.3) and then compute

 $ \mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k $.

In conclusion, we have the following formulas:

 $ \alpha_k = \frac{\mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{p}_k}{\mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k} $,

 $ \mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k $,

 $ \mathbf{r}_{k+1} = \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x}_{k+1} $,

 $ \beta_k = -\frac{\mathbf{r}_{k+1}^T A \mathbf{p}_k}{\mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k} $,

 $ \mathbf{p}_{k+1} = \mathbf{r}_{k+1} + \beta_k \mathbf{p}_k $.

82 Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

After a few elementary computations, we obtain

 $ \alpha_k = \frac{\mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{r}_k}{\mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k}, \quad \beta_k = \frac{\mathbf{r}_{k+1}^T \mathbf{r}_{k+1}}{\mathbf{r}_k^T \mathbf{r}_k}. $

Thus, the scheme of the CG method, one of the most popular and successful iterative methods for solving symmetric positive definite systems  $ Ax = b $, is given as follows. At the initialization step, for  $ k = 0 $, we choose  $ x_0 $ and then calculate

 $ \mathbf{r}_0 = b - A \mathbf{x}_0 $.

While  $ \mathbf{r}_k \neq 0 $, in iteration steps, we have

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

k = k + 1 \\

if k = 1 \\

\end{array}

\right. $

 $ \begin{array}{l}

\mathbf{p}_0 = \mathbf{r}_0 \\

else \\

\end{array} $

 $ \begin{array}{l}

\beta_{k-2} = \mathbf{r}_{k-1}^T \mathbf{r}_{k-1} / \mathbf{r}_{k-2}^T \mathbf{r}_{k-2} \\

\mathbf{p}_{k-1} = \mathbf{r}_{k-1} + \beta_{k-2} \mathbf{p}_{k-2} \\

end \\

\alpha_{k-1} = \mathbf{r}_{k-1}^T \mathbf{r}_{k-1} / \mathbf{p}_{k-1}^T A \mathbf{p}_{k-1} \\

\mathbf{x}_k = \mathbf{x}_{k-1} + \alpha_{k-1} \mathbf{p}_{k-1} \\

\mathbf{r}_k = \mathbf{r}_{k-1} - \alpha_{k-1} A \mathbf{p}_{k-1}

\end{array} $

where  $ \mathbf{r}_k $,  $ \mathbf{p}_k $ are vectors and  $ \alpha_k $,  $ \beta_k $ are scalars,  $ k = 0, 1, \ldots $. The  $ \mathbf{x}_k $ is the approximation to the exact solution after the  $ k $th iteration. When  $ \mathbf{r}_k = 0 $, then the solution is  $ \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_k $.

6.2.2 Basic properties

Theorem 6.3 The vectors  $ \{\mathbf{r}_i\} $ and  $ \{\mathbf{p}_i\} $ satisfy the following properties:

(1)  $ \mathbf{p}_i^T \mathbf{r}_j = 0 $,  $ 0 \leq i < j \leq k $;

(2)  $ \mathbf{r}_i^T \mathbf{r}_j = 0 $,  $ i \neq j $,  $ 0 \leq i, j \leq k $;

(3)  $ \mathbf{p}_i^T A \mathbf{p}_j = 0 $,  $ i \neq j $,  $ 0 \leq i, j \leq k $;

(4) span $ \{\mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{r}_k\} $ = span $ \{\mathbf{p}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{p}_k\} = \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k + 1) $, where  $ \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k + 1) \equiv \text{span}\{\mathbf{r}_0, A \mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, A^k \mathbf{r}_0\} $

is called the Krylov subspace.

Proof By using induction, for  $ k = 1 $, we have

 $ \mathbf{p}_0 = \mathbf{r}_0 $, \quad \mathbf{r}_1 = \mathbf{r}_0 - \alpha_0 A \mathbf{p}_0 $, \quad \mathbf{p}_1 = \mathbf{r}_1 + \beta_0 \mathbf{p}_0 $.

6.2 Conjugate gradient method 83

Then

 $ \mathbf{p}_0^T \mathbf{r}_1 = \mathbf{r}_0^T \mathbf{r}_1 = \mathbf{r}_0^T (\mathbf{r}_0 - \alpha_0 A \mathbf{p}_0) = \mathbf{r}_0^T \mathbf{r}_0 - \alpha_0 \mathbf{p}_0^T A \mathbf{p}_0 = 0 $

provided  $ \alpha_0 = \mathbf{r}_0^T \mathbf{r}_0 / \mathbf{p}_0^T A \mathbf{p}_0 $, and

 $ \mathbf{p}_1^T A \mathbf{p}_0 = (\mathbf{r}_1 + \beta_0 \mathbf{r}_0)^T A \mathbf{r}_0 = \mathbf{r}_1^T A \mathbf{r}_0 - \frac{\mathbf{r}_1^T A \mathbf{r}_0}{\mathbf{r}_0^T A \mathbf{r}_0} \mathbf{r}_0^T A \mathbf{r}_0 = 0 $

Now, we assume that the theorem is true for k and we try to prove that it also holds for  $ k + 1 $.

For (1), by using assumption and  $ \mathbf{r}_{k+1} = \mathbf{r}_k - \alpha_k A \mathbf{p}_k $, we have

 $ \mathbf{p}_i^T \mathbf{r}_{k+1} = \mathbf{p}_i^T \mathbf{r}_k - \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_i^T A \mathbf{p}_k = 0 $,  $ 0 \leq i \leq k-1 $,

and also

 $ \mathbf{p}_k^T \mathbf{r}_{k+1} = \mathbf{p}_k^T \mathbf{r}_k - \frac{\mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{r}_k}{\mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k} \mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k = 0 $

Thus, (1) is true for  $ k+1 $.

For (2), we have by assumption,

span $ \{\mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{r}_k\} = \text{span}\{\mathbf{p}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{p}_k\} $.

By (1), we know that  $ \mathbf{r}_{k+1} $ is orthogonal to this subspace. Therefore, (2) is true for  $ k+1 $.

For (3), by using assumption, (2) and

 $ \mathbf{p}_{k+1} = \mathbf{r}_{k+1} + \beta_k \mathbf{p}_k $,  $ \mathbf{r}_{i+1} = \mathbf{r}_i - \alpha_i A \mathbf{p}_i $,

we have

 $ \mathbf{p}_{k+1}^T A \mathbf{p}_i = \frac{1}{\alpha_i} \mathbf{r}_{k+1}^T (\mathbf{r}_i - \mathbf{r}_{i+1}) + \beta_k \mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_i = 0 $,  $ i = 0, 1, \ldots, k-1 $.

By the definition of  $ \beta_k $, we have

 $ \mathbf{p}_{k+1}^T A \mathbf{p}_k = (\mathbf{r}_{k+1} + \beta_k \mathbf{p}_k)^T A \mathbf{p}_k = \mathbf{r}_{k+1}^T A \mathbf{p}_k - \frac{\mathbf{r}_{k+1}^T A \mathbf{p}_k}{\mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k} \mathbf{p}_k^T A \mathbf{p}_k = 0 $.

Then, (3) holds for  $ k+1 $.

For (4), we know by using assumption that

 $ \mathbf{r}_k, \mathbf{p}_k \in \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k+1) = \text{span}\{\mathbf{r}_0, A \mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, A^k \mathbf{r}_0\} $.

Therefore

 $ \mathbf{r}_{k+1} = \mathbf{r}_k - \alpha_k A \mathbf{p}_k \in \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k+2) = \text{span}\{\mathbf{r}_0, A \mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, A^{k+1} \mathbf{r}_0\} $,

and

 $ \mathbf{p}_{k+1} = \mathbf{r}_{k+1} + \beta_k \mathbf{p}_k \in \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k+2) = \text{span}\{\mathbf{r}_0, A \mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, A^{k+1} \mathbf{r}_0\} $.

By (2) and (3), we note that the vectors  $ \mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{r}_{k+1} $ and  $ \mathbf{p}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{p}_{k+1} $ are linearly independent. Thus, (4) is true for  $ k+1 $.

84 Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

We remark that by Theorem 6.3, at most $n$ steps, we can obtain the exact solution of an $n$-by-$n$ symmetric positive definite system by using the CG method. Therefore, from a theoretical viewpoint, the CG method is a direct method.

Theorem 6.4 The $\mathbf{x}_k$ obtained from the CG method satisfies

$\phi(\mathbf{x}_k) = \min\{\phi(\mathbf{x}) : \mathbf{x} \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)\}$ (6.8)

or

$\|\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*\|_{A} = \min\{\|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_*\|_{A} : \mathbf{x} \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)\}$, (6.9)

where $\|\mathbf{x}\|_{A} = \sqrt{\mathbf{x}^T A \mathbf{x}}$ and $\mathbf{x}_*$ is the exact solution of $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$.

Proof Since (6.8) and (6.9) are equivalent, we only need to prove (6.9). Suppose that $\mathbf{r}_l = 0$ at the $l$th step of the CG method, then we have

$\mathbf{x}_* = \mathbf{x}_l = \mathbf{x}_{l-1} + \alpha_{l-1} \mathbf{p}_{l-1} = \mathbf{x}_{l-2} + \alpha_{l-2} \mathbf{p}_{l-2} + \alpha_{l-1} \mathbf{p}_{l-1} = \mathbf{x}_0 + \alpha_0 \mathbf{p}_0 + \cdots + \alpha_{l-1} \mathbf{p}_{l-1}$.

For $k < l$, we have

$\mathbf{x}_k = \mathbf{x}_0 + \alpha_0 \mathbf{p}_0 + \cdots + \alpha_{k-1} \mathbf{p}_{k-1} \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)$.

Let $\mathbf{x}$ be any vector in $\mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)$. Then by Theorem 6.3 (4), we have

$\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_0 + \gamma_0 \mathbf{p}_0 + \cdots + \gamma_{k-1} \mathbf{p}_{k-1}$.

Moreover,

$\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x} = (\alpha_0 - \gamma_0) \mathbf{p}_0 + \cdots + (\alpha_{k-1} - \gamma_{k-1}) \mathbf{p}_{k-1} + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k + \cdots + \alpha_{l-1} \mathbf{p}_{l-1}$.

Since

$\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_k = \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k + \cdots + \alpha_{l-1} \mathbf{p}_{l-1}$, we have by using Theorem 6.3 (3),

$\|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}\|_{A}^2 = \|(\alpha_0 - \gamma_0) \mathbf{p}_0 + \cdots + (\alpha_{k-1} - \gamma_{k-1}) \mathbf{p}_{k-1}\|_{A}^2 + \|\alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k + \cdots + \alpha_{l-1} \mathbf{p}_{l-1}\|_{A}^2_A \geq \|\alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k + \cdots + \alpha_{l-1} \mathbf{p}_{l-1}\|_{A}^2_A = \|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_k\|_{A}^2_A$.

6.3 Practical CG method and convergence analysis

85

6.3.1 Practical CG method

From Theorem 6.3, we know that the CG method would obtain an accurate solution after $n$ steps in exact arithmetic, where $n$ is the size of the system. In other words, the CG method is thought as a direct method rather than an iterative method. When $n$ is very large, in practice, we use the CG method as an iterative method and stop iterations when

(i) $\|\mathbf{r}_k\|$ is less than $\epsilon$, where $\mathbf{r}_k = \mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x}_k$ and $\epsilon$ is a given error bound; or

(ii) the number of iterations reaches $k_{\max}$, the largest number of iterations provided by us, where $k_{\max} \ll n$.

We then have the following practical algorithm for solving symmetric positive definite systems $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$. At the initialization step $k = 0$, we choose a initial vector $\mathbf{x}$ and calculate

$\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x}$, $\rho = \mathbf{r}^T\mathbf{r}$.

While $\sqrt{\rho} > \epsilon \|\mathbf{b}\|_2$ and $k < k_{\max}$, in iteration steps, we have

$\begin{cases}

k = k + 1 \\

if k = 1 \\

\quad \mathbf{p} = \mathbf{r} \\

else \\

\quad \beta = \rho / \tilde{\rho}; \quad \mathbf{p} = \mathbf{r} + \beta \mathbf{p}

\end{cases}$

end

$\mathbf{w} = A\mathbf{p}; \quad \alpha = \rho / \mathbf{p}^T\mathbf{w}; \quad \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x} + \alpha \mathbf{p} \\

\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{r} - \alpha \mathbf{w}; \quad \tilde{\rho} = \rho; \quad \rho = \mathbf{r}^T\mathbf{r}

\end{cases}$

where $\mathbf{r}$, $\mathbf{p}$, $\mathbf{w}$ are vectors and $\rho$, $\alpha$, $\beta$ are scalars.

We remark that:

(1) We only have the matrix-vector multiplications in the algorithm. Usually, it requires $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$ operation cost for computing such multiplications. If the matrix is sparse or it has a special structure, then these multiplications can be done efficiently by using some sparse solvers or fast solvers.

(2) We do not need to estimate any parameter in the algorithm unlike the SOR method.

(3) For each iteration, we could use the parallel algorithms for matrix and vector operations.

Now, we briefly discuss how to use the CG method to solve general linear systems $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$. Since we cannot use the CG method directly to the system, instead of

86 Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

solving Ax = b, we can use the CG method to solve the normalized system,

 $  A^T A x = A^T b  $.

When the system is well-conditioned, then the normalized CG method is suitable.

But if the system is ill-conditioned, then the condition number of the normalized system could become very large because of  $ \kappa_2 (A^T A) = (\kappa_2 (A))^2 $. Hence the normalized CG method is not suitable for ill-conditioned systems.

6.3.2 Convergence analysis

We have the following theorem for the convergence rate of the CG method.

Theorem 6.5 If  $  A = I + B  $ where  $  I  $ is the identity matrix and  $  \text{rank}(B) = p  $, then by using at most  $  p + 1  $ iterations, the CG method can obtain the exact solution of Ax = b.

Proof Since  $  A = I + B  $, it is easy to show that

span $ \{\mathbf{r}_0, \mathbf{A}\mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{A}^k \mathbf{r}_0\} = \text{span}\{\mathbf{r}_0, \mathbf{B}\mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{B}^k \mathbf{r}_0\} $.

Note that rank( $  B  $) = p and then

 $ \dim(\text{span}\{\mathbf{r}_0, \mathbf{B}\mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{B}^k \mathbf{r}_0\}) \leq p + 1 $.

By Theorem 6.3, we know that

 $ \dim(\text{span}\{\mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, \mathbf{r}_p, \mathbf{r}_{p+1}\}) \leq p + 1 $

and also

 $ \mathbf{r}_i^T \mathbf{r}_{p+1} = 0, \qquad i = 0, 1, \ldots, p $.

Therefore  $  \mathbf{r}_{p+1} = 0  $, i.e.,  $  A \mathbf{x}_{p+1} = b  $.

Theorem 6.6 Let  $  A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $ be symmetric positive definite and  $  \mathbf{x}_*  $ be the exact solution of Ax = b. We then have

 $ \|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_k\|_A \leq 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{\kappa_2} - 1}{\sqrt{\kappa_2} + 1} \right)^k \|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_0\|_A $,

where  $  \mathbf{x}_k  $ is produced by the CG method and

 $ \kappa_2 = \kappa_2(A) = \| A\|_2 \| A^{-1} \|_2 $.

6.3 Practical CG method and convergence analysis

87

Proof By Theorem 6.3, we know that for any  $ \mathbf{x} \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k) $,

 $ \mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_0 + a_{k1} \mathbf{r}_0 + a_{k2} A \mathbf{r}_0 + \cdots + a_{kk} A^{k-1} \mathbf{r}_0 $

 $ = A^{-1} (\mathbf{r}_0 + a_{k1} A \mathbf{r}_0 + a_{k2} A^2 \mathbf{r}_0 + \cdots + a_{kk} A^k \mathbf{r}_0) $

 $ = A^{-1} P_k(A) \mathbf{r}_0, $

where  $ P_k(\lambda) = \sum_{j=0}^{k} a_{kj} \lambda^j $ with  $ P_k(0) = 1 $. Let  $ \mathcal{P}_k $ be the set of all the polynomials  $ P_k $ with order less than or equal to  $ k $ and  $ P_k(0) = 1 $. By Theorem 6.4 and Lemma 6.1, we have

 $ \|\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*\|_{A} = \min\{\|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}_*\|_{A} : \mathbf{x} \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)\} $

 $ = \min_{P_k \in \mathcal{P}_k} \|A^{-1} P_k(A) \mathbf{r}_0\|_{A} = \min_{P_k \in \mathcal{P}_k} \|P_k(A) A^{-1} \mathbf{r}_0\|_{A} $

 $ \leq \min_{P_k \in \mathcal{P}_k} \max_{1 \leq i \leq n} |P_k(\lambda_i)| \cdot \|A^{-1} \mathbf{r}_0\|_{A} $

 $ \leq \min_{P_k \in \mathcal{P}_k} \max_{a_1 \leq \lambda \leq a_2} |P_k(\lambda)| \cdot \|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_0\|_{A}, $ (6.10)

where

 $ 0 < a_1 = \lambda_1 \leq \cdots \leq \lambda_n = a_2 $

are the eigenvalues of  $ A $. By the well-known Approximation Theorem of Chebyshev polynomials [70], we know that there exists a unique solution of the optimal problem

 $ \min_{P_k \in \mathcal{P}_k} \max_{a_1 \leq \lambda \leq a_2} |P_k(\lambda)| $

given by

 $ \widetilde{P}_k(\lambda) = \frac{T_k \left( \frac{a_2 + a_1 - 2\lambda}{a_2 - a_1} \right)}{T_k \left( \frac{a_2 + a_1}{a_2 - a_1} \right)}. $

Here  $ T_k(z) $ is the  $ k $th Chebyshev polynomial defined recursively by

 $ T_k(z) = 2z T_{k-1}(z) - T_{k-2}(z) $

with  $ T_0(z) = 1 $ and  $ T_1(z) = z $. By the properties of Chebyshev polynomials [70] again, we know that

 $ \max_{a_1 \leq \lambda \leq a_2} |\widetilde{P}_k(\lambda)| = \frac{1}{T_k \left( \frac{a_2 + a_1}{a_2 - a_1} \right)} \leq 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{\kappa_2} - 1}{\sqrt{\kappa_2} + 1} \right)^k, $ (6.11)

where  $ \kappa_2 = \kappa_2(A) = \|A\|_2 \|A^{-1}\|_{2} $. Therefore,

88

Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

Furthermore, we have the following theorem [43].

Theorem 6.7 If the eigenvalues  $ \lambda_j $ of a symmetric positive definite matrix  $ A $ are ordered such that

 $ 0 < \lambda_1 \leqslant \cdots \leqslant \lambda_p \leqslant b_1 \leqslant \lambda_{p+1} \leqslant \cdots \leqslant \lambda_{n-q} \leqslant b_2 \leqslant \lambda_{n-q+1} \leqslant \cdots \leqslant \lambda_n $,

where  $ b_1 $ and  $ b_2 $ are two constants, then

 $ \frac{\|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_k\|_A}{\|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_0\|_A} \leqslant 2\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}\right)^{k-p-q} \cdot \max_{\lambda \in [b_1, b_2]} \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left(\frac{\lambda-\lambda_j}{\lambda_j}\right) $,

where  $ \alpha \equiv (b_2/b_1)^{1/2} \geqslant 1 $.

Proof Let  $ \mathbf{e}_k = \mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_k $. Let  $ \mathcal{P}_k $ be the set of all the polynomials  $ P_k $ with order less than or equal to  $ k $ and  $ P_k(0) = 1 $. For any  $ P_k \in \mathcal{P}_k $, we have from (6.10) that

 $ \|\mathbf{e}_k\|_A \leqslant \min_{P_k \in \mathcal{P}_k} \max_{1 \leqslant j \leqslant n} |P_k(\lambda_j)| \cdot \|\mathbf{e}_0\|_A \leqslant \max_{1 \leqslant i \leqslant n} |P_k(\lambda_i)| \cdot \|\mathbf{e}_0\|_A \cdot (6.12) $

Given a polynomial  $ P_{k-p-q} \in \mathcal{P}_{k-p-q} $, we construct a special polynomial as follows:

 $ \widehat{P}_k \equiv P_{k-p-q} \cdot (-1)^p \cdot \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left( \frac{\lambda - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_j - \lambda}{\lambda_j} \right) $.

For this specially defined polynomial  $ \widehat{P}_k $, we have

 $ \widehat{P}_k(\lambda_i) = (-1)^p \cdot P_{k-p-q}(\lambda_i) \cdot \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_j - \lambda_i}{\lambda_j} \right) = 0 $, (6.13)

for  $ i = 1,2,\ldots,p, n-q + 1,\ldots,n $. Here we can see that

 $ \widehat{P}_k \in \mathcal{P}_k $. (6.14)

According to (6.12), (6.13) and (6.14), we have

 $ \|\mathbf{e}_k\|_A \leqslant \max_{1 \leqslant i \leqslant n} |\widehat{P}_k(\lambda_i)| \cdot \|\mathbf{e}_0\|_A = \max_{1 \leqslant i \leqslant n} \left| P_{k-p-q}(\lambda_i) \cdot \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_j - \lambda_i}{\lambda_j} \right) \right| \|\mathbf{e}_0\|_A $

 $ = \max_{1 \leqslant i \leqslant n} \left| P_{k-p-q}(\lambda_i) \cdot \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_j - \lambda_i}{\lambda_j} \right) \right| \|\mathbf{e}_0\|_A $

 $ = \max_{p+1 \leqslant i \leqslant n-q} \left| P_{k-p-q}(\lambda_i) \cdot \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_j - \lambda_i}{\lambda_j} \right) \right| \|\mathbf{e}_0\|_A $

 $ = \max_{p+1 \leqslant i \leqslant n-q} \left| P_{k-p-q}(\lambda_i) \cdot \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_j - \lambda_i}{\lambda_j} \right) \right| \|\mathbf{e}_0\|_A $

 $ = \max_{p+1 \leqslant i \leqslant n-q} \left| P_{k-p-q}(\lambda_i) \right| \cdot \max_{p+1 \leqslant i \leqslant n-q} \left| \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j} \right) \right| \|\mathbf{e}_0\|_A $

 $ = \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_i} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_j - \lambda_i}{\lambda_j} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_i} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_j - \lambda_i}{\lambda_j} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - \lambda_j}{\lambda_i} \right) \cdot \prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n} \left( \frac{\lambda_i - 6.3 Practical CG method and convergence analysis 89

$$\leq\max_{\lambda_{p+1}\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_{n-q}}\left|P_{k-p-q}(\lambda)\right|\cdot\max_{\lambda_{p+1}\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_{n-q}}\left|\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\lambda-\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{j}}\right)\right.$$

$$\cdot\prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}-\lambda}{\lambda_{j}}\right)\left|\|\mathbf{e}_{0}\|_{A}\right.$$

$$\leq\max_{b_{1}\leq\lambda\leq b_{2}}\left|P_{k-p-q}(\lambda)\right|\cdot\max_{b_{1}\leq\lambda\leq b_{2}}\left|\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\lambda-\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{j}}\right)\cdot\prod_{j=n-q+1}^{n}\left(\frac{\lambda_{j}-\lambda}{\lambda_{j}}\right)\right|\|\mathbf{e}_{0}\|_{A}.$$

Since the above inequality holds for any $P_{k-p-q}\in\mathcal{P}_{k-p-q}$, we have by (6.11)

$$\max_{b_{1}\leq\lambda\leq b_{2}}\left|P_{k-p-q}(\lambda)\right|\leq2\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}\right)^{k-p-q},\quad\alpha=\sqrt{b_{2}/b_{1}}.$$ (6.15)

Moreover,

$$0\leq\frac{\lambda_{j}-\lambda}{\lambda_{j}}\leq1,\quad b_{1}\leq\lambda\leq b_{2}.$$ (6.16)

We finally have by using (6.15) and (6.16),

$$\|\mathbf{e}_{k}\|_{A}\leq2\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}\right)^{k-p-q}\cdot\max_{\lambda\in[b_{1},b_{2}]}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\lambda-\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{j}}\right)\|\mathbf{e}_{0}\|_{A}.$$ □

From Theorem 6.7, we note that when $n$ is increased, if $p,q$ are constants that do not depend on $n$ and $\lambda_{1}$ is uniformly bounded from zero, then the convergence rate is linear, i.e., the number of iterations is independent of $n$. We also notice that the more clustered the eigenvalues are, the faster the convergence rate will be.

Corollary 6.1 If the eigenvalues $\lambda_{j}$ of a symmetric positive definite matrix $A$ are ordered such that

$$0<\delta<\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{p}\leq1-\epsilon\leq\lambda_{p+1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{n-q}\leq1+\epsilon\leq\lambda_{n-q+1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{n},$$

where $0<\epsilon<1$, then

$$\frac{\|\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{x}_{k}\|_{A}}{\|\mathbf{x}_{*}-\mathbf{x}_{0}\|_{A}}\leq2\left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{\delta}\right)^{p}\epsilon^{k-p-q},$$

where $k\geq p+q$.

Proof For $\alpha$ given in Theorem 6.7, we have

$$\alpha\equiv\left(\frac{b_{2}}{b_{1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left(\frac{1+\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.$$

Therefore,

$$\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha+1}=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-\epsilon^{2}}}{\epsilon}<\epsilon.$$

90 Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

For  $ 1 \leqslant j \leqslant p $ and  $ \lambda \in [1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon] $, we have

 $ 0 \leqslant \frac{\lambda - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j} \leqslant \frac{1 + \epsilon}{\delta} $.

Thus, by using Theorem 6.7, we obtain

 $ \frac{\|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_k\|_A}{\|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_0\|_A} \leqslant 2\left(\frac{\alpha - 1}{\alpha + 1}\right)^{k-p-q} \cdot \max_{\lambda \in [b_1, b_2]} \prod_{j=1}^{p} \left(\frac{\lambda - \lambda_j}{\lambda_j}\right) \leqslant 2\left(\frac{1 + \epsilon}{\delta}\right)^p \epsilon^{k-p-q} $. □

6.4 Preconditioning

From Section 6.3, we know that if the matrix  $ A $ of the system

 $ A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b} $

is well-conditioned or its spectrum is clustered, then the convergence rate of the CG method is very quick. Therefore, in order to speed up the convergence rate, we usually precondition the system, i.e., instead of solving the original system, we solve the following preconditioned system

 $ \widetilde{A}\widetilde{\mathbf{x}} = \widetilde{\mathbf{b}} $, (6.17)

where

 $ \widetilde{A} = C^{-1}AC^{-1} $,  $ \widetilde{\mathbf{x}} = C\mathbf{x} $,  $ \widetilde{\mathbf{b}} = C^{-1}\mathbf{b} $,

and  $ C $ is symmetric positive definite. We wish that the preconditioned matrix  $ \widetilde{A} $ could have better spectral properties than those of  $ A $.

By using the CG method on (6.17), we have

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

\alpha_k = \frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k^T \widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k}{\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_k^T \widetilde{A} \widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_k}, \\

\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{k+1} = \widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_k + \alpha_k \widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_k, \\

\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_{k+1} = \widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k - \alpha_k \widetilde{A} \widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_k, \\

\beta_k = \frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k^T_k + \widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k + 1}{\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k^T_k \widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k}, \\

\widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_{k+1} = \widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k + \beta_k \widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_k,

\end{array}

\right. $

(6.18)

where  $ \widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_0 $ is any given initial vector,  $ \widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_0 = \widetilde{\mathbf{b}} - \widetilde{A}\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_0 $ and  $ \widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_0 = \widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_0 $. Let

 $ \widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_k = C\mathbf{x}_k $,  $ \widetilde{\mathbf{r}}_k = C^{-1}\mathbf{r}_k $,  $ \widetilde{\mathbf{p}}_k = C\mathbf{p}_k $,  $ M = C^2 $.

6.4 Preconditioning 91

Substituting them into (6.18), we actually have

 $ \mathbf{w}_k = A \mathbf{p}_k $,  $ \alpha_k = \rho_k / \mathbf{p}_k^T \mathbf{w}_k $,

 $ \mathbf{x}_{k+1} = \mathbf{x}_k + \alpha_k \mathbf{p}_k $,  $ \mathbf{r}_{k+1} = \mathbf{r}_k - \alpha_k \mathbf{w}_k $,

 $ \mathbf{z}_{k+1} = M^{-1} \mathbf{r}_{k+1} $,  $ \rho_{k+1} = \mathbf{r}_{k+1}^T \mathbf{z}_{k+1} $,

 $ \beta_k = \rho_{k+1} / \rho_k $,  $ \mathbf{p}_{k+1} = \mathbf{z}_{k+1} + \beta_k \mathbf{p}_k $,

where  $ \mathbf{x}_0 $ is any given initial vector,  $ \mathbf{r}_0 = \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x}_0 $,  $ \mathbf{z}_0 = M^{-1} \mathbf{r}_0 $,  $ \rho_0 = \mathbf{r}_0^T \mathbf{z}_0 $ and  $ \mathbf{p}_0 = \mathbf{z}_0 $.

We then have the following preconditioned algorithm. At the initialization step  $ k = 0 $, we choose an initial vector  $ \mathbf{x} $ and calculate  $ \mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x} $. While  $ \sqrt{\mathbf{r}^T \mathbf{r}} > \epsilon \|\mathbf{b}\|_2 $ and  $ k < k_{\max} $, in iteration steps, we have

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

\text{Solve } M \mathbf{z} = \mathbf{r} \text{ for } \mathbf{z} \\

\mathbf{k} = k + 1 \\

\text{if } k = 1 \\

\mathbf{p} = \mathbf{z} \\

\text{else} \\

\beta = \rho / \tilde{\rho}; \quad \mathbf{p} = \mathbf{z} + \beta \mathbf{p} \\

\text{end} \\

\mathbf{w} = A \mathbf{p}; \quad \alpha = \rho / \mathbf{p}^T \mathbf{w}; \quad \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x} + \alpha \mathbf{p} \\

\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{r} - \alpha \mathbf{w}; \quad \tilde{\rho} = \rho; \quad \rho = \mathbf{r}^T \mathbf{z}

\end{array}

\right. $

where  $ \mathbf{z} $,  $ \mathbf{r} $,  $ \mathbf{p} $,  $ \mathbf{w} $ are vectors and  $ \rho $,  $ \alpha $,  $ \beta $ are scalars. This algorithm is called the preconditioned conjugate gradient (PCG) method. Note that the PCG method has the following properties:

(i)  $ \mathbf{r}_i^T M^{-1} \mathbf{r}_j = 0 $, for  $ i \neq j $.

(ii)  $ \mathbf{p}_i^T A \mathbf{p}_j = 0 $, for  $ i \neq j $.

(iii) The approximation  $ \mathbf{x}_k $ satisfies:

 $ \|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_k\|_{\tilde{A}} \leqslant 2 \left( \frac{\sqrt{\kappa} - 1}{\sqrt{\kappa} + 1} \right)^k \|\mathbf{x}_* - \mathbf{x}_0\|_{\tilde{A}} $,

where  $ \kappa = \lambda_n / \lambda_1 $ with  $ \lambda_n $ being the largest eigenvalue of  $ M^{-1} A $ and  $ \lambda_1 $ being the smallest eigenvalue of  $ M^{-1} A $.

A good preconditioner  $ M = C^2 $ is chosen with three criteria in mind [2, 16, 32, 42, 43]:

(1)  $ M $ is easily constructable.

(2)  $ M \mathbf{z} = \mathbf{d} $ is easy to solve.

92

Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

(3) The spectrum of  $ M^{-1}A $ is clustered and (or)  $ M^{-1}A $ is well-conditioned compared to  $ A $. Note that  $ \tilde{A} $ is similar to  $ M^{-1}A $.

The main work involved in implementing the CG method to the preconditioned system  $ \tilde{A}\tilde{x} = \tilde{b} $ is the matrix-vector product  $ M^{-1}Av $ for some vector v in each iteration.

Usually, it is not easy to choose a preconditioner which satisfies all these three criteria. Now we briefly discuss the following three classes of preconditioners.

1. Diagonal preconditioners. If diagonal entries of the coefficient matrix A are much different, one could use the matrix

 $ M = \text{diag}(a_{11}, \ldots, a_{nn}) $

as a preconditioner to speed up the convergence rate of the CG method hopefully. For block matrix

 $ \left[\begin{array}{cccc} A_{11} & \cdots & A_{1k} \\ \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ A_{k1} & \cdots & A_{kk}\end{array}\right] $

if  $ A_{ii}^{-1} $ are easily to be obtained, then one could use the block diagonal matrix

 $ M = \text{diag}(A_{11}, \ldots, A_{kk}) $

as a preconditioner.

2. Preconditioners based on incomplete Cholesky factorization. If one computes the incomplete Cholesky factorization [32] first,

 $ A = LL^T + R $,

then one can use the matrix  $ M = LL^T $ as a preconditioner. We could require that the matrix L has the same sparse structure as the matrix A and also the matrix  $ LL^T \approx A $.

3. Optimal (circulant) preconditioners. This class of preconditioners were studied in [16, 18, 21, 42, 43, 77]. The circulant matrix is defined as follows:

 $ C_n = \begin{bmatrix} c_0 & c_{-1} & \cdots & c_{2-n} & c_{1-n} \\ c_1 & c_0 & \cdots & c_{-1} & \cdots & c_{2-n} \\ \vdots & c_1 & c_0 & \cdots & \vdots \\ c_{n-2} & \cdots & \ddots & \ddots & c_{-1} \\ c_{n-1} & c_{n-2} & \cdots & c_1 & c_0 \end{bmatrix} $

6.4 Preconditioning

93

where  $ c_{-k} = c_{n-k} $ for  $ 1 \leqslant k \leqslant n-1 $. It is well-known that *circulant* matrices can be diagonalized by the Fourier matrix  $ F_n $ [29], i.e.,

 $ C_n = F_n^* \Lambda_n F_n $, \quad (6.19)

where the entries of  $ F_n $ are given by

 $ (F_n)_{j,k} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}} e^{2\pi i(j-1)(k-1)/n} $, \quad i \equiv \sqrt{-1} $,

for  $ 1 \leqslant j, k \leqslant n $, and  $ \Lambda_n $ is a diagonal matrix holding the eigenvalues of  $ C_n $. We note that  $ \Lambda_n $ can be obtained in  $ \mathcal{O}(n \log n) $ operations by taking the fast Fourier transform (FFT) of the first column of  $ C_n $. Once  $ \Lambda_n $ is obtained, the products  $ C_n y $ and  $ C_n^{-1} y $ for any vector  $ y $ can be computed by FFTs in  $ \mathcal{O}(n \log n) $ operations. For the Fourier matrix  $ F_n $, when there is no ambiguity, we shall denote  $ F $.

Now, we study a kind of preconditioner called the optimal preconditioner [16, 18, 21, 42, 43]. Given any unitary matrix  $ U \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $, let  $ \mathcal{M}_U $ be the set of all matrices simultaneously diagonalized by  $ U $, i.e.,

 $ \mathcal{M}_U = \{U^* \Lambda U : \Lambda $ is an  $ n $-by- $ n $ diagonal matrix\}. \quad (6.20)

We note that in (6.20), when  $ U = F $, the Fourier matrix,  $ \mathcal{M}_F $ is the set of all the circular matrices. Let  $ \delta(A) $ denote the diagonal matrix whose diagonal is equal to the diagonal of the matrix A. We have the following lemma [16, 42, 43, 51, 82].

Lemma 6.2 For any arbitrary  $ A = [a_{pq}] \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $, let  $ c_U(A) $ be the minimizer of  $ \|W - A\|_F $ over all  $ W \in \mathcal{M}_U $. Then

(i)  $ c_U(A) $ is uniquely determined by A and is given by

 $ c_U(A) = U^* \delta(U A U^*) U $.

(ii) If  $ A $ is Hermitian, then  $ c_U(A) $ is also Hermitian. Furthermore, if  $ \lambda_{\min}(\cdot) $ and  $ \lambda_{\max}(\cdot) $ denote the smallest and largest eigenvalues respectively, then we have

 $ \lambda_{\min}(A) \leqslant \lambda_{\min}(c_U(A)) \leqslant \lambda_{\max}(c_U(A)) \leqslant \lambda_{\max}(A) $.

In particular, if  $ A $ is positive definite, then so is  $ c_U(A) $.

(iii) If  $ A $ is normal and stable, i.e.,  $ A^* A = AA^* $ and the real parts of all the eigenvalues of  $ A $ are negative, then  $ c_U(A) $ is also stable.

94

Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

(iv) When U is the Fourier matrix F, we then have

 $$  c_F(A) = \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} \left( \frac{1}{n} \sum_{p-q \equiv j (\mod n)} a_{pq} \right) Q^j,  $$ 

where Q is an n-by-n circulant matrix given by

 $$  Q \equiv \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}  $$ 

The matrix c_U(A) is called the optimal preconditioner of A and the matrix c_F(A) is called the optimal circulant preconditioner of A. We remark that c_F(A) is a good preconditioner for solving a large class of structured linear systems Ax = b, for instance, Toeplitz systems, Hankel systems, etc. See [16,18,21,25,42,43,49,50,52]. For applications of other preconditioners, one can refer to [4,67,97] and also Chapters 9 and 10.

6.5 GMRES method

In this section, we introduce the generalized minimum residual (GMRES) method to solve general systems

 $$  Ax = b,  $$ 

where A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} is nonsingular. The GMRES method was proposed by Saad and Schultz in 1986, which is one of the most important Krylov subspace methods for nonsymmetric systems [70,71].

6.5.1 Basic properties of GMRES method

For the GMRES method, in the kth iteration, we are going to find a solution x_k of the LS problem

 $$  \min_{x \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)} \|\mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x}\|_2,  $$ 

where

 $$  \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k) \equiv \text{span} \{\mathbf{r}_0, A\mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, A^{k-1}\mathbf{r}_0\}  $$ 

with \mathbf{r}_0 = \mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x}_0. Let \mathbf{x} \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k). We have

 $$  \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}_0 + \sum_{j=0}^{k-1} \gamma_j A^j \mathbf{r}_0,  $$ 

6.5 GMRES method 95

and then

 $  \mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x}_0 - \sum_{j=0}^{k-1} \gamma_j A^{j+1} \mathbf{r}_0 = \mathbf{r}_0 - \sum_{j=1}^{k} \gamma_{j-1} A^j \mathbf{r}_0.  $

Hence

 $  \mathbf{r} = \bar{P}_k(A) \mathbf{r}_0,  $

where  $  \bar{P}_k \in \mathcal{P}_k  $ with  $  \mathcal{P}_k  $ being the set of all the polynomials  $  P_k  $ with order less than

or equal to  $  k  $ and  $  P_k(0) = 1  $. We therefore have the following theorem.

Theorem 6.8 Let  $  \mathbf{x}_k  $ be the solution after the  $  k  $th GMRES iteration. Then we

have

 $ \|\mathbf{r}_k\|_2 = \min_{P \in \mathcal{P}_k} \|P(A)\mathbf{r}_0\|_2 \leq \|\bar{P}_k(A)\mathbf{r}_0\|_2. $

Furthermore,

 $ \frac{\|\mathbf{r}_k\|_2}{\|\mathbf{r}_0\|_2} \leq \|\bar{P}_k(A)\|_2. $

Moreover, we have

Theorem 6.9 The GMRES method can obtain the exact solution of  $  A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}  $ within

 $  n  $ iterations, where  $  A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $.

Proof The characteristic polynomial of  $  A  $ is given by

 $  p_A(z) = \det(zI - A)  $,

where the order of  $  p_A(z)  $ is  $  n  $ and  $  p_A(0) = (-1)^n \det(A) \neq 0  $. Then

 $  \bar{P}_n(z) = \frac{p_A(z)}{p_A(0)} \in \mathcal{P}_n.  $

By the Hamilton-Cayley Theorem [39], we know that

 $  \bar{P}_n(A) = p_A(A) = 0.  $

By Theorem 6.8, we have

 $  \mathbf{r}_n = \mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x}_n = 0.  $

Thus  $  \mathbf{x}_n  $ is the exact solution of  $  A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}.  $ □

If  $  A  $ is diagonalizable, i.e.,  $  A = V\Lambda V^{-1}  $ where  $  \Lambda  $ is a diagonal matrix, then we

have

 $  P(A) = VP(\Lambda)V^{-1}.  $

If  $  V  $ is orthogonal, then  $  A  $ is a normal matrix.

96

Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

Theorem 6.10 Let  $ A = V\Lambda V^{-1} $. We have

 $ \frac{\|\mathbf{r}_k\|_2}{\|\mathbf{r}_0\|_2} \leq \kappa_2(V) \min_{P \in \mathcal{P}_k} \max_{\lambda_i} |P(\lambda_i)| $,

where  $ \lambda_i $ ( $ i = 1, 2, \ldots, n $) are the eigenvalues of the matrix  $ A $.

Proof By Theorem 6.8, we have

 $ \|\mathbf{r}_k\|_2 = \min_{P \in \mathcal{P}_k} \|P(A)\mathbf{r}_0\|_2 = \min_{P \in \mathcal{P}_k} \|VP(\Lambda)V^{-1}\mathbf{r}_0\|_2 $

 $ \leq \min_{P \in \mathcal{P}_k} \|V\|_2 \|V^{-1}\|_2 \|P(\Lambda)\|_2 \| \mathbf{r}_0\|_2 $

 $ = \kappa_2(V) \min_{P \in \mathcal{P}_k} \|P(\Lambda)\|_2 \| \mathbf{r}_0\|_2 $

 $ = \kappa_2(V) \min_{P \in \mathcal{P}_k} \max_{\lambda_i} \|P(\lambda_i)\| \cdot \| \mathbf{r}_0\|_2 $

We remark that when  $ A $ is normal, then  $ \kappa_2(V) = 1 $. We therefore have

 $ \frac{\|\mathbf{r}_k\|_2}{\|\mathbf{r}_0\|_2} \leq \min_{P \in \mathcal{P}_k} \max_{\lambda_i} |P(\lambda_i)| $.

Theorem 6.11 If  $ A $ is diagonalizable and has exactly  $ k $ distinct eigenvalues, then the GMRES method terminates in at most  $ k $ iterations.

Proof We construct a polynomial as follows,

 $ P(z) = \prod_{i=1}^{k} \frac{\lambda_i - z}{\lambda_i} \in \mathcal{P}_k $,

where  $ \lambda_i $ are the eigenvalues of  $ A $. By Theorem 6.10, we know that  $ \mathbf{r}_k = 0 $, i.e.,  $ \mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_k = \mathbf{b} $. □

We should emphasize that in general, the behavior of the GMRES method cannot be determined by eigenvalues alone. In fact, it is shown in [35] that any nonincreasing convergence rate is possible for the GMRES method applied to some problem with nonnormal matrix. Moreover, that problem can have any desired distribution of eigenvalues. Thus, for instance, eigenvalues tightly clustered around 1 are not necessarily good for nonnormal matrices, as they are for normal ones. However, we have the following two theorems.

Theorem 6.12 If  $ \mathbf{b} $ is a linear combination of  $ k $ eigenvectors of  $ A $, say

 $ \mathbf{b} = \sum_{l=1}^{k} \gamma_l \mathbf{u}_{i_l} $,

then the GMRES method terminates in at most  $ k $ iterations.

6.5 GMRES method 97

Proof We first extend the set of eigenvectors  $ \{\mathbf{u}_{i_l}\} $ to be a basis of  $ \mathbb{R}^n $, i.e., the vectors

 $ \mathbf{u}_{i_1}, \mathbf{u}_{i_2}, \ldots, \mathbf{u}_{i_k}, \mathbf{v}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{v}_{n-k} $,

form a basis of  $ \mathbb{R}^n $. Let  $ \mathbf{x}_* $ be the exact solution of  $ \mathbf{A}\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b} $. Then

 $ \mathbf{x}_* = \sum_{l=1}^{k} \alpha_l \mathbf{u}_{i_l} + \sum_{j=1}^{n-k} \beta_j \mathbf{v}_j $

Moreover, we have

 $ \mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_* = \sum_{l=1}^{k} \alpha_l \mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}_{i_l} + \sum_{j=1}^{n-k} \beta_j \mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}_j $

 $ \mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}_j = \sum_{l=1}^{k} \alpha_l \lambda_i_l \mathbf{u}_{i_l} + \sum_{j=1}^{n-k} \beta_j \lambda \mathbf{v}_j $

 $ \mathbf{v}_j = \mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}_i_l $

 $ \mathbf{u}_{i_l} = \sum_{l=1}^{k} \gamma_l \mathbf{u}_{i_l} $

 $ \mathbf{A}\mathbf{v}_i_l = \gamma_l / \lambda_{i_l}, \quad j = 1, 2, \ldots, n-k $

Hence,

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

\beta_j = 0, \\

\alpha_l = \gamma_l / \lambda_{i_l}

\end{array}

\right. $

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

j = 1, 2, \ldots, n-k; \\

l = 1, 2, \ldots, k.

\end{array}

\right. $

We therefore have

 $ \mathbf{x}_* = \sum_{l=1}^{k} (\gamma_l / \lambda_{i_l}) \mathbf{u}_{i_l} $

 $ \mathbf{v}_l = \gamma_l / \lambda_{i_l}, \quad i = 1, 2, \ldots, n, k $

Let

 $ \bar{\mathbf{P}}_k(z) = \prod_{l=1}^{k} \frac{\lambda_{i_l} - z}{\lambda_{i_l}} \in \mathcal{P}_k $

Note that  $ \bar{\mathbf{P}}_k(\lambda_{i_l}) = 0 $ for  $ 1 \leq l \leq k $, and

 $ \bar{\mathbf{P}}_k(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{x}_* = \sum_{l=1}^{k} \bar{\mathbf{P}}_k(\lambda_{i_l}) (\gamma_l / \lambda_{i_l}) \mathbf{u}_{i_l} = \mathbf{0} $

We thus have

 $ \|\mathbf{r}_k\|_2 \leq \|\bar{\mathbf{P}}_k(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{r}_0\|_2 = \|\bar{\mathbf{P}}_k(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{b}\|_2 $

 $ \|\bar{\mathbf{P}}_k(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_*\|_2 = \|\bar{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{P}_k(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{x}_*\|_2 = 0 $

where we choose the initial vector  $ \mathbf{x}_0 = \mathbf{0} $.

98

Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

Proof We first recall that the minimal polynomial of  $  \mathbf{r}_0  $ with respect to  $  A  $ is the nonzero monic polynomial  $  p  $ of the lowest degree such that  $  p(A) \mathbf{r}_0 = 0  $ [70]. By Theorem 6.8, the GMRES method must converge within  $  \nu  $ iterations, where  $  \nu  $ is the degree of the minimal polynomials of the residual  $  \mathbf{r}_0  $ with respect to  $  A = I + L  $. Let  $  \mu  $ be the degree of the minimal polynomials of  $  \mathbf{r}_0  $ with respect to  $  L  $. Since

 $ \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \alpha_i (I + L)^{i} \mathbf{r}_0 = 0 $

implies

 $ \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \beta_i L^{i} \mathbf{r}_0 = 0 $

for some constants  $  \beta_i  $ and vice versa, we have  $  \nu = \mu  $. Moreover, from the definition of  $  \mu  $, the set

 $ \{ \mathbf{r}_0, L \mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, L^{\mu-1} \mathbf{r}_0 \} $

is linearly independent. Let  $  B  $ be the column vector space of  $  L  $. Then, the dimension of  $  B  $ is equal to the rank of  $  L  $. Since  $  L^i \mathbf{r}_0 \in B  $ for  $  i \geqslant 1  $, we have

 $ \{ L \mathbf{r}_0, \ldots, L^{\mu-1} \mathbf{r}_0 \} \subseteq B $.

Thus,  $  \mu - 1 \leqslant \text{rank}(L)  $, i.e.,  $  \mu \leqslant \text{rank}(L) + 1  $. Hence, the GMRES method converges within

 $ \nu = \mu \leqslant \text{rank}(L) + 1 $

iterations. □

6.5.2 Implementation of GMRES method

Recall that the LS problem from the  $  k  $th iteration of the GMRES method is

 $ \min_{x \in \mathbf{x}_0 + K(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)} \| \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x} \|_2 $.

Suppose that we have a matrix

 $  V_k = [\mathbf{v}_1^k \mid \mathbf{v}_2^k | \ldots \mid \mathbf{v}_k^k]  $

whose columns form an orthonormal basis of  $  \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)  $. Then for any  $  \mathbf{z} \in \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k)  $, it can be written as

 $ \mathbf{z} = \sum_{l=1}^{k} u_l \mathbf{v}_l^k = V_k \mathbf{u} $,

where  $  \mathbf{u} = [u_1, u_2, \ldots, u_k]^T \in \mathbb{R}^k  $. Thus, once we find  $  V_k  $, we can convert the original LS problem in the Krylov subspace into an LS problem in  $  \mathbb{R}^k  $ as follows. Let  $  \mathbf{x}_k  $ be the solution after the  $  k  $th iteration. We then have

 $ \mathbf{x}_k = \mathbf{x}_0 + V_k \mathbf{y}_k $,

6.5 GMRES method 99

where the vector  $ \mathbf{y}_k $ minimizes

 $ \min_{\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^k} \|\mathbf{b} - A(\mathbf{x}_0 + V_k\mathbf{y})\|_2 = \min_{\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^k} \|\mathbf{r}_0 - A V_k\mathbf{y}\|_2 $

This is a standard linear LS problem that can be solved by a QR decomposition.

One could use the Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization to find an orthonormal basis of  $ \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k) $. The algorithm is given as follows:

(1) Define  $ \mathbf{r}_0 = \mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x}_0 $ and  $ \mathbf{v}_1 = \frac{\mathbf{r}_0}{\|\mathbf{r}_0\|_2} $.

(2) Compute

 $ \mathbf{v}_{i+1} = \frac{A\mathbf{v}_i - \sum_{j=1}^{i} ((A\mathbf{v}_i)^T \mathbf{v}_j)\mathbf{v}_j}{\|A\mathbf{v}_i - \sum_{j=1}^{i} ((A\mathbf{v}_i)^T \mathbf{v}_j)\mathbf{v}_j\|_2} $

for  $ i = 1, 2, ..., k-1 $.

This algorithm produces the columns of the matrix  $ V_k $ which are also an orthonormal basis for  $ \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, k) $. We note that a breakdown happens when a division by zero occurs. We have the following theorem for the breakdown happening.

Theorem 6.14 Let  $ A $ be nonsingular, the vectors  $ \mathbf{v}_j $ be generated by the above algorithm, and  $ i $ be the smallest integer for which

 $$  A\mathbf{v}_i - \sum_{j=1}^{i} ((A\mathbf{v}_i)^T \mathbf{v}_j)\mathbf{v}_j = \mathbf{0}.  $$  (6.21)

Then  $ \mathbf{x} = A^{-1}\mathbf{b} \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, i) $.

Proof Since by (6.21),

 $$  A\mathbf{v}_i = \sum_{j=1}^{i} ((A\mathbf{v}_i)^T \mathbf{v}_j)\mathbf{v}_j \in \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, i),  $$ 

we know that

 $  A\mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, i) \subset \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, i)  $.

Note that the columns of  $ V_i = [\mathbf{v}_1 \mid \mathbf{v}_2 | ... \mid \mathbf{v}_i] $ form an orthonormal basis for  $ \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, i) $, i.e.,

 $ \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, i) = \text{span}\{\mathbf{v}_1, \mathbf{v}_2, ..., \mathbf{v}_i\} $,

and then

 $  A V_i = V_i H  $, (6.22)

100

Chapter 6 Krylov Subspace Methods

where  $ H \in \mathbb{R}^{i \times i} $ is nonsingular since  $ A $ is nonsingular. There exists a vector  $ \mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^i $ such that  $ \mathbf{x}_i - \mathbf{x}_0 = V_i \mathbf{y} $ because  $ \mathbf{x}_i - \mathbf{x}_0 \in \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, i) $. We therefore have

 $ \|\mathbf{r}_i\|_2 = \|\mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x}_i\|_2 = \|\mathbf{r}_0 - A(\mathbf{x}_i - \mathbf{x}_0)\|_2 = \|\mathbf{r}_0 - A V_i \mathbf{y}\|_2 $ (6.23)

Let  $ \beta = \|\mathbf{r}_0\|_2 $ and  $ \mathbf{e}_1 = [1, 0, \ldots, 0]^T \in \mathbb{R}^i $. Then  $ \mathbf{r}_0 = \beta V_i \mathbf{e}_1 $. Since  $ V_i $ is a matrix with orthonormal columns, we have by (6.22) and (6.23),

 $ \|\mathbf{r}_i\|_2 = \|V_i(\beta \mathbf{e}_1 - H \mathbf{y})\|_2 = \|\beta \mathbf{e}_1 - H \mathbf{y}\|_2 $

Setting  $ \mathbf{y} = \beta H^{-1} \mathbf{e}_1 $ and hence  $ \mathbf{r}_i = \mathbf{0} $, i.e.,

 $ \mathbf{x}_i = A^{-1} \mathbf{b} \in \mathbf{x}_0 + \mathcal{K}(A, \mathbf{r}_0, i) $. □

If the Gram-Schmidt process does not breakdown, we can use it to carry out the GMRES method in the following efficient way. Let  $ h_{ij} = (A \mathbf{v}_j)^T \mathbf{v}_i $. By the Gram-Schmidt algorithm, we have a  $ (k+1) $-by- $ k $ matrix  $ H_k $ which is upper Hessenberg, i.e., its entries  $ h_{ij} $ satisfy  $ h_{ij} = 0 $ if  $ i > j + 1 $. This process produces a sequence of matrices  $ \{V_k\} $ with orthonormal columns such that

 $ AV_k = V_{k+1} H_k $

Therefore, we have

 $ \mathbf{r}_k = \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x}_k = \mathbf{r}_0 - A(\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_0) = \beta V_{k+1} \mathbf{e}_1 - A V_k \mathbf{y}_k = V_{k+1} (\beta \mathbf{e}_1 - H_k \mathbf{y}_k) $,

where  $ \mathbf{y}_k $ is the solution of

 $ \min_{y \in \mathbb{R}^k} \|\beta \mathbf{e}_1 - H_k \mathbf{y}\|_2 $

Hence

 $ \mathbf{x}_k = \mathbf{x}_0 + V_k \mathbf{y}_k $

Let the GMRES iterations be ended when one finds a vector  $ \mathbf{x} $ such that for a given  $ \epsilon $,

 $ \|\mathbf{r}\|_2 = \|\mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x}\|_2 \leq \epsilon \|\mathbf{b}\|_2 $

We then have the following GMRES algorithm [70] for solving

 $ Ax = b $

where  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ and  $ \mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^n $ are known. At the initialization step, let

 $ \mathbf{r}_0 = \mathbf{b} - A \mathbf{x}_0 $,  $ \quad \beta = \|\mathbf{r}_0\|_2 $,  $ \quad \mathbf{v}_1 = \mathbf{r}_0/\beta $

6.5 GMRES method 101

In the iteration steps, we have

for  $ j = 1:m $

 $ \mathbf{w}_j = A\mathbf{v}_j $

for  $ i = 1:j $

 $ h_{ij} = \mathbf{w}_j^T\mathbf{v}_i $

 $ \mathbf{w}_j = \mathbf{w}_j - h_{ij}\mathbf{v}_i $

end

 $ h_{j+1,j} = \|\mathbf{w}_j\|_2 $; if  $ h_{j+1,j} = 0 $, set  $ m = j $ and go to (*)

 $ \mathbf{v}_{j+1} = \mathbf{w}_j/h_{j+1,j} $

end

(*) compute  $ \mathbf{y}_m $ the minimizer of  $ \|\beta e_1 - \bar{H}_m\mathbf{y}\|_2 $

 $ \mathbf{x}_m = \mathbf{x}_0 + V_m\mathbf{y}_m $

where  $ \bar{H}_m = (h_{ij})_{1 \leq i \leq m+1,1 \leq j \leq m} $. The matrix  $ V_m = [\mathbf{v}_1 \mid \mathbf{v}_2 \mid ... \mid \mathbf{v}_m] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times m} $ with  $ m \leq n $ in the algorithm is a matrix with orthonormal columns.

Exercises:

1. Suppose that  $ \mathbf{x}_k $ is generated by the steepest descent method. Prove that

 $ \phi(\mathbf{x}_k) \leq \left(1 - \frac{1}{\kappa_2(A)}\right) \phi(\mathbf{x}_{k-1}) $,

where  $ \kappa_2(A) = \|A\|_2\|A^{-1}\|_2 $.

2. Let  $ A $ be a symmetric positive definite matrix. Define  $ \|\mathbf{x}\|_A \equiv \sqrt{\mathbf{x}^T A \mathbf{x}} $. Show that it is a vector norm.

3. Let  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ be a symmetric positive definite, and  $ \mathbf{p}_1, \mathbf{p}_2, \ldots, \mathbf{p}_k \in \mathbb{R}^n $ be mutually  $ A $-conjugate, i.e.,  $ \mathbf{p}_i^T A \mathbf{p}_j = 0 $ for  $ i \neq j $. Prove that  $ \{\mathbf{p}_1, \mathbf{p}_2, \ldots, \mathbf{p}_k\} $ is linearly independent.

4. Let  $ \mathbf{x}_k $ be produced by the CG method. Show that

 $ \|\mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*\|_2 \leq 2 \sqrt{\kappa_2} \left( \frac{\sqrt{\kappa_2} - 1}{\sqrt{\kappa_2} + 1} \right)^k \|\mathbf{x}_0 - \mathbf{x}_*\|_2 $,

where  $ \kappa_2 = \kappa_2(A) = \|A\|_2\|A^{-1}\|_2 $.

5. Suppose  $ \mathbf{z}_k $ and  $ \mathbf{r}_k $ are generated by the PCG method. Show that if  $ \mathbf{r}_k \neq 0 $, then  $ \mathbf{z}_k^T \mathbf{r}_k > 0 $.

6. Find an efficient algorithm for solving  $ A^T A \mathbf{x} = A^T \mathbf{b} $ by the CG method.

7. Show that if  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ is a symmetric positive definite and has exactly  $ k $ distinct eigenvalues, then the CG method terminates in at most  $ k $ iterations.

8. Let the initial vector  $ \mathbf{x}_0 = 0 $. When the GMRES method is used to solve the linear system  $ \mathbf{A}\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b} $ where

 $ \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix} $

 $ \mathbf{A} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix} $

and  $ \mathbf{b} = [1, 0, 0, 0, 0]^T $, what is the convergence rate?

When the GMRES method is used to solve  $ \mathbf{A}\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b} $, what is the maximum number of iterations required to converge?

Chapter 7

Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

Eigenvalue problems are particularly interesting in NLA. In this chapter, we study nonsymmetric eigenvalue problems. Some well-known methods, such as the power method, the inverse power method and the QR method, are discussed.

7.1 Basic properties

Let  $  A \in C^{n \times n}  $. We recall that a complex number  $  \lambda  $ is called an eigenvalue of  $  A  $ if there exists a nonzero vector  $  \mathbf{x} \in C^n  $ such that

 $$ 

\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x} = \lambda \mathbf{x}.

 $$ 

Here  $  \mathbf{x}  $ is called the eigenvector of  $  A  $ associated with  $  \lambda  $. It is well-known that  $  \lambda  $ is an eigenvalue of  $  A  $ if and only if

 $$ 

\det(\lambda I - A) = 0.

 $$ 

Let

 $  p_A(\lambda) = \det(\lambda I - A)  $

be the characteristic polynomial of  $  A  $. By the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, we know that  $  p_A(\lambda)  $ has  $  n  $ roots in  $  \mathbb{C}  $, i.e.,  $  A  $ has  $  n  $ eigenvalues.

Now suppose that  $  p_A(\lambda)  $ has the following factorization:

 $$  p_A(\lambda) = (\lambda - \lambda_1)^{n_1} (\lambda - \lambda_2)^{n_2} \cdots (\lambda - \lambda_p)^{n_p}  $$ 

where  $  n_1 + n_2 + \cdots + n_p = n  $,  $  \lambda_i \neq \lambda_j  $ for  $  i \neq j  $. The  $  n_i  $ is called the algebraic multiplicity of  $  \lambda_i  $ and the number  $  m_i = n - \text{rank}(\lambda_i I - A)  $.

is called the geometric multiplicity of  $  \lambda_i  $. Actually,  $  m_i  $ is the dimension of the eigenspace of  $  \lambda_i  $. We remark that the eigenspace of  $  \lambda_i  $ is the solution space of  $  (\lambda_i I - A)\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{0}  $. It is easy to see that  $  m_i \leq n_i  $ for  $  i = 1, 2, \ldots, p  $. If  $  n_i = 1  $, then  $  \lambda_i  $ is called a simple eigenvalue. If  $  m_i < n_i  $ for some eigenvalue  $  \lambda_i  $, then  $  A  $ is called

104 Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

defective. If the geometric multiplicity is equal to the algebraic multiplicity for each eigenvalue of $A$, then $A$ is said to be nondefective. Note that $A$ is diagonalizable if and only if $A$ is nondefective.

Let $A, B \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$. We recall that $A$ and $B$ are called similar matrices if there is a nonsingular matrix $X \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ such that

$$B = X A X^{-1}.$$

The transformation

$$A \to B = X A X^{-1}$$

is called a similarity transformation by the similarity matrix $X$. The similar matrices have the same eigenvalues. If $\mathbf{x}$ is an eigenvector of $A$, then $\mathbf{y} = X \mathbf{x}$ is an eigenvector of $B$. By the Jordan Decomposition Theorem (Theorem 1.1), we know that any $n$-by-$n$ matrix is similar to its Jordan canonical form. If the similarity matrix is required to be a unitary matrix, we then have the following perhaps the most fundamentally useful theorem in NLA [39].

Theorem 7.1 (Schur Decomposition Theorem) Let $A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ with the eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n$ in any prescribed order. Then there exists a unitary matrix $U \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ such that

$$U^* A U = T = [t_{ij}]$,

where $T$ is an upper triangular matrix with diagonal entries $t_{ii} = \lambda_i$ ($i = 1, \ldots, n$). Furthermore, if $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and if all the eigenvalues of $A$ are real, then $U$ may be chosen to be real and orthogonal.

Proof Let $\mathbf{x}_1 \in \mathbb{C}^n$ be a normalized eigenvector of $A$ associated with the eigenvalue $\lambda_1$. The vector $\mathbf{x}_1$ can be extended to a basis of $\mathbb{C}^n$.

$$\mathbf{x}_1, \mathbf{y}_2, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_n.$$

By applying the Gram-Schmidt orthonormalization procedure to this basis, we therefore have an orthonormal basis of $\mathbb{C}^n$:

$$\mathbf{x}_1, \mathbf{z}_2, \ldots, \mathbf{z}_n.$$

Let $U_1 = [\mathbf{x}_1 \mid \mathbf{z}_2 \mid \ldots \mid \mathbf{z}_n]$$

be a unitary matrix. By a simple calculation, we obtain

7.2 Power method

105

The matrix  $  A_1 \in \mathbb{C}^{(n-1) \times (n-1)}  $ has the eigenvalues  $  \lambda_2, \ldots, \lambda_n  $. Let  $  \mathbf{x}_2 \in \mathbb{C}^{n-1}  $ be a normalized eigenvector of  $  A_1  $ associated with  $  \lambda_2  $, and do it all over again. Determine a unitary matrix  $  V_2 \in \mathbb{C}^{(n-1) \times (n-1)}  $ such that

 $$  V_2^* A_1 V_2 = \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_2 & * \\ 0 & A_2 \end{bmatrix}.  $$ 

Let

Let

 $$  U_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & V_2 \end{bmatrix}.  $$ 

Then the matrices  $  U_2  $ and  $  U_1 U_2  $ are unitary, and

 $$  U_2^* U_1^* A U_1 U_2 = \begin{bmatrix} \lambda_1 & * \\ \lambda_2 & \end{bmatrix}.  $$ 

Continuing this process, we can produce unitary matrices  $  U_1, U_2, \ldots, U_{n-1}  $ such that the matrix

 $$  U = U_1 U_2 \cdots U_{n-1}  $$ 

is unitary and  $  U^* A U  $ yields the desired form.

Theorem 7.2 (Real Schur Decomposition Theorem [39]) Let  $  A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $. Then there exists an orthogonal matrix  $  Q \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $ such that

 $$  Q^T A Q = \begin{bmatrix} R_{11} & R_{12} & \cdots & R_{1m} \\ R_{22} & \cdots & R_{2m} \\ \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & R_{mm} \end{bmatrix},  $$ 

where  $  R_{ii}  $ is either a real number or a 2-by-2 matrix having a pair of complex conjugate eigenvalues.
