In general, one cannot hope to reduce a real matrix to a strictly upper triangular form by using an orthogonal similarity transformation because the diagonal entries would then be eigenvalues, which could not be real.

7.2 Power method

The power method is an iterative algorithm for computing the eigenvalue with the largest absolute value and its corresponding eigenvector. Now, we introduce the basic idea of this method. For simplicity, we first suppose that the matrix  $  A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}  $ is diagonalizable, i.e., A has the following Jordan decomposition:

 $$  A = X \Lambda X^{-1},  $$ 

106

Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

where  $ \Lambda = \mathrm{diag}(\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n) $ with the order

 $ \lvert\lambda_1\rvert > \lvert\lambda_2\rvert \geqslant \cdots \geqslant \lvert\lambda_n\rvert, $

and  $ X = [\mathbf{x}_1 \mid \mathbf{x}_2 \mid \ldots \mid \mathbf{x}_n] \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $. Let  $ \mathbf{u}_0 \in \mathbb{C}^n $ be any vector. Since the column vectors  $ \mathbf{x}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{x}_n $ form a basis of  $ \mathbb{C}^n $, we have

 $ \mathbf{u}_0 = \sum_{j=1}^{n} \alpha_j \mathbf{x}_j, $

where  $ \alpha_j \in \mathbb{C} $. We therefore have,

and then

 $ \lim_{k \to \infty} \frac{A^k \mathbf{u}_0}{\lambda_1^k} = \alpha_1 \mathbf{x}_1. $

When  $ \alpha_1 \neq 0 $ and  $ k $ is sufficiently large, we know that the vector

is a good approximate eigenvector of  $ A $.

In practice, we cannot use (7.1) directly to compute an approximate eigenvector

since we do not know the eigenvalue  $ \lambda_1 $ in advance and the operation cost of  $ A^k $ is

very large when  $ k $ is large. We therefore propose the following iterative algorithm:

 $ \left\{\begin{array}{l} \mathbf{y}_k = A \mathbf{u}_{k-1}, \\ \mu_k = \zeta_k, \\ \mathbf{u}_k = \mathbf{y}_k / \mu_k, \end{array}\right. $

where  $ \mathbf{u}_0 \in \mathbb{C}^n $ is any given initial vector with  $ \|\mathbf{u}_0\|_\infty = 1 $ usually, and  $ \zeta_k $ is the largest absolute value of components of  $ \mathbf{y}_k $. This iterative algorithm is called the power method. We have the following theorem for the convergence of the power method.

Theorem 7.3 Let  $ A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $ with  $ p $ distinct eigenvalues which satisfy

 $ \lvert\lambda_1\rvert > \lvert\lambda_2\rvert \geqslant \cdots \geqslant \lvert\lambda_p\rvert. $

The geometric multiplicity of  $ \lambda_1 $ is equal to its algebraic multiplicity. If the projection of the initial vector  $ \mathbf{u}_0 $ on the eigenspace of  $ \lambda_1 $ is nonzero, then  $ \{\mathbf{u}_k\} $ produced by (7.2) converges to an eigenvector  $ \mathbf{x}_1 $ associated with  $ \lambda_1 $. Also  $ \{\mu_k\} $ produced by (7.2) converges to  $ \lambda_1 $.

7.2 Power method 107

Proof We note that A has the Jordan decomposition as follows,

 $  A = X \mathrm{diag}(J_1, \ldots, J_p) X^{-1},  $ (7.3)

where X \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}, J_i \in \mathbb{C}^{n_i \times n_i} is the Jordan block associated with \lambda_i (i = 1, \ldots, p), and

 $  n_1 + n_2 + \cdots + n_p = n.  $

Since the geometric multiplicity of \lambda_1 is the same as its algebraic multiplicity, we have

 $  J_1 = \lambda_1 I_{n_1},  $

where I_{n_1} \in \mathbb{R}^{n_1 \times n_1} is the identity matrix. Let \mathbf{y} = X^{-1} \mathbf{u}_0 and then decompose \mathbf{y} and X as follows:

 $ \mathbf{y} = [\mathbf{y}_1^T, \mathbf{y}_2^T, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_p^T]^T $,  $ X = [X_1 \mid X_2 \mid \ldots \mid X_p] $,

where \mathbf{y}_i \in \mathbb{C}^{n_i} and X_i \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n_i} for i = 1, \ldots, p. By using (7.3), we have

 $  A^k \mathbf{u}_0 = X \mathrm{diag}(J_1^k, \ldots, J_p^k) X^{-1} \mathbf{u}_0  $

 $  = X_1 J_1^k \mathbf{y}_1 + X_2 J_2^k \mathbf{y}_2 + \cdots + X_p J_p^k \mathbf{y}_p  $

 $  = \lambda_1^k X_1 \mathbf{y}_1 + X_2 J_2^k \mathbf{y}_2 + \cdots + X_p J_p^k \mathbf{y}_p  $

 $  = \lambda_1^k (X_1 \mathbf{y}_1 + X_2 (\lambda_1^{-1} J_2)^k \mathbf{y}_2 + \cdots + X_p (\lambda_1^{-1} J_p)^k \mathbf{y}_p)  $.

Note that the spectral radius of \lambda_1^{-1} J_i satisfies

 $ \rho(\lambda_1^{-1} J_i) = |\lambda_i| / |\lambda_1| < 1 $,

for i = 2, 3, \ldots, p. Therefore,

 $ \lim_{k \to \infty} \frac{1}{\lambda_1^k} A^k \mathbf{u}_0 = X_1 \mathbf{y}_1 $ (7.4)

Since the projection of the initial vector \mathbf{u}_0 on the eigenspace of \lambda_1 is nonzero, we have X_1 \mathbf{y}_1 \neq 0. Let

 $ \mathbf{x}_1 = \zeta^{-1} X_1 \mathbf{y}_1 $,

where \zeta is the largest absolute value of components of X_1 \mathbf{y}_1. Obviously, \mathbf{x}_1 is an eigenvector of A associated with \lambda_1. Let \zeta_k be the largest absolute value of components of A^k \mathbf{u}_0. Then the largest absolute value of components of \lambda_1^{-k} A^k \mathbf{u}_0 is \zeta_k \lambda_1^{-k}. By (7.2), we have

 $ \mathbf{u}_k = \frac{A \mathbf{u}_{k-1}}{\mu_k} = \frac{A^k \mathbf{u}_0}{\mu_k \mu_{k-1} \cdots \mu_1} = \frac{A^k \mathbf{u}_0}{\zeta_k} = \frac{A^k \mathbf{u}_0 / \lambda_1^k}{\zeta_k / \lambda_1^k} $.

By using (7.4), we know that \{\mathbf{u}_k\} is convergent and

 $ \lim_{k \to \infty} \mathbf{u}_k = \frac{k \to \infty}{\lim_{k \to \infty} (\zeta_k / \lambda_1^k)} = \frac{X_1 \mathbf{y}_1}{\zeta} = \mathbf{x}_1 $.

108

Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

By using  $  \mathbf{A}\mathbf{u}_{k-1} = \mu_k \mathbf{u}_k  $ and the fact that  $  \{\mathbf{u}_k\}  $ converges to an eigenvector asso-ciated with  $  \lambda_1  $ having the largest absolute value of components equaling to 1, we immediately know that  $  \{\mu_k\}  $ converges to  $  \lambda_1  $.

We remark that from the proof of Theorem 7.3, the convergence rate of the power method is determined by the value of  $  |\lambda_2| / |\lambda_1|  $. Under the conditions of the theorem, we know that

 $$ 

\frac{|\lambda_2|}{|\lambda_1|} < 1.

 $$ 

The smaller the  $  |\lambda_2| / |\lambda_1|  $ is, the faster the convergence rate can be. When  $  |\lambda_2| / |\lambda_1|  $ is closed to 1, then the convergence rate is very slow. In order to speed up the convergence of the power method, we could use the method on  $  A - \mu I  $ where  $  \mu  $ is called a shift. The  $  \mu  $ could be chosen such that the distance between the eigenvalue with the largest absolute value and the other eigenvalues becomes larger. Therefore, the convergence rate of the power method can be increased.

7.3 Inverse power method

If one uses the power method on  $  A^{-1}  $ to obtain an eigenvalue of  $  A  $ with the smallest absolute value and its corresponding eigenvector, then this algorithm is called the inverse power method. Its basic iterative scheme is given as follows:

 $$ 

\left\{

\begin{array}{l}

A \mathbf{y}_k = \mathbf{z}_{k-1}, \\

\mu_k = \zeta_k, \\

\mathbf{z}_k = \mathbf{y}_k / \mu_k

\end{array}

\right.

 $$ 

where  $  \mathbf{z}_0 \in \mathbb{C}^n  $ is any given initial vector and  $  \zeta_k  $ is the largest absolute value of components of  $  \mathbf{y}_k  $. From Theorem 7.3, we know that if the eigenvalues of  $  A  $ satisfy  $  |\lambda_n| < |\lambda_{n-1}| \leqslant \cdots \leqslant |\lambda_1|  $,

then  $  \{\mathbf{z}_k\}  $ converges to an eigenvector associated with  $  \lambda_n  $ and  $  \{\mu_k\}  $ converges to  $  \lambda_n^{-1}  $. The convergence rate of the inverse power method is determined by  $  |\lambda_n| / |\lambda_{n-1}|  $.

In practice, usually, the inverse power method is used on the matrix

 $  A - \mu I  $

to compute an approximate eigenvector when an approximate eigenvalue  $  \mu  $ of a distinct eigenvalue  $  \lambda_i  $ of  $  A  $ is obtained in advance. Therefore, we have the following inverse power method with a shift  $  \mu  $:

 $$ 

\left\{

\begin{array}{l}

(A - \mu I) \mathbf{v}_k = \mathbf{z}_{k-1}, \\

\mathbf{z}_k = \mathbf{v}_k / \| \mathbf{v}_k \|_2.

\end{array}

\right.

 $$ 

7.4 QR method 109

From (7.5), we know that in each iteration of the inverse power method, one needs to solve a linear system. Hence, its operation cost is much larger than that of the power method. However, one could use the LU factorization with partial pivoting in advance and then in each iteration later on, one only needs to solve two triangular systems.

Suppose that the eigenvalues of the  $ A - \mu I $ are ordered as follows:

 $ 0 < |\lambda_1 - \mu| < |\lambda_2 - \mu| \leqslant |\lambda_3 - \mu| \leqslant \cdots \leqslant |\lambda_n - \mu| $.

From Theorem 7.3 again, we know that the sequence  $ \{z_k\} $ produced by (7.5) converges to an eigenvector associated with  $ \lambda_1 $. The convergence rate is determined by the value of  $ |\lambda_1 - \mu| / |\lambda_2 - \mu| $. The more closer of  $ \mu $ to  $ \lambda_1 $, the faster the convergence rate will be. But if  $ \mu $ is closed to an eigenvalue of  $ A $, then  $ A - \mu I $ is closed to a singular matrix. Therefore, one needs to solve an ill-conditioned linear system in each iteration of the inverse power method. However, from practical computations, the illness of systems has no effect on the convergence rate of the method. Usually, only one iteration could produce a good approximate eigenvector of  $ A $ if  $ \mu $ is closed to an eigenvalue of  $ A $.

7.4 QR method

In this section, we introduce the well-known QR method which is one of main important developments in matrix computations. For any given  $ A_0 = A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $, the basic iterative scheme of the QR algorithm is given as follows:

 $ \left\{ \begin{array}{l} A_{m-1} = Q_m R_m, \\ A_m = R_m Q_m, \end{array} \right. $

(7.6)

for  $ m = 1, 2, \ldots $, where  $ Q_m $ is a unitary matrix and  $ R_m $ is an upper triangular matrix. For simplicity in later analysis, we require that diagonal entries of  $ R_m $ are nonnegative. By (7.6), one can easily obtain

 $ A_m = Q_m^* A_{m-1} Q_m $, (7.7)

i.e., each matrix in the sequence  $ \{A_m\} $ is similar to the matrix  $ A $. By using (7.7) again and again, we have

 $ A_m = \widetilde{Q}_m^* A \widetilde{Q}_m $, (7.8)

where  $ \widetilde{Q}_m = Q_1 Q_2 \cdots Q_m $. Substituting  $ A_m = Q_{m+1} R_{m+1} $ into (7.8), we obtain

 $ \widetilde{Q}_m Q_{m+1} R_{m+1} = A \widetilde{Q}_m $.

Therefore,

 $ \widetilde{Q}_m Q_{m+1} R_{m+1} R_m \cdots R_1 = A \widetilde{Q}_m R_m \cdots R_1 $,

110 Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

i.e.,

 $ \widetilde{Q}_{m+1}\widetilde{R}_{m+1} = A\widetilde{Q}_{m}\widetilde{R}_{m}, $

where  $ \widetilde{R}_{k} = R_{k}R_{k-1}\cdots R_{1} $ for  $ k = m, m+1 $. Moreover, we have

 $ A^{m} = \widetilde{Q}_{m}\widetilde{R}_{m}. $ (7.9)

Theorem 7.4 Suppose that the eigenvalues of A satisfy:

 $ |\lambda_{1}| > |\lambda_{2}| > \cdots > |\lambda_{n}| > 0. $

Let Y be a matrix and  $ \mathbf{y}_{i}^{T} $ denote the ith row of Y satisfying

 $ \mathbf{y}_{i}^{T}A = \lambda_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{T}. $

If Y has an LU factorization, then the entries under the diagonal of the matrix

 $ A_{m} = [\alpha_{ij}^{(m)}] $

produced by (7.6) tend to zero as  $ m \to \infty $. At the same time,

 $ \alpha_{ii}^{(m)} \to \lambda_{i}, $

for  $ i = 1,2,\ldots,n. $

Proof Let

 $ X = Y^{-1},\quad\Lambda = \text{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}). $

Then  $ A = X\Lambda Y $. By assumption, Y has an LU factorization

 $ Y = LU, $

where L is a unit lower triangular matrix and U is an upper triangular matrix.

Hence

 $ A^{m} = X\Lambda^{m}Y = X\Lambda^{m}LU = X(\Lambda^{m}L\Lambda^{-m})\Lambda^{m}U $

 $ = X(I + E_{m})\Lambda^{m}U, $ (7.10)

where  $ I + E_{m} = \Lambda^{m}L\Lambda^{-m} $. Since L is a unit lower triangular matrix and  $ |\lambda_{i}| < |\lambda_{j}| $ for i > j, we have

 $ \lim_{m \to \infty} E_{m} = 0. $ (7.11)

Let X = QR where Q is a unitary matrix and R is an upper triangular matrix. Since X is nonsingular, we can require that diagonal entries of R are positive. Substituting

X = QR into (7.10), we obtain

 $ A^{m} = QR(I + E_{m})\Lambda^{m}U = Q(I + RE_{m}R^{-1})R\Lambda^{m}U. $ (7.12)

7.4 QR method 111

When m is sufficiently large, I + RE_m R^{-1} is nonsingular and has the following QR decomposition,

 $$  I + RE_m R^{-1} = \widehat{Q}_m \widehat{R}_m, \tag{7.13}  $$ 

where diagonal entries of \widehat{R}_m are positive. By using (7.11) and (7.13), it is easy to show that

 $ \lim_{m \to \infty} \widehat{Q}_m = \lim_{m \to \infty} \widehat{R}_m = I. $ (7.14)

Substituting (7.13) into (7.12), we have

 $$  A^m = (Q\widehat{Q}_m)(\widehat{R}_m R \Lambda^m U),  $$ 

which is a QR decomposition of A^m. In order to guarantee all the diagonal entries of the upper triangular matrix to be positive, we define

 $$  D_1 = \text{diag}\left(\frac{\lambda_1}{|\lambda_1|}, \ldots, \frac{\lambda_n}{|\lambda_n|}\right),  $$ 

and

 $$  D_2 = \text{diag}\left(\frac{u_{11}}{|u_{11}|}, \ldots, \frac{u_{nn}}{|u_{nn}|}\right),  $$ 

where u_{ii} is the ith diagonal entry of U. Hence, we have

 $$  A^m = (Q\widehat{Q}_m D_1^m D_2)(D_2^{-1} D_1^{-m} \widehat{R}_m R \Lambda^m U).  $$ 

Comparing with (7.9) and noting that the QR decomposition is unique, we obtain

 $ \widetilde{Q}_m = Q\widehat{Q}_m D_1^m D_2, \quad \widetilde{R}_m = D_2^{-1} D_1^{-m} \widehat{R}_m R \Lambda^m U.  $

Substituting them into (7.8), we have

 $$  A_m = D_2^*(D_1^*)^m \widehat{Q}_m Q^* A Q \widehat{Q}_m D_1^m D_2.  $$ 

Note that

 $$  A = X \Lambda Y = X \Lambda X^{-1} = Q R \Lambda R^{-1} Q^*.  $$ 

We finally have

 $$  A_m = D_2^*(D_1^*)^m \widehat{Q}_m^* R \Lambda R^{-1} \widehat{Q}_m D_1^m D_2.  $$ 

When m \to \infty, we know that by (7.14) the entries under the diagonal of the matrix A_m produced by (7.6) tend to zero. At the same time,

 $ \alpha_{ii}^{(m)} \to \lambda_i,  $

for i=1,2,\ldots,n. \Box

From Theorem 7.4, we know that the sequence \{A_m\} produced by (7.6) converges to the Schur decomposition of A.

112

Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

7.5 Real version of QR algorithm

If  $  A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $, we wish that we could find a fast and effective QR algorithm with real number operations only. We concentrate on developing the real analog of (7.6) as follows: let  $  A_1 = A  $ and then construct an iterative algorithm:

 $ \begin{cases} A_m = Q_m R_m, \\ A_{m+1} = R_m Q_m, \end{cases} $ (7.15)

for  $  m = 1, 2, \ldots  $, where  $  Q_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $ is an orthogonal matrix and  $  R_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $ is an upper triangular matrix. A difficulty associated with (7.15) is that  $  A_m  $ can never converge to a strictly upper triangular form in the case that  $  A  $ has complex eigenvalues. By Theorem 7.2, we can expect that (7.15) converges to the real Schur decomposition of A.

We note that in practice that (7.15) is not a good iterative method because the following two reasons:

(i) the operation cost in each iteration is too large;

(ii) the convergence rate is too slow.

We therefore need to reduce the operation cost in each iteration and increase the convergence rate of the method. Thus, the upper Hessenberg reduction and the shift strategy are introduced.

7.5.1 Upper Hessenberg reduction

We construct an orthogonal matrix  $  Q_0  $ such that

 $  Q_0^T A Q_0 = H  $ (7.16)

has some special structure (Hessenberg) with many zero entries. Afterwards, we can apply the QR algorithm (7.15) to the matrix  $  H  $ in (7.16). Then the operation cost per iteration can be dramatically reduced.

For  $  A = [\alpha_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $, at the first step, we can choose a Householder transformation  $  H_1  $ such that the first column of  $  H_1 A  $ has many zero entries (at most  $  n - 1  $ zero entries). In order to keep a similarity transformation, we need to add one more column transformation:

 $  H_1 A H_1  $.

Hence  $  H_1  $ could have the following form

 $  H_1 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & \tilde{H}_1 \end{bmatrix}  $ (7.17)

7.5 Real version of QR algorithm 113

to keep the zero entries in the first column unchanged. By using  $ H_1 $ defined as in (7.17), we have

 $$ H_1A H_1 = \begin{bmatrix} \alpha_{11} & a_2^T \widetilde{H}_1 \\ \widetilde{H}_1a_1 & \widetilde{H}_1A_{22} \widetilde{H}_1 \end{bmatrix} (7.18) $$ 

where  $ a_1^T = [\alpha_{21}, \alpha_{31}, \ldots, \alpha_{n1}] $,  $ a_2^T = [\alpha_{12}, \alpha_{13}, \ldots, \alpha_{1n}] $, and  $ A_{22} $ is the  $ (n-1) $-by- $ (n-1) $ principal submatrix in the lower right corner of A. We know by (7.18) that the best choice of the Householder transformation  $ \widetilde{H}_1 $ should be

 $ \widetilde{H}_1a_1 = p e_1 $, (7.19)

where  $ p \in \mathbb{R} $ and  $ e_1 \in \mathbb{R}^{n-1} $ is the first unit vector. Therefore, the first column of the matrix in (7.18) has  $ n-2 $ zeros by using  $ H_1 $ defined by (7.17) and (7.19).

Afterwards, for  $ \widetilde{A}_{22} = \widetilde{H}_1A_{22} \widetilde{H}_1 $, we could find a Householder transformation

 $ \widetilde{H}_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & \widetilde{H}_2 \end{bmatrix} $

such that

 $ (\widetilde{H}_2\widetilde{A}_{22}\widetilde{H}_2)e_1 = [*,*,0,\ldots,0]^T $.

Let

 $ \begin{bmatrix} H_1 & h_{12} & \cdots & * \\ h_{21} & h_{22} & \vdots & \vdots \\ 0 & h_{32} & \vdots & * \\ 0 & 0 & \vdots & * \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & \cdots & * \end{bmatrix} $

We therefore have

 $ H_2H_1A H_1H_2 = \begin{bmatrix} h_{11} & h_{12} & \cdots & * \\ h_{21} & h_{22} & \vdots & \vdots \\ 0 & h_{32} & \vdots & * \\ 0 & 0 & \vdots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & \cdots & * \end{bmatrix} $

After  $ n-2 $ steps, we have found  $ n-2 $ Householder transformations  $ H_1, H_2, \ldots, H_{n-2} $, such that

 $ H_{n-2} \cdots H_2H_1A H_1H_2 \cdots H_{n-2} = H $,

where

 $ \begin{bmatrix} h_{11} & h_{12} & h_{13} & \cdots & h_{1,n-1} & h_{1n} \\ h_{21} & h_{22} & h_{23} & \cdots & h_{2,n-1} & h_{2n} \\ 0 & h_{32} & h_{33} & \cdots & h_{3,n-1} & h_{3n} \end{bmatrix} H = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & h_{43} & \vdots & \vdots \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & h_{n,n-1} & h_{nn} \end{bmatrix} $

114 Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

with  $ h_{ij} = 0 $ for  $ i > j + 1 $, and is called the upper Hessenberg matrix. Let

 $ Q_0 = H_1 H_2 \cdots H_{n-2} $

and therefore,

 $ Q_0^T A Q_0 = H $

which is called the upper Hessenberg decomposition of A. We have the following algorithm by using Householder transformations.

Algorithm 7.1 (Upper Hessenberg decomposition)

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

\text{for } k = 1 : n - 2 \\

\text{ } [\mathbf{v}, \beta] = \text{house}(A(k + 1 : n, k)) \\

\text{ } A(k + 1 : n, k : n) = (I - \beta \mathbf{v} \mathbf{v}^T) A(k + 1 : n, k : n) \\

\text{ } A(1 : n, k + 1 : n) = A(1 : n, k + 1 : n) (I - \beta \mathbf{v} \mathbf{v}^T)

\end{array}

\right. $

end

The operation cost of the Hessenberg decomposition by Householder transformation is  $ \mathcal{O}(n^3) $. Of course, the Hessenberg decomposition can also be obtained by using Givens rotations but the operation cost will be double of that by using Householder transformations. Although the Hessenberg decomposition of a matrix is not unique, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 7.5 Suppose that  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ has the following two upper Hessenberg decompositions:

 $ U^T A U = H, \quad V^T A V = G, \quad (7.20) $

where

 $ U = [\mathbf{u}_1 \mid \mathbf{u}_2 \mid \ldots \mid \mathbf{u}_n], \quad V = [\mathbf{v}_1 \mid \mathbf{v}_2 \mid \ldots \mid \mathbf{v}_n] $

are  $ n $-by- $ n $ orthogonal matrices, and  $ H = [h_{ij}] $,  $ G = [g_{ij}] $ are upper Hessenberg matrices. If  $ \mathbf{u}_1 = \mathbf{v}_1 $ and all the entries  $ h_{i+1,i} $ are not zero, then there exists a diagonal matrix  $ D $ with diagonal entries being either 1 or -1 such that

 $ U = V D, \quad H = D G D $

Proof Assume that we have proved for some  $ m, 1 \leq m < n $, that

 $ \mathbf{u}_j = \epsilon_j \mathbf{v}_j, \quad j = 1, 2, \ldots, m, $

(7.21)

where  $ \epsilon_1 = 1 $,  $ \epsilon_j = 1 $ or  $ -1 $ ( $ j = 2, 3, \ldots, m $). Now, we want to show that there exists  $ \epsilon_{m+1} = 1 $ or  $ -1 $ such that

 $ \mathbf{u}_{m+1} = \epsilon_{m+1} \mathbf{v}_{m+1} $.

7.5 Real version of QR algorithm 115

From (7.20), we have

 $ AU = UH, \quad AV = VG. $

Comparing with the mth column of above matrix equalities respectively, we obtain

 $ Au_m = h_{1m}u_1 + \cdots + h_{mm}u_m + h_{m+1,m}u_{m+1}, $ (7.22)

and

 $ Av_m = g_{1m}v_1 + \cdots + g_{mm}v_m + g_{m+1,m}v_{m+1}. $ (7.23)

Multiplying (7.22) and (7.23) by  $ \mathbf{u}_i^T $ and  $ \mathbf{v}_i^T $, respectively, we have

 $ h_{im} = \mathbf{u}_i^T A u_m,\quad g_{im} = \mathbf{v}_i^T A v_m,\quad i=1,2,\ldots,m. $

Therefore by (7.21),

 $ h_{im} = \epsilon_i \epsilon_m g_{im},\quad i=1,2,\ldots,m. $ (7.24)

Substituting (7.24) into (7.22), and using (7.21) and (7.23), we obtain

 $ h_{m+1,m}u_{m+1} = \epsilon_m(Av_m - \epsilon_1^2g_{1m}v_1 - \cdots - \epsilon_m^2g_{mm}v_m) $

 $ = \epsilon_m(Av_m - g_{1m}v_1 - \cdots - g_{mm}v_m) $

 $ = \epsilon_m g_{m+1,m}v_{m+1}. $ (7.25)

Hence,

 $ |h_{m+1,m}| = |g_{m+1,m}|. $

Since  $ h_{m+1,m} \neq 0 $, from (7.25), we know that

 $ \mathbf{u}_{m+1} = \epsilon_{m+1}v_{m+1}, $

where  $ \epsilon_{m+1} = 1 $ or -1. By induction, the proof is complete. □

We note that for an upper Hessenberg matrix  $ H = [h_{ij}] $, if  $ h_{i+1,i} \neq 0 $ ( $ i = 1,2,\ldots,n-1 $), then it is irreducible. Theorem 7.5 said that if  $ Q^T A Q = H $ is irreducible upper Hessenberg where Q is an orthogonal matrix, then Q and H are determined completely by the first column of Q (up to  $ \pm 1 $).

Now, suppose that  $ H \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ is an upper Hessenberg matrix, we want to apply a QR iteration to H. Firstly, we need to construct a QR decomposition for H. Since H has a special structure, we can use n - 1 Givens rotations to obtain the QR decomposition. For simplicity, in the following, we consider the case of n = 5. Suppose that we have already found two Givens rotations  $ P_{12} $ and  $ P_{23} $ such that

 $ P_{23}P_{12}H = \begin{bmatrix} x & x & x & x & x \\ 0 & x & x & x & x \\ 0 & 0 & h_{33} & x & x \\ 0 & 0 & h_{43} & x & x \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & x & x \end{bmatrix}. $

116

Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

Then we construct a Givens rotation  $ P_{34} = G(3,4,\theta_{3}) $ such that the  $ \theta_{3} $ satisfies

 $ \left[\begin{array}{c} \cos\theta_{3} \quad \sin\theta_{3} \\ -\sin\theta_{3} \quad \cos\theta_{3} \end{array}\right]\left[\begin{array}{c} h_{33} \\ h_{43} \end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}{c} x \\ 0 \end{array}\right] $.

Hence,

 $ P_{34}P_{23}P_{12}H = \begin{bmatrix} x & x & x & x \\ 0 & x & x & x \\ 0 & 0 & x & x \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & x \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & x \end{bmatrix} $.

Therefore, it is easy to see that for  $ n $-by- $ n $ upper Hessenberg matrix  $ H $, we can construct  $ n-1 $ Givens rotations  $ P_{12} $,  $ P_{23} $, ...,  $ P_{n-1,n} $ such that

 $ P_{n-1,n}P_{n-2,n-1} \cdots P_{12}H = R $

is an upper triangular matrix. Let

 $ Q = (P_{n-1,n}P_{n-2,n-1} \cdots P_{12})^{T} $.

Then we have  $ H = QR $. In order to complete a QR iteration, we have to compute

 $ \widetilde{H} = RQ = RP_{12}^{T}P_{23}^{T} \cdots P_{n-1,n}^{T} $.

Note that  $ RP_{12}^{T} $ is different from  $ R $ only in the first two columns. Since  $ R $ is an upper triangular matrix,  $ RP_{12}^{T} $ should have the following form (for  $ n=5 $),

 $ RP_{12}^{T} = \begin{bmatrix} x & x & x & x \\ x & x & x & x \\ 0 & 0 & x & x \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & x \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} $.

Similarly,  $ RP_{12}^{T}P_{23}^{T} $ is different from  $ RP_{12}^{T} $ only in the second column and the third column. Hence,

 $ RP_{12}^{T}P_{23}^{T} = \begin{bmatrix} x & x & x & x \\ x & x & x & x \\ 0 & x & x & x \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & x \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} $.

Continuously, we finally obtain the matrix  $ \widetilde{H} $ which is also an upper Hessenberg matrix. It is easy to know that the operation cost of a QR iteration for an upper Hessenberg matrix is  $ \mathcal{O}(n^{2}) $. Note that the operation cost of a QR iteration for a general matrix is  $ \mathcal{O}(n^{3}) $.

7.5 Real version of QR algorithm 117

7.5.2 QR iteration with single shift

Now, we only need to discuss the Hessenberg form. From Theorem 7.4, we know that the convergence rate of the basic QR algorithm is linear and is depending on the distance between eigenvalues. In order to speed up the convergence rate, we introduce the shift strategy. The QR iteration with a single shift is given as follows:

$$\left\{ \begin{array} { l } { H _ { m } - \mu _ { m } I = Q _ { m } R _ { m } , } \\ { H _ { m + 1 } = R _ { m } Q _ { m } + \mu _ { m } I , } \end{array} \right.$$

(7.26)

for $m = 1, 2, \ldots$, where $H_1 = H \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n \times n }$ is a given upper Hessenberg matrix satisfying the conditions of Theorem 7.4 and $\mu _ { m } \in \mathbb { R}$. We consider how to choose a shift if all the eigenvalues of $H$ are assumed to be real. Since $H _ { m }$ is upper Hessenberg, there are only two nonzero entries $h _ { n , n - 1 } ^ { ( m ) }$ and $h _ { n n } ^ { ( m ) }$ in the last row (for $n = 5$):

$$\left[ \begin{array} { l l l l } { \times } & { \times } & { \times } & { \times } \\ { \times } & { \times } & { \times } & { \times } \\ { 0 } & { \times } & { \times } & { \times } \\ { 0 } & { 0 } & { \times } & { \times } \\ { 0 } & { 0 } & { 0 } & { h _ { n , n - 1 } ^ { ( m ) } } \; h _ { n n } ^ { ( m ) } \; } \end{array} \right]$$

When the QR algorithm converges, $h _ { n , n - 1 } ^ { ( m ) }$ is very small and $h _ { n n } ^ { ( m ) }$ approaches to an eigenvalue of $H$. Therefore, we can choose a shift $\mu _ { m } = h _ { n n } ^ { ( m ) }$. In fact, we can prove that if $h _ { n , n - 1 } ^ { ( m ) } = \epsilon$ is very small, then after one iteration of the QR algorithm, we have

$$h _ { n , n - 1 } ^ { ( m + 1 ) } = \mathcal { O } ( \epsilon ^ { 2 } )$$

(7.27)

From the discussion above, we know that there are $n - 1$ steps to reduce $H _ { m } - h _ { n n } ^ { ( m ) } I$ to be an upper triangular matrix. Assume that the first $n - 2$ steps are completed:

$$\left[ \begin{array} { l l l l } { \times } & { \times } & { \times } & { \times } \\ { 0 } & { \times } & { \times } & { \times } \\ { 0 } & { 0 } & { \times } & { \times } \\ { 0 } & { 0 } & { 0 } & { \alpha } \\ { 0 } & { 0 } & { 0 } & { \epsilon } \end{array} \right]$$

Actually, we only need to study the 2-by-2 submatrix

$$H _ { m 2 } = \left[ \begin{array} { l } { \alpha } \; \beta } \\ { \epsilon } \; 0 } \end{array} \right]$$

in the lower right corner of the matrix $\widehat { H }$. In the $(n - 1)$th step of reduction, we want to determine $c = \cos \theta$ and $s = \sin \theta$ such that

$$\left[ \begin{array} { l } { c } \; s } \\ { - s } \; c } \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array} { l } { \alpha } \\ { \epsilon } \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array} { l } { \sigma } \\ { 0 } \end{array} \right]$$

118 Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

It is easy to see that

 $$  c = \alpha / \sigma, \qquad s = \epsilon / \sigma, \qquad \sigma = \sqrt{\alpha^2 + \epsilon^2}.  $$ 

Thus, after a few simple computations on the 2-by-2 submatrix in the lower right corner of the matrix  $ H_{m+1} = R_m Q_m + h_{nn}^{(m)} I $, we have

 $$  h_{n,n-1}^{(m+1)} = -s^2 \beta = -\beta \epsilon^2 / \sigma^2 = \mathcal{O}(\epsilon^2),  $$ 

i.e., (7.27) holds. Through a shift, the convergence rate of the QR iteration is expected to be quadratic. If  $ H $ has complex eigenvalues, then a double shift strategy can be used to speed up the convergence rate of the QR iteration.

7.5.3 QR iteration with double shift

Note that difficulties with (7.26) can be expected if the submatrix

 $$  G = \begin{bmatrix} h_{pp}^{(m)} & h_{pn}^{(m)} \\ h_{np}^{(m)} & h_{nn}^{(m)} \end{bmatrix}, \qquad p = n - 1  $$ 

in the lower right corner of  $ H_m $ has a pair of complex conjugate eigenvalues  $ \mu_1 $ and  $ \mu_2 $. We cannot expect that  $ h_{nn}^{(m)} $ tends to an eigenvalue of A. A way around this difficulty is to perform the following QR algorithm with double shifts:

 $$ 

\begin{cases}

H - \mu_1 I = Q_1 R_1, \\

H_1 = R_1 Q_1 + \mu_1 I, \\

H_1 - \mu_2 I = Q_2 R_2, \\

H_2 = R_2 Q_2 + \mu_2 I,

\end{cases}

 $$ 

where  $ H = H_m $. Let

 $$  M \equiv (H - \mu_1 I)(H - \mu_2 I).  $$ 

(7.29) By a few simple computations, we have

 $$  M = QR, \quad (7.30)  $$ 

and

 $$  H_2 = Q^T HQ, \quad (7.31)  $$ 

where

 $$  Q = Q_1 Q_2, \quad R = R_2 R_1.  $$ 

By (7.29), we obtain

 $$  M = H^2 - sH + tI,  $$ 

7.5 Real version of QR algorithm 119

where

 $ s = \mu_1 + \mu_2 = h_{pp}^{(m)} + h_{nn}^{(m)} \in \mathbb{R} $,

and

 $ t = \mu_1 \mu_2 = \det(G) \in \mathbb{R} $.

Hence M is also real. If  $ \mu_1 $,  $ \mu_2 $ are not eigenvalues of H and diagonal entries of  $ R_1 $,  $ R_2 $ in each iteration are chosen to be positive, then by using (7.30), Q is also real. By (7.31), it then follows that  $ H_2 $ is real. Therefore, under an assumption of without rounding error, by using (7.28),  $ H_2 $ is still a real upper Hessenberg matrix. In practice, because of rounding error, usually,  $ H_2 $ is not real. In order to keep the reality of  $ H_2 $, by using (7.30) and (7.31), we propose the following process to compute  $ H_2 $:

(1) Compute  $ M = H^2 - sH + tI $.

(2) Compute the QR decomposition of  $ M $:  $ M = QR $.

(3) Compute  $ H_2 = Q^T HQ $.

Note that the operation cost of forming the matrix M in (1) needs  $ \mathcal{O}(n^3) $. We remark that actually, we do not need to form matrix M explicitly [30]. For a practical implementation of the QR algorithm with the shift strategy, we refer to [30, 32, 98].

Exercises:

1. Show that if  $ T \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $ is upper triangular and normal, then T is diagonal.

2. Let  $ A $,  $ B \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $. Prove that the spectrum of AB is equal to the spectrum of BA.

3. Let  $ A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $,  $ \mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{C}^n $ and  $ X = [\mathbf{x} \mid A\mathbf{x} | ... \mid A^{n-1}\mathbf{x}] $. Show that if X is nonsingular, then  $ X^{-1} A X $ is an upper Hessenberg matrix.

4. Suppose that  $ A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n} $ has distinct eigenvalues. Show that if  $ Q^* A Q = T $ is the Schur decomposition and AB = BA, then  $ Q^* B Q $ is upper triangular.

5. Suppose that  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ and  $ \mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^n $. Find a detailed algorithm for computing an orthogonal matrix Q such that  $ Q^T A Q $ is upper Hessenberg and  $ Q^T \mathbf{z} $ is a multiple of  $ e_1 $ where  $ e_1 $ is the first unit vector.

6. Suppose that  $ W $,  $ Y \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ and define matrices  $ C $,  $ B $ by

 $ C = W + iY $,  $ B = \begin{bmatrix} W & -Y \\ Y & W \end{bmatrix} $.

Show that if  $ \lambda \in \mathbb{R} $ is an eigenvalue of  $ C $, then  $ \lambda $ is also an eigenvalue of  $ B $. What is the relation between two corresponding eigenvectors?

120 Chapter 7 Nonsymmetric Eigenvalue Problems

7. Suppose that

 $ A = \begin{bmatrix} w & x \\ y & z \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{2 \times 2} $

has eigenvalues  $ \lambda \pm i\mu $, where  $ \mu \neq 0 $. Find an algorithm that determines  $ c = \cos\theta $ and  $ s = \sin\theta $ stably such that

 $ \begin{bmatrix} c & s \\ -s & c \end{bmatrix}^T \begin{bmatrix} w & x \\ y & z \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} c & s \\ -s & c \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \lambda & \beta \\ \alpha & \lambda \end{bmatrix} $,

where  $ \alpha\beta = -\mu^2 $.

8. Find a 2-by-2 diagonal matrix  $ D $ that minimizes  $ \|D^{-1}AD\|_F $ where

 $ A = \begin{bmatrix} w & x \\ y & z \end{bmatrix} $.

9. Let  $ H = H_1 $ be a given matrix. We generate matrices  $ H_k $ via

 $ H_k - \mu_k I = Q_k R_k $,  $ H_{k+1} = R_k Q_k + \mu_k I $.

Show that

 $ (Q_1 \cdots Q_j)(R_j \cdots R_1) = (H - \mu_1 I) \cdots (H - \mu_j I) $.

10. Show that if

 $ Y = \begin{bmatrix} I & Z \\ 0 & I \end{bmatrix} $,

then

 $ \kappa_2(Y) \equiv \|Y\|_2 \|Y^{-1}\|_2 = \frac{1}{2} \left(2 + \sigma^2 + \sqrt{4\sigma^2 + \sigma^4}\right) $,

where  $ \sigma = \|Z\|_2 $.

11. Let  $ A $ be a matrix with real diagonal entries. Show that

 $ |\text{Im}(\lambda)| \leq \max_{j \neq i} \sum |a_{ij}| $,

where  $ \lambda $ is any eigenvalue of  $ A $ and  $ \text{Im}(\cdot) $ denotes the imaginary part of a complex number.

12. Show that if

 $ \frac{1}{2}(A + A^T) $

is positive definite, then  $ \text{Re}(\lambda) > 0 $, where  $ \lambda $ is any eigenvalue of  $ A $ and  $ \text{Re}(\cdot) $ denotes the real part of a complex number.

13. Let  $ B $ be a matrix with  $ \|B\|_2 < 1 $. Show that  $ I - B $ is nonsingular and the eigenvalues of  $ I + 2(B - I)^{-1} $ have negative real parts.

Chapter 8

Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

The symmetric eigenvalue problem with its nice properties and rich mathematical theory is one of the most pleasing topics in NLA. In this chapter, we study symmetric eigenvalue problems. We begin by introducing some basic spectral properties of symmetric matrices. Then the symmetric QR method, the Jacobi method and the bisection method are discussed. Finally, a divide-and-conquer algorithm is described.

8.1 Basic spectral properties

We first introduce some basic properties of eigenvalues and eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $. It is well known that the eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix  $ A $ are real and there is an orthonormal basis of  $ \mathbb{R}^n $ formed by the eigenvectors of  $ A $.

Theorem 8.1 (Spectral Decomposition Theorem [39]) If  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ is symmetric, then there exists an orthogonal matrix  $ Q \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ such that

 $$  Q^T A Q = \text{diag}(\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n)  $$ 

The eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix have minimax properties based on the values called the Rayleigh quotient:

 $$  \frac{x^T A x}{x^T x}  $$ 

We have the following theorem and its proof can be found in [32].

Theorem 8.2 (Courant-Fischer Minimax Theorem) Let  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ be symmetric and its eigenvalues be ordered as

 $ \lambda_1 \geq \cdots \geq \lambda_n $.

Then

 $ \lambda_i = \max_{\text{dim}(S) = i} \min_{0 \neq u \in S} \frac{u^T A u}{u^T u} = \min_{\text{dim}(S) = n} \max_{i+1} \frac{u^T A u}{u^T u} $

where  $ S $ is any subspace of  $ \mathbb{R}^n $.

122

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

The next theorem [95] shows the sensitivity of eigenvalues of symmetric matrices.

Theorem 8.3 (Weyl, Wielandt-Hoffman Theorem) If $A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ are symmetric matrices and the eigenvalues of $A, B$ are ordered respectively as follows,

$\lambda_1(A) \geqslant \cdots \geqslant \lambda_n(A), \quad \mu_1(B) \geqslant \cdots \geqslant \mu_n(B)$,

then

$|\lambda_i(A) - \mu_i(B)| \leqslant \|A - B\|_2, \quad i = 1, 2, \ldots, n$,

and

$\sum_{i=1}^{n} (\lambda_i(A) - \mu_i(B))^2 \leqslant \|A - B\|_F$.

Theorem 8.3 tells us that the eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix are well conditioned, i.e., small perturbations on the entries of $A$ cause only small changes in the eigenvalues of $A$.

Theorem 8.4 (Cauchy Interlace Theorem [39,95]) If $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is symmetric and $A_r$ denotes the $r$-by-$r$ leading principal submatrix of $A$, then

$\lambda_{r+1}(A_{r+1}) \leqslant \lambda_r(A_r) \leqslant \lambda_r(A_{r+1}) \leqslant \cdots \leqslant \lambda_2(A_{r+1}) \leqslant \lambda_1(A_r) \leqslant \lambda_1(A_{r+1})$,

for $r = 1, 2, \ldots, n-1$.

As for the sensitivity of eigenvectors, we have the following theorem [95].

Theorem 8.5 Suppose $A, A + E \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ are symmetric matrices and

$Q = [\mathbf{q}_1 \mid Q_2] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$

is an orthogonal matrix where $\mathbf{q}_1$ is a unit eigenvector of $A$. Partition the matrices $Q^T A Q$ and $Q^T E Q$ as follows:

$Q^T A Q = \begin{bmatrix} \lambda & 0 \\ 0 & D_{22} \end{bmatrix}, \quad Q^T E Q = \begin{bmatrix} \epsilon & \mathbf{e}^T \\ \mathbf{e} & E_{22} \end{bmatrix}$,

where $D_{22}, E_{22} \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-1) \times (n-1oked)}$. If

$d = \min_{\mu} |\lambda - \mu| > 0, \quad \|E\|_2 \leqslant d/4$,

where $\mu$ is any eigenvalue of $D_{22}$, then there exists a unit eigenvector $\tilde{\mathbf{q}}_1$ such that

$\sin \theta = \sqrt{1 - |\mathbf{q}_1^T \tilde{\mathbf{q}}_1|^2} \leqslant \frac{4}{d} \|\mathbf{e}\|_2 \leqslant \frac{4}{d} \|E\|_2$,

where $\theta = \arccos |\mathbf{q}_1^T \tilde{\mathbf{q}}_1|$.

8.2 Symmetric QR method

123

It seems that \theta could be a good measurement between q_1 and q_1. We can see that the sensitivity to a perturbation of a single eigenvector depends on the separation of its corresponding eigenvalue from the rest of eigenvalues.

The eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix are closely related with the singular values of the matrix. The singular value decomposition [32] is essential in NLA.

Theorem 8.6 (Singular Value Decomposition Theorem) Let A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} with rank(A) = r. Then there exist orthogonal matrices

 $$  U = [\mathbf{u}_1 \mid \mathbf{u}_2 \mid \ldots \mid \mathbf{u}_m] \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}, \qquad V = [\mathbf{v}_1 \mid \mathbf{v}_2 \mid \ldots \mid \mathbf{v}_n] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $$ 

such that

 $$  U^T A V = \begin{bmatrix} \Sigma_r & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix},  $$ 

where

 $ \Sigma_r = \text{diag}(\sigma_1, \ldots, \sigma_r) $

with  $ \sigma_1 \geqslant \sigma_2 \geqslant \cdots \geqslant \sigma_r > 0 $.

The  $ \sigma_i $ (i = 1, 2, \ldots, n) are called the singular values of A. The vectors u_i and v_i are called the ith left singular vector and the ith right singular vector respectively. The next corollary is the Weyl, Wielandt-Hoffman Theorem for the singular values.

Corollary 8.1 Let A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} and their singular values be ordered respectively as follows:

 $ \sigma_1(A) \geqslant \cdots \geqslant \sigma_n(A), \quad \tau_1(B) \geqslant \cdots \geqslant \tau_n(B) $,

then

 $ |\sigma_i(A) - \tau_i(B)| \leqslant \|A - B\|_2, \quad i = 1, 2, \ldots, n, $

and

 $ \sum_{i=1}^{n} (\sigma_i(A) - \tau_i(B))^2 \leqslant \|A - B\|_F^2. $

The corollary shows that the singular values of any real matrix are also well conditioned, i.e., small perturbations on the entries of A cause only small changes in the singular values of A.

8.2 Symmetric QR method

The symmetric QR method is a QR iteration for solving symmetric eigenvalue problem. It applies the QR algorithm to a symmetric matrix A by using its symmetry. In order to construct an efficient QR method, we first reduce the symmetric matrix A to a tridiagonal matrix T and then apply the QR iteration to the matrix T.

124

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

8.2.1 Tridiagonal QR iteration

Let $A$ be symmetric and suppose that $A$ can be decomposed as

$$Q^TAQ = T,$$

where $Q$ is an orthogonal matrix and $T$ is an upper Hessenberg matrix. Then, $T$ should be symmetric tridiagonal. In this case we only need to handle with an eigenvalue problem of a symmetric tridiagonal matrix $T$.

Partition $A$ as follows,

$$A = \begin{bmatrix} \alpha_1 & \mathbf{v}_0^T \\ \mathbf{v}_0 & A_0 \end{bmatrix},$$

where $A_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-1)\times(n-1)}$. By using Householder transformations, we can reduce the matrix $A$ into a symmetric tridiagonal matrix as follows: at the $k$th step,

(1) Compute a Householder transformation $\tilde{H}_k \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-k)\times(n-k)}$ such that

$$\tilde{H}_k\mathbf{v}_{k-1} = \beta_k\mathbf{e}_1, \quad \beta_k \in \mathbb{R}.$$

(2) Compute

$$\begin{bmatrix} \alpha_{k+1} & \mathbf{v}_k^T \\ \mathbf{v}_k & A_k \end{bmatrix} = \tilde{H}_k A_{k-1} \tilde{H}_k,$$

where $A_k \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-k-1)\times(n-k-1)}$

If we use $\alpha_k$, $\beta_k$ and $\tilde{H}_k$ generated by the reduction above to define

$$T = \begin{bmatrix} \alpha_1 & \beta_1 & 0 \\ \beta_1 & \alpha_2 & \cdots \\ \cdots & \beta_{n-1} & \alpha_n \end{bmatrix},$$

$$H_k = \mathrm{diag}(I_k, \tilde{H}_k) \in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}, \quad Q = H_1H_2\cdots H_{n-2},$$

where

$$\begin{bmatrix} \alpha_{n-1} & \beta_{n-1} \\ \beta_{n-1} & \alpha_n \end{bmatrix} = \tilde{H}_{n-2}A_{n-3}\tilde{H}_{n-2},$$

then we have

$$Q^TAQ = T.$$

From the reduction above, it is easy to see that the main operation cost of the $k$th step is to compute $\tilde{H}_k A_{k-1}\tilde{H}_k$. Let

$$\tilde{H}_k = I - \beta\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T, \quad \mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{R}^{n-k}.$$

8.2 Symmetric QR method

By using the symmetry of  $ A_{k-1} $, we then have

 $ \tilde{H}_k A_{k-1} \tilde{H}_k = A_{k-1} - \mathbf{v} \mathbf{w}^T - \mathbf{w} \mathbf{v}^T $,

where

 $ \mathbf{w} = \mathbf{u} - \frac{1}{2} \beta (\mathbf{v}^T \mathbf{u}) \mathbf{v} $, \quad  $ \mathbf{u} = \beta A_{k-1} \mathbf{v} $.

Since only the upper triangular portion of this matrix needs to be computed, we see that the transition from  $ A_{k-1} $ to  $ A_k $ can be computed in  $ 4(n - k)^2 $ operations only. Given a symmetric matrix  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $, the following algorithm overwrites  $ A $ with  $ T = Q^T A Q $, where  $ T $ is a tridiagonal matrix and  $ Q $ is a product of Householder transformations.

Algorithm 8.1 (Householder tridiagonalization)

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

\text{for } k = 1: n - 2 \\

\text{[v, } \beta] = \text{house}(A(k + 1: n, k)) \\

\mathbf{u} = \beta A(k + 1: n, k + 1: n) \mathbf{v} \\

\mathbf{w} = \mathbf{u} - (\beta \mathbf{u}^T \mathbf{v} / 2) \mathbf{v} \\

\mathbf{A}(k + 1, k) = \| A(k + 1: n, k) \|_2 \\

\mathbf{A}(k, k + 1) = A(k + 1, k) \\

\mathbf{A}(k + 1: n, k + 1: n) = A(k + 1: n, k + 1: n) - \mathbf{v} \mathbf{w}^T - \mathbf{w} \mathbf{v}^T

\end{array}

\right. $

end

This algorithm requires  $ 4n^3 / 3 $ operations. If  $ Q $ is explicitly required, then it can be formed with additional  $ 4n^3 / 3 $ operations.

After a symmetric matrix  $ A $ has been reduced to a symmetric tridiagonal matrix  $ T $, our aim turns to choose a suitable shift for the QR iteration. Consider the following QR iteration with a single shift  $ \mu_k $:

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

T_k - \mu_k I = Q_k R_k, \\

T_{k+1} = R_k Q_k + \mu_k I,

\end{array}

\right. $

(8.1)

for  $ k = 1, 2, \ldots $, where  $ T_1 = T $ is a symmetric tridiagonal matrix and so is each matrix  $ T_k $ in (8.1). Just as the QR algorithm for nonsymmetric case,  $ T_k $ is assumed to be irreducible, i.e., sub-diagonal entries are nonzero. Let us discuss how to choose the shift  $ \mu_k $. We can take  $ \mu_k = T_k(n, n) $, the  $ (n, n) $th entry at each iteration as a shift. However, a better way is to select

 $ \mu_k = \alpha_n + \delta - \text{sign}(\delta) \sqrt{\delta^2 + \beta_{n-1}^2} $,

where  $ \delta = (\alpha_{n-1} - \alpha_n)/2 $. This is the well-known Wilkinson shift [95]. Note that  $ \mu_k $ is just the eigenvalue of the matrix

 $ T_k(n-1: n, n-1: n) = \begin{bmatrix} \alpha_{n-1} & \beta_{n-1} \\ \beta_{n-1} & \alpha_n \end{bmatrix} $,

126

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

which is closer to \alpha_n.

8.2.2 Implicit symmetric QR iteration

For the explicit QR algorithm (8.1), it is possible to execute the transition from

 $ T - \mu I = QR $

to

 $ \tilde{T} = RQ + \mu I $

without explicitly forming the matrix  $ T - \mu I $. The essence of (8.1) is to transform  $ T $ to  $ \tilde{T} $ by orthogonal similarity transformations. It follows from Theorem 7.5 that  $ \tilde{T} $ can be determined completely by the first column of Q. From the process of the QR decomposition of  $ T - \mu I $ by Givens rotations, we know that

 $ Q\mathbf{e}_1 = G_1\mathbf{e}_1 $,

where  $ G_1 = G(1, 2, \theta_1) $ is a rotation which makes the second entry in the first column of  $ T - \mu I $ to be zero. The  $ \theta_1 $ can be computed from

 $ \left[\begin{array}{cc} \cos\theta_1 & \sin\theta_1 \\ -\sin\theta_1 & \cos\theta_1 \end{array}\right] \left[\begin{array}{c}\alpha_1 - \mu \\ \beta_1 \end{array}\right] = \left[\begin{array}{c} * \\ 0 \end{array}\right] $.

Let

 $ B = G_1 T G_1^T $.

Then B has the following form  $ (n = 4) $,

 $ \left[\begin{array}{cc} * & * + 0 \\ * & * * 0 \\ + & * * * 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 * * \end{array}\right] $

Let  $ G_i = G(i, i + 1, \theta_i) $,  $ i = 2, 3 $. Then  $ G_i $ of this form can be used to chase the unwanted nonzero entry “+” out of the matrix B as follows:

 $ \left[\begin{array}{cc} * & * 0 \\ * & 0 \\ 0 & 0 * \end{array}\right] = G_2 \xrightarrow{G_2} \left[\begin{array}{cc} * & * 0 \\ * & 0 \\ 0 & 0 * \end{array}\right] $

In general, if  $ Z = G_1 G_2 \cdots G_{n-1} $, then

 $ Z\mathbf{e}_1 = G_1\mathbf{e}_1 = Q\mathbf{e}_1 $

and  $ Z T Z^T $ is tridiagonal. Thus, from Theorem 7.5, the tridiagonal matrix  $ Z T Z^T $ produced by this zero-chasing technique is essentially the same as the tridiagonal matrix T obtained by the explicit method (8.1). Overall, we obtain

8.2 Symmetric QR method 127

Algorithm 8.2 (Implicit symmetric QR iteration with Wilkinson shift)

 $ \begin{cases}

d = (T(n-1, n-1) - T(n, n))/2 \\

\mu = T(n, n) - T(n, n-1)^2 / (d + \text{sign}(d) \sqrt{d^2 + T(n, n-1)^2}) \\

x = T(1, 1) - \mu; \quad z = T(2, 1) \\

for \quad k = 1 : n - 1 \\

\quad [c, s] = \text{givens}(x, z) \\

\quad T = G_k T G_k^T, \quad \text{where} \quad G_k = G(k, k+1, \theta_k) \\

\quad if \quad k < n - 1 \\

\quad x = T(k+1, k); \quad z = T(k+2, k) \\

\quad end \\

\quad end \\

\end{cases} $

This algorithm requires about 30n operations and n square roots. Of course, the tridiagonal matrix  $ T $ would be stored in a pair of n-vectors in any practical implementation.

8.2.3 Implicit symmetric QR algorithm

Algorithm 8.2 is a base of the symmetric QR algorithm – the standard means for computing the spectral decomposition of any symmetric matrix. By applying Algorithm 8.2, we develop the following algorithm.

Algorithm 8.3 (Implicit symmetric QR algorithm)

(1) Input  $ A $ (real symmetric matrix).

(2) Tridiagonalization: Compute the tridiagonalization of  $ A $ by Algorithm 8.1,

 $$  T = U_0^T A U_0.  $$ 

Set  $ Q = U_0 $.

(3) Criterion for convergence:

(i) For  $ i = 1, \ldots, n-1 $, let  $ t_{i+1,i} $ and  $ t_{i,i+1} $ be zero if

 $$ 

|t_{i+1,i}| = |t_{i,i+1}| \leqslant (|t_{i,i}| + |t_{i+1,i+1}|) \mu_e,

 $$ 

where  $ \mu_e $ is the machine precision.

(ii) Find the largest integer  $ m \geqslant 0 $ and the smallest integer  $ l \geqslant 0 $ such that

 $$  T = \begin{bmatrix} T_{11} & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & T_{22} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & T_{33} \end{bmatrix},  $$ 

where  $ T_{11} \in \mathbb{R}^{l \times l} $,  $ T_{22} \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-l-m) \times (n-l-m)} $ is an irreducible tridiagonal matrix and  $ T_{33} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m} $ is a diagonal matrix.

128

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

(iii) If $m = n$, then output; otherwise

(4) QR iteration: Apply Algorithm 8.2 to $T_{22}$:

$T_{22} = GT_{22} G^{T}$, $G = G_{1} G_{2} \cdots G_{n-l-m-1}$.

(5) Set $Q = Q$diag($I_l, G, I_m$), then go to (3).

If we only need to compute the eigenvalues, this algorithm requires about $4n^{3}/3$ operations. If we need both the eigenvalues and eigenvectors, it requires about $9n^{3}$ operations. It can be shown [95] that the computed eigenvalues $\tilde{\lambda}_i$ ($i = 1, 2, \ldots, n$) obtained by Algorithm 8.3 satisfy

$Q^{T}(A + E)Q = \mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\lambda}_1, \ldots, \tilde{\lambda}_n)$,

where $Q \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is orthogonal and $\|E\|_2 \approx \|A\|_2 \mu_\epsilon$ where $\mu_\epsilon$ is the machine precision. Using Theorem 8.3, we have

$|\lambda_i - \tilde{\lambda}_i| \approx \|A\|_2 \mu_\epsilon$, $i = 1, 2, \ldots, n$,

where each $\lambda_i$ is an eigenvalue of $A$. The absolute error in each $\tilde{\lambda}_i$ is small and the relative error is less than the machine precision $\mu_\epsilon$. If $\tilde{Q} = [\tilde{q}_1 \mid \tilde{q}_2 \mid \ldots \mid \tilde{q}_n]$ is the matrix of computed orthonormal eigenvectors, then the accuracy of each $\tilde{q}_i$ depends on the separation of $\lambda_i$ from the rest of eigenvalues.

8.3 Jacobi method

The Jacobi method is one of the earliest methods for computing the symmetric eigenvalue problem. It was developed by Jacobi in 1846 (see [32]). It is well-known that a real symmetric matrix can be reduced to a diagonal matrix by orthogonal similarity transformations. The Jacobi method exploits the symmetry of the matrix and chooses suitable rotations to reduce a symmetric matrix to a diagonal form. The Jacobi method is usually much slower than the symmetric QR algorithm. However, the Jacobi method remains to be interesting because the method is capable of programming and inherently parallelism recognized in recent years.

8.3.1 Basic idea

Let $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a symmetric matrix and

off($A) \equiv \left( \| A \|_F^2 - \sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{ii}^2 \right)^{1/2} = \left( \sum_{i=1}^{n} \sum_{j=1}^{n} a_{ij}^2 \right)^{1/2}$.

8.3 Jacobi method 129

The idea of the Jacobi method is to systematically reduce the off(A) to be zero. The basic tools for doing this are called Jacobi rotations defined as follows,

 $ J(p, q, \theta) = I + \sin \theta (\mathbf{e}_p \mathbf{e}_q^T - \mathbf{e}_q \mathbf{e}_p^T) + (\cos \theta - 1) (\mathbf{e}_p \mathbf{e}_p^T + \mathbf{e}_q \mathbf{e}_q^T) $,

where  $ p < q $ and  $ \mathbf{e}_k $ denotes the  $ k $th unit vector. Note that Jacobi rotations are no different from Givens rotations (see Subsection 4.2.2). We change the name in this section to honour the inventor. The basic step in a Jacobi procedure involves:

(1) Choose  $ p $ and  $ q $ for a rotation with  $ 1 \leq p < q \leq n $.

(2) Compute a rotation angle  $ \theta $ such that

 $ \left[ \begin{array}{c} b_{pp} \quad b_{pq} \\ b_{qp} \quad b_{qq} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{cc} c & s \\ -s & c \end{array} \right]^T \left[ \begin{array}{cc} a_{pp} & a_{pq} \\ a_{qp} & a_{qq} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{cc} c & s \\ -s & c \end{array} \right] $ (8.2)

is diagonal, i.e.,  $ b_{pq} = b_{qp} = 0 $, where  $ c = \cos \theta $ and  $ s = \sin \theta $.

(3) Overwrite  $ A $ with  $ B = [b_{ij}] = J^T A J $, where  $ J = J(p, q, \theta) $.

Note that the matrix  $ B $ agrees with the matrix  $ A $ except the  $ p $th row (column) and the  $ q $th row (column). The relations are:

 $ b_{ip} = b_{pi} = c a_{ip} - s a_{iq} $,  $ i \neq p, q $,

 $ b_{iq} = b_{qi} = s a_{ip} + c a_{iq} $,  $ i \neq p, q $,

 $ b_{pp} = c^2 a_{pp} - 2 s c a_{pq} + s^2 a_{qq} $,

 $ b_{qq} = s^2 a_{pp} + 2 s c a_{pq} + c^2 a_{qq} $,

 $ b_{pq} = b_{qp} = (c^2 - s^2) a_{pq} + s c (a_{pp} - a_{qq}) $.

Let us first consider actual computations of  $ s = \sin \theta $ and  $ c = \cos \theta $ such that  $ b_{pq} = b_{qp} = 0 $ in (8.2). Actually it is equivalent to

 $ a_{pq}(c^2 - s^2) + (a_{pp} - a_{qq}) c s = 0 $. (8.3)

If  $ a_{pq} = 0 $, then we just set  $ c = 1 $ and  $ s = 0 $. Otherwise define

 $ \tau = \frac{a_{qq} - a_{pp}}{2a_{pq}}, \quad t = \tan \theta = \frac{s}{c} $,

and conclude from (8.3) that  $ t $ solves the quadratic equation

 $ t^2 + 2\tau t - 1 = 0 $.

130

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

Then,

 $$  t = -\tau \pm \sqrt{1 + \tau^2}.  $$ 

We select  $  t  $ to be the smaller of the two roots which ensures that  $  |\theta| \leq \pi/4  $ and has the effect of minimizing of  $  \|B - A \|_F^2  $ because

 $$  \|B - A \|_F^2 = 4(1 - c) \sum_{i=1}^{n} (a_{ip}^2 + a_{iq}^2) + \frac{2a_{pq}^2}{c^2}.  $$ 

After  $  t  $ is determined, we can obtain  $  c  $ and  $  s  $ from the formulas

 $$  c = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + t^2}}, \qquad s = tc.  $$ 

We summarize the computation of Jacobi rotations  $  J(p, q, \theta)  $ as follows. Given a symmetric matrix  $  A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $ and indices  $  p, q  $ with  $  1 \leq p < q \leq n  $, the following algorithm computes a cosine-sine pair such that  $  b_{pq} = b_{qp} = 0  $ where  $  b_{jk}  $ is the  $  (j, k)  $th entry of the matrix  $  B = J(p, q, \theta)^T A J(p, q, \theta)  $.

Algorithm 8.4

function:  $$  [c, s] = \text{sym}(A, p, q)  $$ 

if  $  A(p, q) \neq 0  $

 $  \tau = (A(q, q) - A(p, p))/(2A(p, q))  $

if  $  \tau \geq 0  $

 $  t = 1/(\tau + \sqrt{1 + \tau^2})  $

else

 $  t = -1/(-\tau + \sqrt{1 + \tau^2})  $

end

 $  c = 1/\sqrt{1 + t^2}  $

 $  s = tc  $

else

 $  c = 1  $

 $  s = 0  $

end

Once  $  J(p, q, \theta)  $ is determined, then the updated

 $$  A \leftarrow J(p, q, \theta)^T A J(p, q, \theta)  $$ 

can be computed in  $  6n  $ operations.

How can we choose the integers  $  p  $ and  $  q  $? Since the Frobenius norm is preserved by the orthogonal transformation (see Section 3.1), we have  $  \| B \|_F = \| A \|_F  $. Note that

 $$  a_{pp}^2 + a_{qq}^2 + 2a_{pq}^2 = b_{pp}^2 + b_{qq}^2 + 2b_{pq}^2 = b_{pp}^2 + b_{qq}^2,  $$ 

8.3 Jacobi method 131

and then

off(B)^2 = \|B\|_F^2 - \sum_{i=1}^{n} b_{ii}^2

= \|A\|_F^2 - \sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{ii}^2 + (a_{pp}^2 + a_{qq}^2 - b_{pp}^2 - b_{qq}^2)

= off(A)^2 - 2a_{pq}^2.

(8.4)

Our goal is to minimize off(B), the best choice of p, q should be

|a_{pq}| = \max_{1 \le i < j \le n} |a_{ij}|.

This is the basic idea of the classical Jacobi method.

8.3.2 Classical Jacobi method

Given a symmetric matrix  $ A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ and a tolerance  $ \epsilon > 0 $, the following algorithm overwrites  $ A $ with  $ U^T A U $, where  $ U $ is orthogonal and off( $ U^T A U $)  $ \leqslant \epsilon \|A\|_F $.

Algorithm 8.5 (Classical Jacobi method)

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

U = I_n; \quad \text{eps} = \epsilon \|A\|_F \\

\text{while} \quad \text{off}(A) > \text{eps} \\

\text{choose} \quad p, q \quad \text{such that} \quad |a_{pq}| = \max_{i \neq j} |a_{ij}| \\

[c, s] = \text{sym}(A, p, q) \\

A = J(p, q, \theta)^T A J(p, q, \theta) \\

U = U J(p, q, \theta)

\end{array}

\right. $

end

Since  $ |a_{pq}| $ is the largest off-diagonal entry, we have

off(A)^2 \leqslant N(a_{pq}^2 + a_{qp}^2),

where  $ N = n(n - 1)/2 $. It follows from (8.4) that

off(B)^2 \leqslant \left(1 - \frac{1}{N}\right) \text{off}(A)^2.

If  $ A_k $ denotes the matrix  $ A $ after the kth Jacobi iteration and  $ A_0 = A $, we have by induction,

off( $ A_k $)^2 \leqslant \left(1 - \frac{1}{N}\right)^k \text{off}(A_0)^2.

132

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

This implies that the classical Jacobi method converges linearly. However, actually, the asymptotic convergence rate of the Jacobi method is quadratic. We can prove that for $k$ large enough, there is a constant $c$ such that

$$

\text{off}(A_{k+N}) \leqslant c \cdot \text{off}(A_k)^2.

$$

See [32] and references therein. Therefore, the off-diagonal “norm” approaches to zero at a quadratic rate after a sufficient number of iterations.

Another advantage of the Jacobi method is easy to compute the eigenvectors. If the iteration stops after $k$th rotation, we then have

$$A_k = J_k^T J_{k-1}^T \cdots J_1^T A J_1 J_2 \cdots J_k.$$

Denote

$$Q_k = J_1 J_2 \cdots J_k.$$

Thus

$$AQ_k = Q_k A_k.$$

Since off-diagonal entries of $A_k$ are tiny and then diagonal entries of $A_k$ are good approximations to the eigenvalues of $A$, the identity above shows that the columns of $Q_k$ are good approximations to the eigenvectors of $A$ and all the approximate eigenvectors are orthonormal. We can obtain $Q_k$, the approximate eigenvectors, during the Jacobi iterative process.

8.3.3 Parallel Jacobi method

The Jacobi method to the symmetric eigenvalue problem is inherently parallelism. To illustrate this, let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{8 \times 8}$ be symmetric. If one has a parallel computer with 4 processors, then one can group the 28 subproblems into 7 groups of rotations as follows:

group (1): (1,2), (3,4), (5,6), (7,8);

group (2): (1,3), (2,4), (5,7), (6,8);

group (3): (1,4), (2,3), (5,8), (6,7);

group (4): (1,5), (2,6), (3,7), (4,8);

group (5): (1,6), (2,5), (3,8), (4,7);

group (6): (1,7), (2,8), (3,5), (4,6);

group (7): (1,8), (2,7), (3,6), (4,5).

8.4 Bisection method 133

Note that all 4 rotations within each group are nonconflicting. For instance, the subproblems  $ J(2i - 1, 2i, \theta_i) $ ( $ i = 1, 2, 3, 4 $) in the first group can be carried out in parallel. When we compute  $ J(1, 2, \theta_1)^T A J(1, 2, \theta_1) $, it has no effect on the rotations (3, 4), (5, 6) and (7, 8). Then the computation of

 $$  A = A J(1, 2, \theta_1), \quad A = A J(3, 4, \theta_2),  $$ 

 $$  A = A J(5, 6, \theta_3), \quad A = A J(7, 8, \theta_4)  $$ 

can be executed in parallel by 4 processors. Similarly, the computation of

 $$  A = J(1, 2, \theta_1)^T A, \quad A = J(3, 4, \theta_2)^T A,  $$ 

 $$  A = J(5, 6, \theta_3)^T A, \quad A = J(7, 8, \theta_4)^T A  $$ 

can also be carried out in parallel by 4 processors. For the example above, it only needs 1/4 computing time of a computer with a single processor. A parallel Jacobi algorithm can be found in [32].

8.4 Bisection method

In this section we present the bisection method for symmetric tridiagonal eigenvalue problems. Combining the bisection method with the tridiagonal skill, we can obtain a numerical method for a specified eigenvalue and its corresponding eigenvector of a symmetric matrix. For a given tridiagonal matrix

 $$ 

\begin{bmatrix}

a_1 & b_1 & 0 \\

b_1 & a_2 & \cdots \\

b_{n-1} & b_{n-1}

\end{bmatrix}

\in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n},

\quad (8.5)

 $$ 

we consider the computation of eigenvalues of T. Without loss of generality, we assume that  $ b_i \neq 0 $ ( $ i = 1, 2, \ldots, n-1 $), i.e., T is an irreducible symmetric tridiagonal matrix. Otherwise, T can be divided into several smaller irreducible symmetric tridiagonal matrices.

Let  $ p_i(\lambda) $ be the characteristic polynomial of the i-by-i leading principal submax_trix  $ T_i $ of T for each i. Then these polynomials satisfy a three-term recurrence:

 $$  p_0(\lambda) \equiv 1, \quad p_1(\lambda) = a_1 - \lambda,  $$ 

 $$  p_i(\lambda) = (a_i - \lambda) p_{i-1}(\lambda) - b_{i-1}^2 p_{i-2}(\lambda), \quad i = 2, 3, \ldots, n.  $$ 

Since T is symmetric, the roots of polynomial  $ p_i(\lambda) $ ( $ i = 1, 2, \ldots, n $) are real. The following interlacing property is very important.

134 Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

Theorem 8.7 (Sturm Sequence Property) Let the symmetric tridiagonal matrix $T$ be irreducible. Then the eigenvalues of $T_{i-1}$ strictly separate the eigenvalues of $T_i$.

$$\lambda_i(T_i) < \lambda_{i-1}(T_{i-1}) < \lambda_{i-1}(T_i) < \cdots < \lambda_2(T_i) < \lambda_1(T_{i-1}) < \lambda_1(T_i).$$

Moreover, if $s_n(\lambda)$ denotes the number of sign changes in the sequence $\{p_0(\lambda), p_1(\lambda), \ldots, p_n(\lambda)\}$, then $s_n(\lambda)$ is equal to the number of eigenvalues of $T$ that are less than $\lambda$, where $p_i(\lambda)$ are defined by (8.6). If $p_i(\lambda) = 0$, then $p_{i-1}(\lambda)p_{i+1}(\lambda) < 0$.

Proof It follows from Theorem 8.4 that the eigenvalues of $T_{i-1}$ weakly separate those of $T_i$. Next we show that the separation must be strict. Assume that $p_i(\mu) = p_{i-1}(\mu) = 0$ for some $i$ and $\mu$. Since $T$ is irreducible and, we note that by (8.6), $p_0(\mu) = p_1(\mu) = \cdots = p_i(\mu) = 0$, which is a contradiction with $p_0(\lambda) \equiv 1$. Thus we have a strict separation. The assertion about $s_n(\lambda)$ is developed in [95]. The bisection method for computing a specified eigenvalue of $T$ can be stated as follows:

Algorithm 8.6 (Bisection algorithm) Let $\lambda_1 < \lambda_2 < \cdots < \lambda_n$ be the eigenvalues of $T$, $i.e.,$ the roots of $p_n(\lambda)$, and $\epsilon$ be a tolerance. Suppose that the desired eigenvalue is $\lambda_m$ for a given $m \leq n$. Then (1) Find an interval $[l_0, u_0]$ including $\lambda_m$. Since $|\lambda_i| \leq \rho(T) \leq \|T\|_\infty$, we can take $l_0 = -\|T\|_\infty$ and $u_0 = \|T\|_\infty$.

(2) Compute $r_1 = \frac{l_0 + u_0}{2}$ and $s_n(r_1)$.

(3) If $s_n(r_1) \geq m$, then $\lambda_m \in [l_0, r_1]$, set $l_1 = l_0$ and $u_1 = r_1$; otherwise $\lambda_m \in [r_1, u_0]$, set $l_1 = r_1$ and $u_1 = u_0$.

(4) If $|l_1 - u_1| < \epsilon$, take $r_2 = \frac{l_1 + u_1}{2}$ as an approximate value of $\lambda_m$. Otherwise go to (2).

From the algorithm above, we can see that the main operation cost is to compute $s_n(\mu)$. However in practice, $s_n(\mu)$ cannot be obtained through computing the value of $p_i(\mu)$ because it is difficult to evaluate polynomials of high order. In order to avoid such a problem, we define $q_i(\lambda) = \frac{p_i(\lambda)}{p_{i-1}(\lambda)}$, $i = 1,2,\ldots,n$.

8.5 Divide-and-conquer method 135

From (8.6), we have

 $ q_1(\lambda) = p_1(\lambda) = a_1 - \lambda, \qquad q_i(\lambda) = a_i - \lambda - \frac{b_{i-1}^2}{q_{i-1}(\lambda)}, \quad i = 2, 3, \ldots, n. $

It is easy to check that  $ s_n(\mu) $ is exactly the number of negative values in the sequence of  $ q_1(\mu), \ldots, q_n(\mu) $. The following is a practical algorithm for computing  $ s_n(\mu) $.

Algorithm 8.7 (Compute sign changes)

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

x = [a_1, a_2, \cdots, a_n] \\

y = [0, b_1, \cdots, b_{n-1}] \\

s = 0; q = x(1) - \mu \\

for k = 1 : n \quad if q < 0 \quad s = s + 1 \quad end \\

if k < n \quad if q = 0 \quad q = |y(k + 1)| \mu_e \quad end \\

q = x(k + 1) - \mu - y(k + 1)^2 / q \\

end

\end{array}

\right. $

where  $ \mu_\epsilon $ is the machine precision.

When  $ q_i(\mu) = 0 $, we treat  $ q_i $ as a positive number. Therefore, we can use a small positive number  $ |b_i|_{\mu_\epsilon} $ instead of  $ q_i(\mu) $ in Algorithm 8.7. If we store  $ b_i^2 $ in advance, then Algorithm 8.7 needs 3n operations. If an eigenvalue is computed by using the bisection method m times on average, then the operation cost is 3nm. Thus, it is efficient to compute the eigenvalues of any symmetric tridiagonal matrix by the bisection method. On the other hand, the rounding error analysis shows that the bisection method is numerically stable [95].

8.5 Divide-and-conquer method

A divide-and-conquer method is a numerical method developed by Dongarra and Sorensen in 1987 for computing all the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of symmetric tridiagonal matrices [32]. The basic idea is to tear the original symmetric tridiagonal matrix into  $ 2^k $ symmetric tridiagonal matrices with smaller sizes and then compute the spectral decomposition of each smaller symmetric tridiagonal matrix. Once we obtained these smaller spectral decompositions, we then combine them together to form a spectral decomposition of the original matrix. Thus, this method is suitable for parallel computing.

136

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

8.5.1 Tearing

Let  $  T \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}  $ be given as follows,

Without loss of generality, assume  $  n = 2m  $. Let

Consider the matrix

where  $  \theta, \rho \in \mathbb{R}  $ are needed to be determined. It is easy to see that  $  \tilde{T}  $ is identical to  $  T  $ except its 4 middle entries:

If we set  $  \rho\theta = b_m  $, then

where

 $  T_1 = \begin{bmatrix} a_1 & b_1 \\ b_1 & a_2 \\ b_1 & a_2 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} + \rho\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T  $

 $  b_m - b_1 = \begin{bmatrix} T_1 & 0 \\ T_2 & 0 \\ T_2 \end{bmatrix} + \rho\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^T  $

 $  b_m - 2a_m - 1a_m - 1  $

and

 $  T_2 = \begin{bmatrix} \tilde{a}_{m+1} & b_{m+1} \\ b_{m+1} & a_{m+2} & b_{m+2} \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} + \tilde{b}_{m-1}  $

 $  b_{n-2} \quad a_{n-1} \quad b_{n-1} \quad a_n  $

with

Therefore,  $  T  $ is divided into a sum of a partitioned matrix and a rank-one matrix. If we divide  $  T_1  $ and  $  T_2  $ repeatedly, then finally we can divide  $  T  $ into  $  2^k  $ blocks.

8.5 Divide-and-conquer method 137

8.5.2 Combining

Once we obtained the spectral decompositions of T_1 and T_2:

 $$ 

Q_1^T T_1 Q_1 = D_1, \qquad Q_2^T T_2 Q_2 = D_2,

 $$ 

where Q_1, Q_2 \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m} are orthogonal matrices, and D_1, D_2 \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m} are diagonal matrices, our aim now is to compute the spectral decomposition of T from that of T_1 and T_2, i.e., to find an orthogonal matrix V such that

 $$ 

V^T TV = \text{diag}(\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n).

 $$ 

Let

 $$ 

U = \begin{bmatrix} Q_1 & 0 \\ 0 & Q_2 \end{bmatrix},

 $$ 

then

 $$ 

U^T T U = \begin{bmatrix} Q_1 & 0 \\ 0 & Q_2 \end{bmatrix}^T \left( \begin{bmatrix} T_1 & 0 \\ 0 & T_2 \end{bmatrix} + \rho \mathbf{v} \mathbf{v}^T \right) \begin{bmatrix} Q_1 & 0 \\ 0 & Q_2 \end{bmatrix}

 $$ 

 $$  = D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T,

 $$ 

where

 $$ 

D = \text{diag}(D_1, D_2), \quad \mathbf{z} = U^T \mathbf{v}.

 $$ 

Now the problem of finding the spectral decomposition of T is reduced to the problem of computing the spectral decomposition of D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T. We consider how to compute the spectral decomposition of D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T quickly and stably.

Lemma 8.1 Let D = \text{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} with d_1 > d_2 > \cdots > d_n. Assume that 0 \neq \rho \in \mathbb{R} and \mathbf{z} = [z_1, z_2, \ldots, z_n]^T \in \mathbb{R}^n with z_i \neq 0 for all i. Let \mathbf{u} \in \mathbb{R}^n and \lambda \in \mathbb{R} satisfy

 $$ 

(D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T) \mathbf{u} = \lambda \mathbf{u}, \quad \mathbf{u} \neq 0.

 $$ 

Then \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{u} \neq 0 and D - \lambda I is nonsingular.

Proof If \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{u} = 0, then D \mathbf{u} = \lambda \mathbf{u} with \mathbf{u} \neq 0, i.e., \lambda is the eigenvalue of D and \mathbf{u} is the eigenvector associated with \lambda. Since D is a diagonal matrix with distinct entries, there must exist some i such that d_i = \lambda and \mathbf{u} = \alpha \mathbf{e}_i with \alpha \neq 0, where \mathbf{e}_i is the ith unit vector. Thus

 $$ 

0 = \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{u} = \alpha \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{e}_i = \alpha \mathbf{z}_i,

 $$ 

which implies z_i = 0, a contradiction. Therefore, \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{u} \neq 0.

138

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

On the other hand, if  $ D - \lambda I $ is singular, then there exists some  $ i $ such that  $ \mathbf{e}_i^T(D - \lambda I) = 0 $, and then

 $ 0 = \mathbf{e}_i^T(D - \lambda I) \mathbf{u} = -\rho \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{u} \mathbf{e}_i^T \mathbf{z} $.

Since  $ \rho \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{u} \neq 0 $, we have  $ \mathbf{e}_i^T \mathbf{z} = z_i = 0 $, a contradiction. Thus,  $ D - \lambda I $ is nonsingular.

□

Theorem 8.8 Let  $ D = \text{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ with  $ d_1 > d_2 > \cdots > d_n $. Assume that  $ 0 \neq \rho \in \mathbb{R} $ and  $ \mathbf{z} = [z_1, z_2, \ldots, z_n]^T \in \mathbb{R}^n $ with  $ z_i \neq 0 $ for all  $ i $. Suppose that the spectral decomposition of  $ D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T $ is

 $ V^T(D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T) V = \text{diag}(\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n) $,

where  $ V = [\mathbf{v}_1 \mid \mathbf{v}_2 | \ldots \mid \mathbf{v}_n] $ is an orthogonal matrix and  $ \lambda_1 \geq \cdots \geq \lambda_n $. Then

(i)  $ \lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n $ are  $ n $ roots of the function

 $ f(\lambda) = 1 + \rho \mathbf{z}^T (D - \lambda I)^{-1} \mathbf{z} $.

(ii) If  $ \rho > 0 $, then

 $ \lambda_1 > d_1 > \lambda_2 > \cdots > \lambda_n > d_n $;

if  $ \rho < 0 $, then

 $ d_1 > \lambda_1 > d_2 > \cdots > d_n > \lambda_n $.

(iii) There exists a constant  $ \alpha_i \neq 0 $ such that

 $ \mathbf{v}_i = \alpha_i (D - \lambda_i I)^{-1} \mathbf{z} $, \quad i = 1, 2, \ldots, n $.

Proof From the assumption, we have

 $ (D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T) \mathbf{v}_i = \lambda_i \mathbf{v}_i $, \quad \|\mathbf{v}_i\|_2 = 1 $.

It follows from Lemma 8.1 that  $ D - \lambda_i I $ is nonsingular. Thus,

 $ \mathbf{v}_i = -\rho \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{v}_i (D - \lambda_i I)^{-1} \mathbf{z} $, \quad i = 1, 2, \ldots, n, \quad (8.7) $

thereby establishing (iii). Note that  $ D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T $ has distinct eigenvalues. Other-wise, if  $ \lambda_i = \lambda_j $, then  $ \mathbf{v}_i $ and  $ \mathbf{v}_j $ are linearly dependent, which contradicts with the orthogonality between  $ \mathbf{v}_i $ and  $ \mathbf{v}_j $.

By multiplying  $ \mathbf{z}^T $ to the both sides of (8.7) and noting that  $ \mathbf{z}^T \mathbf{v}_i \neq 0 $, we have

 $ 1 = -\rho \mathbf{z}^T (D - \lambda_i I)^{-1} \mathbf{z} $,

8.5 Divide-and-conquer method 139

i.e.,

 $ f(\lambda_i) = 0, \qquad i = 1, 2, \ldots, n. $

Thus,  $ \lambda_i $ ( $ i = 1, 2, \ldots, n $) are the roots of  $ f(\lambda) $. Next we prove that  $ f(\lambda) $ has exactly n zeros. Note that

 $ f(\lambda) = 1 + \rho\left(\frac{z_1^2}{d_1 - \lambda} + \cdots + \frac{z_n^2}{d_n - \lambda}\right), $

and moreover,

 $ f'(\lambda) = \rho\left(\frac{z_1^2}{(d_1 - \lambda)^2} + \cdots + \frac{z_n^2}{(d_n - \lambda)^2}\right). $

Thus,  $ f(\lambda) $ is strictly monotone between the poles  $ d_i $ and  $ d_{i+1} $. If  $ \rho > 0 $,  $ f(\lambda) $ is strictly increasing; if  $ \rho < 0 $,  $ f(\lambda) $ is strictly decreasing. Therefore, it is easy to see that  $ f(\lambda) $ has exactly n roots, one of each in the intervals

 $ (d_n, d_{n-1}), \ldots, (d_2, d_1), (d_1, \infty) $,

if  $ \rho > 0 $; and one of each in the intervals

 $ (-\infty, d_n), (d_n, d_{n-1}), \ldots, (d_2, d_1) $,

if  $ \rho < 0 $. Thus, (i) and (ii) are established. □

By Theorem 8.8, we can compute the spectral decomposition of  $ D + \rho \mathbf{zz}^T $ efficiently in the following two steps:

(1) Find roots  $ \lambda_1, \lambda_2, \ldots, \lambda_n $ of  $ f(\lambda) $. There is a unique root of  $ f(\lambda) $ in each of the intervals  $ (d_{i+1}, d_i) $ and  $ f(\lambda) $ is strictly monotone in the interval. Thus, this step can be implemented quickly and stably by using a Newton-like method [30].

(2) Compute

 $ \mathbf{v}_i = \frac{(D - \lambda_i I)^{-1} \mathbf{z}}{\|(D - \lambda_i I)^{-1} \mathbf{z}\|_2}, \qquad i = 1, 2, \ldots, n. $

The spectral decomposition of a general  $ D + \rho \mathbf{zz}^T $ can be turned into the case as in Theorem 8.8. To this end, we can prove the following theorem constructively.

Theorem 8.9 Let  $ D = \text{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ and  $ \mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^n $. Then there exists an orthogonal matrix  $ V $ and a permutation  $ \pi $ of  $ \{1, 2, \ldots, n\} $ such that

(i)  $ V^T \mathbf{z} = [\eta_1, \ldots, \eta_r, 0, \ldots, 0]^T $ where  $ \eta_i \neq 0 $ for  $ i = 1, 2, \ldots, r $.

(ii)  $ V^T DV = \text{diag}(d_{\pi(1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi(n)}) $ where  $ d_{\pi(1)} > d_{\pi(2)} > \cdots > d_{\pi(r)} $.

140 Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

Proof Suppose that two indices  $ i < j $ satisfy  $ d_i = d_j $. Then we can set a rotation  $ P_{ij} = G(i, j, \theta) $ such that the  $ j $th component of  $ P_{ij}z $ is zero. It is easy to show that  $ P_{ij}^TDP_{ij} = D $. After several steps, we can find an orthogonal matrix  $ V_1 $ which is a product of some rotations such that

 $ V_1^TDV_1 = D $,  $ V_1^Tz = [\xi_1, \ldots, \xi_n]^T $

with the property that if  $ \xi_i\xi_j \neq 0 $ ( $ i \neq j $), then  $ d_i \neq d_j $.

If  $ \xi_i = 0 $,  $ \xi_j \neq 0 $ for  $ i < j $, then we can find a permutation matrix to interchange the columns  $ i $ and  $ j $. In such a way, we can find a permutation matrix  $ P_1 $ which permutes all the nonzero  $ \xi_j $ to the front, i.e.,

 $ P_1^TDV_1^Tz = [\xi_{\pi_1(1)}, \ldots, \xi_{\pi_1(n)}]^T $

with

 $ \xi_{\pi_1(i)} \neq 0 $,  $ i = 1, 2, \ldots, r $,

and

 $ \xi_{\pi_1(i)} = 0 $,  $ i = r + 1, \ldots, n $.

Here  $ \pi_1 $ is a permutation of  $ \{1, 2, \ldots, n\} $. It follows from the construction of  $ P_1 $ that

 $ P_1^TDV_1^TDV_1P_1 = P_1^TDP_1 = \text{diag}(d_{\pi_1(1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi_1(n)}) $,

where the first  $ r $ diagonal entries  $ d_{\pi_1(1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi_1(r)} $ are distinct.

Finally, we can find another permutation matrix  $ P_2 $ of order  $ r $ such that

 $ P_2^TDiag(d_{\pi_1(1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi_1(r)})P_2 = \text{diag}(\mu_1, \ldots, \mu_r) $,

where  $ \mu_1 > \mu_2 > \cdots > \mu_r $. Let

 $ V = V_1P_1\text{diag}(P_2, I_{n-r}) $

and  $ \pi $ be a permutation of  $ \{1, 2, \ldots, n\} $ determined by  $ P_1 $ and  $ P_2 $. Then,

 $ V^Tz = [\xi_{\pi(1)}, \ldots, \xi_{\pi(r)}, 0, \ldots, 0]^T = [\eta_1, \ldots, \eta_r, 0, \ldots, 0]^T $,

where  $ \eta_i \neq 0 $ for  $ i = 1, 2, \ldots, r $, and

 $ V^TDV = \text{diag}(d_{\pi(1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi(n)}) $

with

 $ d_{\pi(1)} > d_{\pi(2)} > \cdots > d_{\pi(r)} $.

The proof is complete.

8.5 Divide-and-conquer method

141

For any  $ D = \text{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ and  $ \mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^n $, by Theorem 8.9, we can construct an orthogonal matrix  $ V $ such that

 $$ 

V^T(D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T) V = \begin{bmatrix} D_1 + \rho \mathbf{w} \mathbf{w}^T & 0 \\ 0 & D_2 \end{bmatrix},

 $$ 

where

 $ D_1 = \text{diag}(d_{\pi(1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi(r)}) \in \mathbb{R}^{r \times r}, \quad d_{\pi(1)} > \cdots > d_{\pi(r)}; $

 $ D_2 = \text{diag}(d_{\pi(r+1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi(n)}) \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-r) \times (n-r)}; $

and

 $ \mathbf{w} = [\eta_1, \ldots, \eta_r]^T, \quad \eta_i \neq 0, \quad i = 1, 2, \ldots, r. $

Then we only need to compute the spectral decomposition of  $ D_1 + \rho \mathbf{w} \mathbf{w}^T $ instead of  $ D + \rho \mathbf{z} \mathbf{z}^T $.

Finally, we briefly introduce the parallel computation of the divide-and-conquer method. For simplicity, we use a parallel computer with 4 processors to compute the spectral decomposition of a 4N-by-4N symmetric tridiagonal matrix  $ T $. It can be summarized by the following 4 steps:

(1) Tear

 $$ 

T = \begin{bmatrix} T_1 & 0 \\ 0 & T_2 \end{bmatrix} + \rho \mathbf{v} \mathbf{v}^T, \quad T_1 \in \mathbb{R}^{2N \times 2N}, \quad \mathbf{v} \in \mathbb{R}^{4N};

 $$ 

and

 $$ 

T_i = \begin{bmatrix} T_{i1} & 0 \\ 0 & T_{i2} \end{bmatrix} + \rho_i \mathbf{w}_i \mathbf{w}_i^T,

 $$ 

where  $ T_{ij} \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times N} $ and  $ \mathbf{w}_i \in \mathbb{R}^{2N} $ for  $ i = 1, 2 $.

(2) Compute the spectral decompositions of  $ T_{11}, T_{12}, T_{21} $ and  $ T_{22} $ by 4 processors in parallel.

(3) Combine the spectral decompositions of  $ T_{11}, T_{12} $ to form a spectral decomposition of  $ T_{21}, T_{22} $ to form a situation of  $ T_1 $, and combine the spectral decompositions of  $ T_{21}, T_{22} $ to form a spectral decomposition of  $ T_2 $. These can be implemented by 4 processors at the same time.

(4) Combine the spectral decompositions of  $ T_1 $ and  $ T_2 $ to form a spectral decomposition of  $ T $. This can be derived by 4 processors in parallel.

From discussions above, we know that the divide-and-conquer method can be used for computing all the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of any large symmetric tridiagonal matrix in parallel.

142

Chapter 8 Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

Exercises:

1. Compute the Schur decomposition of

 $ A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 3 \end{bmatrix}. $

2. Show that if  $ X \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times r} $ with  $ r \leqslant n $, and  $ \|X^T X - I\|_2 = \tau < 1 $, then

 $ \sigma_{\min}(X) \geqslant 1 - \tau, $

where  $ \sigma_{\min} $ denotes the smallest singular value.

3. Show that  $ A = B + iC $ is Hermitian if and only if

 $ M = \begin{bmatrix} B & -C \\ C & B \end{bmatrix} $

is symmetric. Relate the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of  $ A $ to those of  $ M $.

4. Relate the singular values and the singular vectors of  $ A = B + iC $ to those of

 $ \begin{bmatrix} B & -C \\ C & B \end{bmatrix}, $

where  $ B, C \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} $.

there exist a matrix  $ Q \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} $ with  $ Q^T Q = I $ and a positive semi-definite matrix

 $ P \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $ such that  $ A = QP $.

with  $ \|B\|_2 < 1 $. Show that

7. Let

 $ A = \begin{bmatrix} a_1 & b_1 \\ c_1 & a_2 & b_2 \\ \ddots & \ddots & \ddots \\ \ddots & \ddots & b_n-1 \end{bmatrix}. $

where  $ b_i c_i > 0 $. Then there exists a diagonal  $ D $ such that  $ D^{-1} A D $ is a symmetric tridiagonal matrix.

8. Let

 $ T = \begin{bmatrix} 2 & -1 & 0 & 0 \\ -1 & 2 & -1 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 & 2 & -1 \\ 0 & 0 & -1 & 2 \end{bmatrix}. $

8.5 Divide-and-conquer method 143

(1) Is $T$ positive definite?

(2) How many eigenvalues of $T$ lie in the interval $[0,2]$?

9. Let $A, E \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be two symmetric matrices. Show that if $A$ is positive definite and

$$\|A^{-1}\|_{2} \|E\|_{2} < 1\), then $A + E$ is also positive definite.

10. Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ with $m \geq n$, and assume that the singular values of $A$ are ordered as

$$\sigma_1 \geq \sigma_2 \geq \cdots \geq \sigma_n.$$

Show that

$$\sigma_i = \max_{\dim(S)=i} \min_{0 \neq u \in S} \frac{\|Au\|_2}{\|u\|_2} = \min_{\dim(S)=n-i+1} \max_{0 \neq u \in S} \frac{\|Au\|_2}{\|u\|_2},$$

where $S$ is any subspace of $\mathbb{R}^n$.

Prove that the eigenvalues of $A$ are nonnegative.
