Chapter 9

Applications in ODEs and DDEs

In this chapter, we briefly study some recent developments in using boundary value methods (BVMs) for solving initial value problems of systems of ordinary differential equations (ODEs). These methods require the solution of one or more nonsymmetric, large and sparse linear systems. Therefore, we use the GMRES method studied in Chapter 6 with some preconditioners for solving these linear systems. One of the main results is that if an  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable BVM is used for an  $ n $-by- $ n $ system of ODEs, then the preconditioned matrix can be decomposed as  $ I + L $ where  $ I $ is the identity matrix and the rank of  $ L $ is at most  $ 2n(\nu_1 + \nu_2) $. When the GMRES method is applied to the preconditioned systems, the method converges in at most  $ 2n(\nu_1 + \nu_2) + 1 $ iterations. Applications to different kinds of delay differential equations (DDEs) are also given. For a literature on BVMs for ODEs and DDEs, we refer to [5, 8, 10, 17, 19, 44, 45, 47, 48, 55, 58].

9.1 Introduction

Let us begin with the initial value problem:

 $ \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf{y}'(t) = J_n \mathbf{y}(t) + \mathbf{g}(t), \quad t \in (t_0, T], \\ \mathbf{y}(t_0) = \mathbf{z}, \end{array} \right. $

where  $ \mathbf{y}(t) $,  $ \mathbf{g}(t) $ :  $ \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}^n $,  $ \mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^n $, and  $ J_n \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $. The initial value methods (IVMs), such as the Runge-Kutta methods, are well-known methods for solving (9.1) [83]. Recently, another class of methods called the boundary value methods (BVMs) has been proposed in [10]. Using BVMs to discretize (9.1), we obtain a linear system

 $ \mathbf{M} \mathbf{u} = \mathbf{b} $.

The advantage of using BVMs is that the methods are more stable and the resulting linear system  $ \mathbf{M} \mathbf{u} = \mathbf{b} $ is hence more well-conditioned. However, this system is in general large and sparse (with band-structure), and solving it is a major

9.2 Background of BVMs

145

problem in the application of BVMs. The GMRES method studied in Chapter 6 is used for solving  $ Mu = b $. In order to speed up the convergence of the GMRES iterations, a preconditioner  $ S $ called the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner [19] is used to precondition the discrete system. The advantage of the Strang-type preconditioner is that if an  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable BVM is used for solving (9.1), then  $ S $ is invertible and the preconditioned matrix can be decomposed as

 $ S^{-1}M = I + L $,

where the rank of  $ L $ is at most  $ 2n(\nu_1 + \nu_2) $ which is independent of the integration step size. It follows that the GMRES method applied to the preconditioned system converges in at most  $ 2n(\nu_1 + \nu_2) + 1 $ iterations in exact arithmetic.

The outline of this chapter is as follows. In Section 9.2, we give some background knowledge about the linear multistep formulas (LMFs) and BVMs. Then, we investigate the properties of the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner for ODEs in Section 9.3. The convergence and cost analysis of the method is also given with a numerical example. Finally, we discuss the applications of the Strang-type preconditioner with BVMs for solving different kinds of delay differential equations (DDEs) in Sections 9.4–9.6.

9.2 Background of BVMs

We give some background knowledge on LMFs and BVMs in this section.

9.2.1 Linear multistep formulas

Consider an initial value problem

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

y' = f(t, y), \quad t \in (t_0, T], \\

y(t_0) = y_0,

\end{array}

\right. $

where  $ y(t) : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R} $ and  $ f(t, y) : \mathbb{R}^2 \to \mathbb{R} $. The  $ \mu $-step linear multistep formula (LMF) over a uniform mesh with step size  $ h $ is defined as follows:

 $ \sum_{j=0}^{\mu} \alpha_j y_{m+j} = h \sum_{j=0}^{\mu} \beta_j f_{m+j}, \quad m = 0, 1, \ldots, (9.2) $

where  $ y_m $ is the discrete approximation to  $ y(t_m) $ and  $ f_m $ denotes  $ f(t_m, y_m) $.

To get the solution of (9.2), we need  $ \mu $ initial conditions

 $ y_0, y_1, \ldots, y_{\mu-1} $.

146

Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

Since only  $ y_0 $ is provided from the original problem, we have to find additional conditions for the remaining values

 $ y_1, y_2, \ldots, y_{\mu-1} $.

The equation (9.2) with  $ \mu - 1 $ additional conditions is called initial value methods (IVMs). An IVM is called implicit if  $ \beta_\mu \neq 0 $ and explicit if  $ \beta_\mu = 0 $. If an IVM is applied to an initial value problem on the interval  $ [t_0, t_{N+\mu-1}] $, we have the following discrete problem

 $ A_N y = h B_N f - \begin{bmatrix} \sum_{i=0}^{\mu-1} (\alpha_i y_i - h \beta_i f_i) \\ \alpha_0 y_{\mu-1} - h \beta_0 f_{\mu-1} \\ 0 \\ \vdots \\ 0 \end{bmatrix} $, (9.3)

where

 $ y = [y_\mu, y_{\mu+1}, \ldots, y_{N+\mu-1}]^T $,  $ f = [f_\mu, f_{\mu+1}, \ldots, f_{N+\mu-1}]^T $,

 $ a_\mu \begin{bmatrix} \alpha_0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} $,  $ B_\mu \begin{bmatrix} \beta_0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} $,  $ A_N = \alpha_0 \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} $,  $ B_N = \beta_0 \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix} $, Note that the matrices  $ A_N, B_N \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times N} $ are lower triangular band Toeplitz matrices with lower bandwidth  $ \mu $. We recall that a matrix is said to be Toeplitz if its entries are constant along its diagonals. Moreover, the linear system (9.3) can be solved easily by forward recursion. A classical example of IVM is the second order backward differentiation formula (BDF),

 $ 3y_{m+2} - 4y_{m+1} + y_m = 2h f_{m+1} $,

which is a two-step method with  $ \alpha_0 = 1 $,  $ \alpha_1 = -4 $,  $ \alpha_2 = 3 $ and  $ \beta_1 = 2 $.

Instead of using an IVM with  $ \mu $ initial conditions for solving (9.1), we can also use the so-called boundary value methods (BVMs). Given  $ \nu_1, \nu_2 \geq 0 $ such that  $ \nu_1 + \nu_2 = \mu $, then the corresponding BVM requires  $ \nu_1 $ initial additional conditions

 $ y_0, y_1, \ldots, y_{\nu_1-1} $,

9.2 Background of BVMs

147

and \nu_2 final additional conditions

\gamma_N, \gamma_N+1, \ldots, \gamma_N+\nu_2-1,

which are called (\nu_1, \nu_2)-boundary conditions. Note that the class of BVMs contains the class of IVMs (i.e., \nu_1 = \mu, \nu_2 = 0).

The discrete problem generated by a \mu-step BVM with (\nu_1, \nu_2)-boundary conditions can be written in the following matrix form

 $$ 

\begin{bmatrix}

\sum_{i=0}^{\nu_1-1} (\alpha_i y_i - h \beta_i f_i) \\

\alpha_0 y_{\nu_1-1} - h \beta_0 f_{\nu_1-1} \\

\alpha_\mu y_N - h \beta_\mu f_N \\

\sum_{i=1}^{\nu_2} (\alpha_{\nu_1+i} y_{N-1+i} - h \beta_{\nu_1+i} f_{N-1+i})

\end{bmatrix}

 $$ 

where

 $ \mathbf{y} = [y_{\nu_1}, y_{\nu_1+1}, \ldots, y_{N-1}]^T $, \quad \quad \quad \quad \quad \quad \quad f = [f_{\nu_1}, f_{\nu_1+1}, \ldots, f_{N-1}]^T $,

 $ A $ and  $ B \in \mathbb{R}^{(N-\nu_1) \times (N-\nu_1)} $ are defined as follows,

 $$ 

\left[ \begin{array}{c|ccc}

\alpha_{\nu_1} & \cdots & \alpha_\mu & 0 \\

\vdots & & & \\

\alpha_0 & & & \alpha_\mu \\

0 & & & \vdots

\end{array} \right] \cdot B = 0

 $$ 

 $ \beta_{\nu_1} \quad \cdots \quad \beta_\mu \quad 0 \\

\vdots & & & \\

\beta_0 & & & \beta_\mu \quad (9.4) \\

0 & & & \beta_0 \quad \cdots \quad \beta_{\nu_1} \end{array}  $$ 

Note that the coefficient matrices are band Toeplitz with lower bandwidth \nu_1 and upper bandwidth \nu_2. An example of BVMs is the third order generalized backward differentiation formula (GBDF),

 $ 2y_{m+1} + 3y_m - 6y_{m-1} + y_{m-2} = 6h f_m $,

which is a three-step method with (2, 1)-boundary conditions where

 $ \alpha_0 = 1 $,  $ \alpha_1 = -6 $,  $ \alpha_2 = 3 $,  $ \alpha_3 = 2 $,  $ \beta_2 = 6 $.

148

Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

Although IVMs are more efficient than BVMs (which cannot be solved by forward recursion), the advantage in using BVMs over IVMs comes from their stability properties. For example, the usual BDF are not A-stable for  $ \mu > 2 $ but the GBDF are  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable for any  $ \mu \geq 1 $. See for instance [1] and [10, p. 79 and Figures 5.1–5.3].

9.2.2 Block-BVMs and their matrix forms

Let  $ \mu = \nu_1 + \nu_2 $. By using the  $ \mu $-step block-BVM based on LMF over a uniform mesh  $ h = (T - t_0)/s $ for solving (9.1), we have:

 $ \sum_{i=-\nu_1}^{\nu_2} \alpha_{i+\nu_1} \mathbf{y}_{m+i} = h \sum_{i=-\nu_1}^{\nu_2} \beta_{i+\nu_1} \mathbf{f}_{m+i} $, \quad m =  $ \nu_1, \ldots, s - \nu_2 $. (9.5)

Here,  $ \mathbf{y}_m $ is the discrete approximation to  $ \mathbf{y}(t_m) $,

 $ \mathbf{f}_m = J_n \mathbf{y}_m + \mathbf{g}_m $, \quad \quad \mathbf{g}_m = \mathbf{g}(t_m) $.

Also, (9.5) requires  $ \nu_1 $ initial conditions and  $ \nu_2 $ final conditions which are provided by the following  $ \mu - 1 $ additional equations:

 $ \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \alpha_i^{(j)} \mathbf{y}_i = h \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \beta_i^{(j)} \mathbf{f}_i $, \quad j = 1, ...,  $ \nu_1 - 1 $, (9.6)

and

 $ \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \alpha_{\mu-i}^{(j)} \mathbf{y}_{s-i} = h \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \beta_{\mu-i}^{(j)} \mathbf{f}_{s-i} $, \quad j = s -  $ \nu_2 + 1 $, ..., s. (9.7)

The coefficients  $ \{\alpha^{(j)}\} $,  $ \{\beta^{(j)}\} $ in (9.6) and (9.7) should be chosen such that the truncation errors for these initial and final conditions are of the same order as that in (9.5). By combining (9.5), (9.6), (9.7) and the initial condition  $ \mathbf{y}(t_0) = \mathbf{y}_0 = \mathbf{z} $, the discrete system of (9.1) is given by the following block form

 $ \mathbf{M}\mathbf{y} \equiv (\widetilde{A} \otimes I_n - h \widetilde{B} \otimes J_n)\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{e}_1 \otimes \mathbf{z} + h (\widetilde{B} \otimes I_n)\mathbf{g} $. (9.8)

Here

 $ \mathbf{e}_1 = [1, 0, ..., 0]^T \in \mathbb{R}^{s+1} $, \quad \quad \mathbf{y} = [\mathbf{y}_0^T, ..., \mathbf{y}_s^T]^T \in \mathbb{R}^{(s+1)n} $,

 $ \mathbf{g} = [\mathbf{g}_0^T, ..., \mathbf{g}_s^T]^T \in \mathbb{R}^{(s+1)n} $,

9.2 Background of BVMs

149

 $ \widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B} \in \mathbb{R}^{(s+1) \times (s+1)} $ given by:

 $ \begin{bmatrix}

1 & \cdots & 0 \\

\alpha_0^{(1)} & \cdots & \alpha_\mu^{(1)} \\

\vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\

\alpha_0^{(\nu_1-1)} & \cdots & \alpha_\mu^{(\nu_1-1)} \\

\alpha_0 & \cdots & \alpha_\mu \\

\alpha_0 & \cdots & \alpha_\mu

\end{bmatrix}

 $

 $ \widetilde{A} = $

 $ \begin{bmatrix}

0 & \alpha_0 & \cdots & \alpha_\mu \\

0 & \alpha_0^{(s-\nu_2+1)} & \cdots & \alpha_\mu^{(s-\nu_2+1)} \\

\vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\

\alpha_0^{(s)} & \cdots & \alpha_\mu^{(s)} \\

\beta_0^{(1)} & \cdots & \beta_\mu^{(1)} \\

\vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\

\beta_0^{(\nu_1-1)} & \cdots & \beta_\mu^{(\nu_1-1)} \\

\beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu

\end{bmatrix}

 $

 $ \widetilde{B} = $

 $ \begin{bmatrix}

0 & \alpha_0 & \cdots & \alpha_\mu \\

0 & \alpha_0^{(s-\nu_2+1)} & \cdots & \alpha_\mu^{(s-\nu_2+1)} \\

\vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\

\alpha_0 & \cdots & \alpha_\mu

\end{bmatrix}

 $

 $ \beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu \\

\beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu \\

0 & \alpha_0 & \cdots & \alpha_\mu \\

\beta_0^{(s-\nu_2+1)} & \cdots & \beta_\mu^{(s-\nu_2+1)} \\

\vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\

\beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu

\end{bmatrix} $

and “ $ \otimes $” is the tensor product.

We recall that the tensor product of  $ A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} $ and  $ B \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q} $ is defined as follows:

150

Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

 $$ 

\begin{bmatrix}

a_{11}B & a_{12}B & \cdots & a_{1n}B \\

a_{21}B & a_{22}B & \cdots & a_{2n}B \\

\vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\

a_{m1}B & a_{m2}B & \cdots & a_{mn}B

\end{bmatrix}

 $$ 

which is an mp-by-nq matrix. The basic properties of the tensor product can be found in [32, 42].

We remark that usually the linear system (9.8) is large and sparse (with band-structure), and solving it is a major problem in the application of the BVMs. We use the GMRES method in Chapter 6 for solving (9.8). In order to speed up the convergence rate of the GMRES iterations, we use a preconditioner  $ S $ called the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner.

9.3 Strang-type preconditioner for ODEs

Now, we construct the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner  $ S $ for solving (9.8). We show that the main advantages of the Strang-type preconditioner are:

(1)  $ S $ is invertible if an  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable BVM is used.

(2) The spectrum of the preconditioned system is clustered.

(3) The operation cost for each iteration of the preconditioned GMRES method is smaller than that of direct solvers.

9.3.1 Construction of preconditioner

We first recall the definition of Strang's circulant preconditioner for Toeplitz matrices. Given any Toeplitz matrix

 $$  T_l = [t_{i-j}]_{i,j=1}^{l} = [t_q],  $$ 

Strang's preconditioner  $ s(T_l) $ is a circulant matrix with diagonals given by

 $$  [s(T_l)]_q = \begin{cases} t_q, & 0 \leq q \leq [l/2], \\ t_{q-l}, & [l/2] < q < l, \\ [s(T_l)]_{l+q}, & 0 < -q < l. \end{cases}  $$ 

See [16, 18, 42, 43, 77].

Neglecting the perturbations of  $ \widetilde{A} $ and  $ \widetilde{B} $, we propose the following preconditioner  $ S \in \mathbb{R}^{(s+1)n \times (s+1)n} $ called the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner for  $ M $ given in (9.8):

9.3 Strang-type preconditioner for ODEs

 $$  S = s(A) \otimes I_n - hs(B) \otimes J_n, \quad (9.9)  $$ 

where

 $$ 

\alpha_{\nu_1} \quad \alpha_\mu \quad \alpha_0 \quad \alpha_{\nu_1-1}

 $$ 

 $$ 

\alpha_0 \quad \alpha_0

 $$ 

 $$ 

s(A) = \begin{bmatrix} 

\alpha_{\nu_1} & \cdots & \alpha_1 \\

\vdots & & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots \\

\alpha_0 & & & \ddots & & \ddots \\

\alpha_{\nu_1} & \cdots & \alpha_1

\end{bmatrix}

 $$ 

 $$ 

0 \quad \alpha_0

 $$ 

 $$ 

\alpha_\mu \quad \alpha_1

 $$ 

 $$ 

\alpha_0 \quad \alpha_0

 $$ 

 $$ 

\alpha_\nu_1 \quad \alpha_1

 $$ 

 $$ 

\alpha_0 \quad \alpha_0

 $$ 

 $$ 

\alpha_\nu_1 + 1 \quad \alpha_\mu

 $$ 

 $$ 

\alpha_0 \quad \alpha_1

 $$ 

and  $  s(B)  $ is defined similarly by using  $  \{\beta_i\}_{i=0}^{\mu}  $ instead of  $  \{\alpha_i\}_{i=0}^{\mu}  $ in  $  s(A)  $. The  $  \{\alpha_i\}_{i=0}^{\mu}  $ and  $  \{\beta_i\}_{i=0}^{\mu}  $ here are the coefficients given in (9.5). We remark that actually  $  s(A)  $,  $  s(B)  $ are just Strang's circulant preconditioners for Toeplitz matrices A, B respectively, where A, B are given by (9.4).

We show that the preconditioner S is invertible provided that the given BVM is  $  A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}  $-stable and the eigenvalues of  $  J_n  $ are in

 $$ 

\mathbb{C}^- \equiv \{q \in \mathbb{C} : \text{Re}(q) < 0\}

 $$ 

where  $  \text{Re}(\cdot)  $ denotes the real part of a complex number. The stability of a BVM is closely related to two characteristic polynomials of degree  $  \mu = \nu_1 + \nu_2  $, defined as follows:

 $$ 

\rho(z) \equiv \sum_{j=-\nu_1}^{\nu_2} \alpha_{j+\nu_1} z^{j+\nu_1} \quad \text{and} \quad \sigma(z) \equiv \sum_{j=-\nu_1}^{\nu_2} \beta_{j+\nu_1} z^{j+\nu_1}

 $$ 

(9.10)

The  $  A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}  $-stability polynomial is defined by

 $$ 

\pi(z, q) \equiv \rho(z) - q \sigma(z)

 $$ 

(9.11)

where  $  z, q \in \mathbb{C}  $.

Consider now the equation  $  \pi(z, q) = 0  $. It defines a mapping between the complex  $  z  $-plane and the complex  $  q  $-plane. For every  $  z \in \mathbb{C}  $ which is a root of  $  \pi(z, q)  $, (9.11) provides

 $$ 

q = q(z) = \frac{\rho(z)}{\sigma(z)}

 $$ 

Let

 $$ 

\Gamma \equiv \begin{cases}

q \in \mathbb{C} : q = \frac{\rho(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})}{\sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})}, & 0 \leq \theta < 2\pi

\end{cases}

 $$ 

(9.12)

152 Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

The Γ is the set corresponding to the roots on the unit circumference and is called the boundary locus. We have the following definition and lemma [10].

Definition 9.1 Consider a BVM with an  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stability polynomial  $ \pi(z, q) $ defined by (9.10). The region

 $ \mathcal{D}_{\nu_1, \nu_2} = \{q \in \mathbb{C} : \pi(z, q) \text{ has } \nu_1 \text{ zeros inside } |z| = 1 \text{ and } \nu_2 \text{ zeros outside } |z| = 1\} $

is called the region of  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stability of the given BVM. Moreover, the BVM is said to be  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable if

 $ \mathbb{C}^- \subseteq \mathcal{D}_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $.

Lemma 9.1 If a BVM is  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable and  $ \Gamma $ is defined by (9.12), then  $ \mathrm{Re}(q) \geq 0 $ for all  $ q \in \Gamma $.

Now, we want to show that the preconditioner  $ S $ is invertible under the stability condition.

Theorem 9.1 If the BVM for (9.1) is  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable and  $ h\lambda_k(J_n) \in \mathcal{D}_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $ where  $ \lambda_k(J_n) (k = 1, \ldots, n) $ are the eigenvalues of  $ J_n $, then the preconditioner  $ S $ defined by (9.9) is invertible.

Proof Since  $ s(A) $ and  $ s(B) $ are circulant matrices, their eigenvalues are given by

 $ g_A(z) \equiv \alpha_\mu z^{\nu_2} + \cdots + \alpha_{\nu_1} + \alpha_{\nu_1-1} \frac{1}{z} + \cdots + \alpha_0 \frac{1}{z^{\nu_1}} = \frac{\rho(z)}{z^{\nu_1}} $

and

 $ g_B(z) \equiv \beta_\mu z^{\nu_2} + \cdots + \beta_{\nu_1} + \beta_{\nu_1-1} \frac{1}{z} + \cdots + \beta_0 \frac{1}{z^{\nu_1}} = \frac{\sigma(z)}{z^{\nu_1}} $,

evaluated at  $ \omega_j = e^{\frac{2\pi \nu j}{s+1}} $ where  $ i \equiv \sqrt{-1} $ for  $ j = 0, \ldots, s $. See [18,29]. The eigenvalues  $ \lambda_{jk}(S) $ of  $ S $ are therefore given by

 $ \lambda_{jk}(S) = g_A(\omega_j) - h\lambda_k(J_n) g_B(\omega_j), \quad j = 0, \ldots, s, \quad k = 1, \ldots, n $.

Since the BVM is  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable, the  $ \mu $-degree polynomial

 $ \pi[z, h\lambda_k(J_n)] = \rho(z) - h\lambda_k(J_n) \sigma(z) $

has no roots on the unit circle  $ |z| = 1 $ if  $ h\lambda_k(J_n) \in \mathcal{D}_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $. Thus for all  $ k = 1, \ldots, n $, and any arbitrary  $ |z| = 1 $, we have

 $ g_A(z) - h\lambda_k(J_n) g_B(z) = \frac{1}{z^{\nu_1}} \pi[z, h\lambda_k(J_n)] \neq 0 $.

It follows that

 $ \lambda_{jk}(S) \neq 0, \quad j = 0, \ldots, s, \quad k = 1, \ldots, n $.

Thus  $ S $ is invertible.

9.3 Strang-type preconditioner for ODEs

153

In particular, we have

Corollary 9.1 If the BVM is  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable and  $ \lambda_k(J_n) \in \mathbb{C}^- $, then the precondi-tioner S is invertible.

9.3.2 Convergence rate and operation cost

We have the following theorem for the convergence rate.

Theorem 9.2 We have

 $ S^{-1}M = I_{n(s+1)} + L $,

where

rank $ (L) \leq 2n\mu $.

Proof Let  $ E = M - S $. We have by (9.8) and (9.9),

 $ E = \left( \widetilde{A} - s(A) \right) \otimes I_n - h\left( \widetilde{B} - s(B) \right) \otimes J_n = L_A \otimes I_n - hL_B \otimes J_n $.

It is easy to check that  $ L_A $ and  $ L_B $ are  $ (s + 1) $-by- $ (s + 1) $ matrices with nonzero entries only in the following four corners: a  $ \nu_1 $-by- $ (\mu + 1) $ block in the upper left; a  $ \nu_1 $-by- $ \nu_1 $ block in the upper right; a  $ \nu_2 $-by- $ (\mu + 1) $ block in the lower right; and a  $ \nu_2 $-by- $ \nu_2 $ block in the lower left. By noting that  $ \mu = \nu_1 + \nu_2 $, we then have

 $ \text{rank}(L_A) \leq \mu $, \quad \text{rank}(L_B) \leq \mu $.

Therefore,

 $ \text{rank}(L_A \otimes I_n) = \text{rank}(L_A) \cdot n \leq n\mu $

and

 $ \text{rank}(L_B \otimes J_n) = \text{rank}(L_B) \cdot n \leq n\mu $.

Thus,

 $ S^{-1}M = I_{n(s+1)} + S^{-1}E = I_{n(s+1)} + L $,

where the rank of L is at most  $ 2n\mu $.  $ \square $

Therefore, when the GMRES method is applied to

 $ S^{-1}M y = S^{-1}b $,

by Theorems 6.13 and 9.2, we know that the method converges in at most  $ 2n\mu + 1 $ iterations in exact arithmetic.

Regarding the cost per iteration, the main work in each iteration for the GMRES method is the matrix-vector multiplication.

 $ S^{-1}M z = (s(A) \otimes I_n - hs(B) \otimes J_n)^{-1} (\widetilde{A} \otimes I_n - h\widetilde{B} \otimes J_n)z $

154

Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

(see Section 6.5). Since \tilde{A}, \tilde{B} are band matrices and J_n is assumed to be sparse, the matrix-vector multiplication

 $ M\mathbf{z} = (\tilde{A}\otimes I_n - h\tilde{B}\otimes J_n)\mathbf{z} $

can be done very fast.

Now we compute S^{-1}(M\mathbf{z}). Note that any circulant matrix can be diagonalized by the Fourier matrix F (see Section 6.4 and [29]). Since s(A) and s(B) are circulant, we have the following decompositions by (6.19),

 $ s(A) = F\Lambda_A F^*,\qquad s(B) = F\Lambda_B F^*, $

where \Lambda_A, \Lambda_B are diagonal matrices containing the eigenvalues of s(A), s(B) respectively. It follows that

S^{-1}(M\mathbf{z}) = (F^*\otimes I_n)(\Lambda_A\otimes I_n - h\Lambda_B\otimes J_n)^{-1}(F\otimes I_n)(M\mathbf{z}).

This product can be obtained by using FFTs and solving s+1 linear systems of order n. Since J_n is sparse, the matrix

\Lambda_A \otimes I_n - h\Lambda_B \otimes J_n

is also sparse. Thus S^{-1}(M\mathbf{z}) can be obtained by solving s+1 sparse linear systems of order n. It follows that the total number of operations per iteration is

\gamma_1 n(s+1)\log(s+1) + \gamma_2 (s+1) n q,

where q is the number of nonzeros of J_n, and \gamma_1 and \gamma_2 are some positive constants. For comparing the computational cost of the method with direct solvers for the linear system (9.8), we refer to [19].

9.3.3 Numerical result

Now we give an example to illustrate the efficiency of the preconditioner S by solving a test problem given in [8]. The experiments were performed in MATLAB. We used the MATLAB-provided M-file “gmres” to solve the preconditioned systems. We should emphasize that in all of our tests in this chapter, the zero vector is the initial guess and the stopping criterion is

 $ \frac{\|\mathbf{r}_q\|_2}{\|\mathbf{r}_0\|_2} < 10^{-6} $,

where \mathbf{r}_q is the residual after q iterations. The BVM we used is the third order generalized Adam's method (GAM). Its formula and the initial and final additional conditions can be found in [10].

9.4 Strang-type preconditioner for DDEs

155

 $$ 

\left\{

\displaystyle \frac{\partial u}{\partial t} = \frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2}, \\

u(0, t) = \frac{\partial u}{\partial x}(\pi, t) = 0, \quad t \in [0, 2\pi], \\

u(x, 0) = x, \quad x \in [0, \pi].

\right.

Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

9.4.1 Differential equations with multi-delays

Consider the solution of differential equations with multi-delays:

 $ \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf{y}'(t) = J_n \mathbf{y}(t) + D_n^{(1)} \mathbf{y}(t - \tau_1) + \cdots + D_n^{(s)} \mathbf{y}(t - \tau_s) + \mathbf{f}(t), \quad t \geqslant t_0, \\ \mathbf{y}(t) = \phi(t), \end{array} \right. $

 $ t \leqslant t_0, \quad (9.13) $

where  $ \mathbf{y}(t) $,  $ \mathbf{f}(t) $,  $ \phi(t) : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}^n $;  $ J_n $,  $ D_n^{(1)}, \ldots, D_n^{(s)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $; and  $ \tau_1, \ldots, \tau_s > 0 $ are some rational numbers.

In order to find a reasonable numerical solution, we require that the solution of (9.13) is asymptotically stable. We have the following lemma [63,64,91].

Lemma 9.2 For any  $ s \geqslant 1 $, if

 $ \eta(J_n) \equiv \frac{1}{2} \lambda_{\max}(J_n + J_n^T) < 0 $

and

 $ \eta(J_n) + \sum_{j=1}^{s} \| D_n^{(j)} \|_2 < 0, \quad (9.14) $

then the solution of (9.13) is asymptotically stable.

In the following, for simplicity, we only consider the case of  $ s = 2 $ in (9.13). The generalization to any arbitrary  $ s $ is straightforward. Let

 $ h = \tau_1 / m_1 = \tau_2 / m_2 $

be the step size where  $ m_1 $ and  $ m_2 $ are positive integers with  $ m_2 > m_1 $ ( $ \tau_2 > \tau_1 $). For (9.13), by using a BVM with ( $ \nu_1, \nu_2 $)-boundary conditions over a uniform mesh  $ t_j = t_0 + jh $, \quad  $ j = 0, \ldots, r_1 $,

on the interval  $ [t_0, t_0 + r_1 h] $, we have

 $ \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \alpha_i \mathbf{y}_{p+i-\nu_1} = h \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \beta_i (J_n \mathbf{y}_{p+i-\nu_1} + D_n^{(1)} \mathbf{y}_{p+i-\nu_1-m_1} + D_n^{(2)} \mathbf{y}_{p+i-\nu_1-m_2} + \mathbf{f}_{p+i-\nu_1}) $,

(9.15)

for  $ p = \nu_1, \ldots, r_1 - 1 $, where  $ \mu = \nu_1 + \nu_2 $. By providing the values

 $ \mathbf{y}_{-m_2}, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{-m_1}, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_0 $, \quad  $ \mathbf{y}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{\nu_1-1} $, \quad  $ \mathbf{y}_{r_1}, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{r_1+\nu_2-1} $, (9.16)

(9.15) can be written in a matrix form as

 $ \mathbf{R}\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{b} $,

9.4 Strang-type preconditioner for DDEs

157

where

 $ R \equiv A \otimes I_n - hB \otimes J_n - hC^{(1)} \otimes D_n^{(1)} - hC^{(2)} \otimes D_n^{(2)} $, \quad (9.17)

 $ \mathbf{y} = [\mathbf{y}_{\nu_1}^T, \mathbf{y}_{\nu_1+1}^T, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{r_1-1}^T]^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n(r_1-\nu_1)}, $

 $ \mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^{n(r_1-\nu_1)} $ depends on  $ \mathbf{f} $, the boundary values and the coefficients of the method.

The matrices  $ A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{(r_1-\nu_1) \times (r_1-\nu_1)} $ are defined as in (9.4) and  $ C^{(1)}, C^{(2)} \in \mathbb{R}^{(r_1-\nu_1) \times (r_1-\nu_1)} $ are defined as follows:

 $$ 

C^{(1)} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ \beta_\mu & \ddots \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots \\ \beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu \\ 0 & \beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu \end{bmatrix}, \quad C^{(2)} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ \beta_\mu & \ddots \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots \\ \beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu \\ 0 & \beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu \end{bmatrix}

 $$ 

We remark that the first column of  $ C^{(1)} $ is given by

 $ \underbrace{[0, \ldots, 0, \beta_\mu, \ldots, \beta_0, \underbrace{0, \ldots, 0}_{m_1-\nu_2}, \underbrace{1}_{r_1-m_1-2\nu_1-1}}_{r_1-m_1-2\nu_1-1} $ and the first column of  $ C^{(2)} $ is given by

 $ \underbrace{[0, \ldots, 0, \beta_\mu, \ldots, \beta_0, \underbrace{0, \ldots, 0}_{m_2-\nu_2}, \underbrace{1}_{r_1-m_2-2\nu_1-1}}_{r_1-m_2-2\nu_1-1} $

9.4.2 Construction of preconditioner

The Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner for (9.17) is defined as follows:

 $ \widetilde{S} \equiv s(A) \otimes I_n - hs(B) \otimes J_n - hs(C^{(1)}) \otimes D_n^{(1)} - hs(C^{(2)}) \otimes D_n^{(2)}, $ (9.18)

where  $ s(E) $ is Strang's circulant preconditioner of Toeplitz matrix  $ E $, for  $ E = A, B, C^{(1)}, C^{(2)} $ respectively.

Now we discuss the invertibility of the Strang-type preconditioner  $ \widetilde{S} $ defined by

(9.18). Since any circulant matrix can be diagonalized by the Fourier matrix  $ F $, we have by (6.19),

 $ s(E) = F^* \Lambda_E F $,

where  $ \Lambda_E $ is the diagonal matrix holding the eigenvalues of  $ s(E) $, for  $ E = A, B, C^{(1)}, C^{(2)} $ respectively. Therefore, we obtain

 $ \widetilde{S} = (F^* \otimes I_n)(\Lambda_A \otimes I_n - h\Lambda_B \otimes J_n - h\Lambda_{C^{(1)}} \otimes D_n^{(1)} - h\Lambda_{C^{(2)}} \otimes D_n^{(2)})(F \otimes I_n). $

158

Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

Note that the jth block of

 $ \Lambda_A \otimes I_n - h \Lambda_B \otimes J_n - h \Lambda_{C(1)} \otimes D_n^{(1)} - h \Lambda_{C(2)} \otimes D_n^{(2)} $

is given by

 $ \widetilde{S}_j = [\Lambda_A]_{jj} I_n - h[\Lambda_B]_{jj} J_n - h[\Lambda_{C(1)}]_{jj} D_n^{(1)} - h[\Lambda_{C(2)}]_{jj} D_n^{(2)}, $

for  $ j = 1, 2, \ldots, r_1 - \nu_1 $. Let

 $ w_j = e^{\frac{2\pi ij}{r_1 - \nu_1}}. $

We have

 $ [\Lambda_A]_{jj} = \rho(w_j)/w_j^{\nu_1}, \quad [\Lambda_B]_{jj} = \sigma(w_j)/w_j^{\nu_1}, $

 $ [\Lambda_{C(1)}]_{jj} = \beta_\mu w_j^{-m_1+\nu_2} + \cdots + \beta_0 w_j^{-m_1-\nu_1} = \sigma(w_j)/w_j^{m_1+\nu_1}, $

and

 $ [\Lambda_{C(2)}]_{jj} = \beta_\mu w_j^{-m_2+\nu_2} + \cdots + \beta_0 w_j^{-m_2-\nu_1} = \sigma(w_j)/w_j^{m_2+\nu_1}, $

where  $ \rho(z) $ and  $ \sigma(z) $ are defined as in (9.10). Therefore,

 $ \widetilde{S}_j = \frac{1}{w_j^{m_2+\nu_1}} \left[ w_j^{m_2} \left( \rho(w_j) I_n - h \sigma(w_j) J_n - hw_j^{-m_1} \sigma(w_j) D_n^{(1)} \right) - h \sigma(w_j) D_n^{(2)} \right]. $

We therefore only need to prove that  $ \widetilde{S}_j $ ( $ j = 1, 2, \ldots, r_1 - \nu_1 $) are invertible. We have the following theorem.

Theorem 9.3 If the BVM with  $ (\nu_1, \nu_2) $-boundary conditions is  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable and (9.14) holds, then for any arbitrary  $ \theta \in \mathbb{R} $, the matrix

 $ e^{\mathrm{im}_2\theta} \left( \rho(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) I_n - h \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) J_n - h e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta} \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) D_n^{(1)} \right) - h \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) D_n^{(2)} $

is invertible. It follows that the Strang-type preconditioner  $ \widetilde{S} $ defined by (9.18) is also invertible.

Proof Suppose that there exist  $ \mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{C}^n $ with  $ \|\mathbf{x}\|_2 = 1 $ and  $ \theta \in \mathbb{R} $ such that

 $ \left[ e^{\mathrm{im}_2\theta} \left( \rho(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) I_n - h \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) J_n - h e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta} \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) D_n^{(1)} \right) - h \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) D_n^{(2)} \right] \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{0}. $

Then

 $ \mathbf{x}^* \left[ \rho(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) I_n - h \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) J_n - h e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta} \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) D_n^{(1)} - h e^{-\mathrm{im}_2\theta} \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) D_n^{(2)} \right] \mathbf{x} = 0, $

i.e.,

 $ \rho(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) - h \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) \mathbf{x}^* J_n \mathbf{x} - h e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta} \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) \mathbf{x}^* D_n^{(1)} \mathbf{x} - h e^{-\mathrm{im}_2\theta} \sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) \mathbf{x}^* D_n^{(2)} \mathbf{x} = 0. $

9.4 Strang-type preconditioner for DDEs 159

We therefore have

 $ \rho(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) - (h\mathbf{x}^*J_n\mathbf{x} + h e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(1)}\mathbf{x} + h e^{-\mathrm{im}_2\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(2)}\mathbf{x})\sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}) $

 $ =\pi\left(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta},h(\mathbf{x}^*J_n\mathbf{x} + e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(1)}\mathbf{x} + e^{-\mathrm{im}_2\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(2)}\mathbf{x})\right)=0 $

where  $ \pi(z,q) $ is given by (9.11). Thus,

 $ h(\mathbf{x}^*J_n\mathbf{x} + e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(1)}\mathbf{x} + e^{-\mathrm{im}_2\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(2)}\mathbf{x})\in\Gamma $

where  $ \Gamma $ is the boundary locus defined by (9.12). Since the BVM is  $ A_{\nu_1,\nu_2} $-stable, from Lemma 9.1, we know that

 $ \mathrm{Re}(\mathbf{x}^*J_n\mathbf{x} + e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(1)}\mathbf{x} + e^{-\mathrm{im}_2\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(2)}\mathbf{x})\geq0 $

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

 $ \mathrm{Re}(e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(1)}\mathbf{x})\leq|\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(1)}\mathbf{x}|\leq\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\|D_n^{(1)}\mathbf{x}\|_{2}\leq\|D_n^{(1)}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}=\|D_n^{(1)}\|_{2} $

and similarly,  $ \mathrm{Re}(e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta}\mathbf{x}^*D_n^{(2)}\mathbf{x})\leq\|D_n^{(2)}\|_{2} $. Note that

 $ \eta(J_n)=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|_{2}=1}\mathrm{Re}(\mathbf{x}^*J_n\mathbf{x})\geq\mathrm{Re}(\mathbf{x}^*J_n\mathbf{x}) $

Thus we have

 $ \eta(J_n)+\|D_n^{(1)}\|_{2}+\|D_n^{(2)}\|_{2}\geq0 $

which is a contradiction to (9.14). Therefore, the matrix

 $ e^{\mathrm{im}_2\theta}\left(\rho(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})I_n-h\sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})J_n-h e^{-\mathrm{im}_1\theta}\sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})D_n^{(1)}\right)-h\sigma(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})D_n^{(2)} $

is invertible and it follows that the Strang-type preconditioner  $ \tilde{S} $ is also invertible.

□

9.4.3 Convergence rate

Now, we discuss the convergence rate of the preconditioned GMRES method with the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner. We have the following result for the spectra of preconditioned matrices [44].

Theorem 9.4 Let  $ R $ be given by (9.17) and  $ \tilde{S} $ be given by (9.18). Then we have

 $ \tilde{S}^{-1}R=I_{n(r_1-\nu_1)}+L $

where

rank $ (L)\leq(2\mu+m_1+m_2+2\nu_1+2) $

160 Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

By Theorems 6.13 and 9.4, when the GMRES method is applied to

 $ \widetilde{S}^{-1}Ry = \widetilde{S}^{-1}b $

the method converges in at most  $ (2\mu + m_1 + m_2 + 2\nu_1 + 2)n + 1 $ iterations in exact arithmetic.

We know from Theorem 9.4 that if the step size  $ h = \tau_1/m_1 = \tau_2/m_2 $ is fixed, the number of iterations for convergence of the GMRES method, when applied to the preconditioned system

 $ \widetilde{S}^{-1}Ry = \widetilde{S}^{-1}b $

is independent of  $ r_1 $ and therefore is independent of the length of the interval that we considered. We should emphasize that the numerical example in Subsection 9.4.4 shows a much faster convergence rate than that predicted by the estimate provided by Theorem 9.4. For the operation cost of our algorithm, we refer to [44, 58].

9.4.4 Numerical result

We illustrate the efficiency of our preconditioner by solving the following example. The BVM we used is the third order GBDF for  $ t \in [0, 4] $.

Example 9.2 Consider

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

y'(t) = J_n\mathbf{y}(t) + D_n^{(1)}\mathbf{y}(t-0.5) + D_n^{(2)}\mathbf{y}(t-1), \quad t \geq 0, \\

\mathbf{y}(t) = [\sin t, 1, \ldots, 1]^T,

\end{array}

\right. $

where

 $$ 

\left[

\begin{array}{c}

-10 \quad 2 \\

2 \quad \ddots \quad \ddots \\

1 \quad \ddots \quad \ddots \quad \ddots \\

\ddots \quad \ddots \quad \ddots \quad \ddots \quad 2 \\

0 \quad 1 \quad 2 \quad -10

\end{array}

\right]

 $$ 

and

In practice, we do not have the boundary values

 $ \mathbf{y}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{\nu_1-1}, \quad \mathbf{y}_{r_1}, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{r_1+\nu_2-1},  $

9.5 Strang-type preconditioner for NDDEs

161

provided by (9.16). Instead of giving the above values, as in Section 9.2,  $ \nu_1 - 1 $ initial additional equations and  $ \nu_2 $ final additional equations are given. We remark that after introducing the additional equations, the matrices  $ A, B, C^{(1)} $ and  $ C^{(2)} $ in (9.17) are Toeplitz matrices with small rank perturbations. Neglecting the small rank perturbations, we can also construct the Strang-type preconditioner (9.18).

Table 9.2 shows the number of iterations required for convergence of the GMRES method with different combinations of matrix size  $ n $ and step size  $ h $. In the table,  $ I $ means no preconditioner is used and  $ \widetilde{S} $ denotes the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner defined by (9.18). We see that the numbers of iterations required for convergence increase slowly for increasing  $ n $ and decreasing  $ h $ under the column  $ \widetilde{S} $.

9.5 Strang-type preconditioner for NDDEs

In this section, we study neutral delay differential equations (NDDEs).

9.5.1 Neutral delay differential equations

We consider the solution of NDDE:

where  $ \mathbf{y} = \phi(t) $,  $ t \leq t_0 $,

where  $ \mathbf{y}(t) = L_n \mathbf{y}'(t - \tau) + M_n \mathbf{y}(t) + N_n \mathbf{y}(t - \tau) $,  $ t \geq t_0 $,

As in Subsection 9.4.1, we want to find an asymptotically stable solution for (9.19). We have the following lemma [41,54].

Lemma 9.3 Let  $ L_n $,  $ M_n $ and  $ N_n $ be any matrices with  $ \|L_n\|_2 < 1 $. Then the solution of (9.19) is asymptotically stable if  $ \text{Re}(\lambda_i) < 0 $ where  $ \lambda_i $ ( $ i = 1,2,\ldots,n $) are the eigenvalues of matrix

 $ (I_n - \eta L_n)^{-1}(M_n + \eta N_n) $

with  $ |\eta| \leq 1 $.

Let  $ h = \tau/k_1 $ be the step size where  $ k_1 $ is a positive integer. For (9.19), by using a BVM with  $ (\nu_1, \nu_2) $-boundary conditions over a uniform mesh

 $ t_j = t_0 + jh $, \quad  $ j = 0,\ldots,r_2 $,

162

Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

on the interval  $ [t_0, t_0 + r_2h] $, we have

 $ \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \alpha_i \mathbf{y}_p + i - \nu_1 = \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \alpha_i L_n \mathbf{y}_p + i - \nu_1 - k_1 + h \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \beta_i (M_n \mathbf{y}_p + i - \nu_1 + N_n \mathbf{y}_p + i - \nu_1 - k_1) $,

(9.20)

for  $ p = \nu_1, \ldots, r_2 - 1 $, where  $ \mu = \nu_1 + \nu_2 $. By providing the values

 $ \mathbf{y}_{-k_1}, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_0, \quad \mathbf{y}_{1}, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{\nu_1-1}, \quad \mathbf{y}_{r_2}, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{r_2 + \nu_2-1} $, (9.21)

(9.20) can be written in a matrix form as

 $ \mathbf{y} = [\mathbf{y}_{\nu_1}^T, \mathbf{y}_{\nu_1 + 1}^T, \ldots, \mathbf{y}_{r_2 - 1}^T]^T \in \mathbb{R}^{n(r_2 - \nu_1)} $,

where

 $ H \equiv A \otimes I_n - A^{(1)} \otimes L_n - h B \otimes M_n - h B^{(1)} \otimes N_n $, (9.22)

 $ \mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^{n(r_2 - \nu_1)} $ depends on the boundary values and the coefficients of the method.

In (9.22), the matrices  $ A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{(r_2 - \nu_1) \times (r_2 - \nu_1)} $ are defined as in (9.4), and  $ A^{(1)} $,  $ B^{(1)} \in \mathbb{R}^{(r_2 - \nu_1) \times (r_2 - \nu_1)} $ are given as follows [5]:

 $$ A^{(1)} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ \alpha_\mu & \ddots \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots \\ \alpha_0 & \cdots & \alpha_\mu \end{bmatrix}, \quad B^{(1)} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ \beta_\mu & \ddots \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots \\ \beta_0 & \cdots & \beta_\mu \end{bmatrix} \quad \alpha_0 \cdots \alpha_\mu \quad \ddots \quad \beta_0 \cdots \beta_\mu \quad \ddots \quad \alpha_0 \cdots \alpha_\mu \quad 0 \quad 0 \quad 0  $$ 

We remark that the first column of  $ A^{(1)} $ is given by:

 $ [0, \ldots, 0, \alpha_\mu, \ldots, \alpha_0, \underbrace{0, \ldots, 0}_{r_2 - k_1 - 2\nu_1 - 1}]^T $

and the first column of  $ B^{(1)} $ is given by

 $ [0, \ldots, 0, \beta_\mu, \ldots, \beta_0, \underbrace{0, \ldots, 0}_{r_2 - k_1 - 2\nu_1 - 1}]^T $.

9.5.2 Construction of preconditioner

The Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner for (9.22) is defined as follows:

 $ \bar{S} \equiv s(A) \otimes I_n - s(A^{(1)}) \otimes L_n - hs(B) \otimes M_n - hs(B^{(1)}) \otimes N_n $, (9.23)

9.5 Strang-type preconditioner for NDDEs 163

where  $  s(E)  $ is Strang’s circulant preconditioner of Toeplitz matrix  $  E  $, for  $  E = A  $,  $  B  $,  $  A^{(1)}  $,  $  B^{(1)}  $ respectively. By using (6.19) again, we have

 $ \bar{S} = (F^* \otimes I_n) (\Lambda_A \otimes I_n - \Lambda_{A^{(1)}} \otimes L_n - h \Lambda_B \otimes M_n - h \Lambda_{B^{(1)}} \otimes N_n) (F \otimes I_n) $,

where  $ \Lambda_E $ is the diagonal matrix holding the eigenvalues of  $  s(E)  $, for  $  E = A  $,  $  B  $,  $  A^{(1)}  $,  $  B^{(1)}  $ respectively.

Now we discuss the invertibility of the Strang-type preconditioner  $  \bar{S}  $. Let

 $  w_j = e^{\frac{2\pi ij}{r_2 - \nu_1}}  $.

We have

 $ [ \Lambda_A ]_{jj} = \rho (w_j) / w_j^{\nu_1}  $, \quad [ \Lambda_B ]_{jj} = \sigma (w_j) / w_j^{\nu_1}  $,

 $ [ \Lambda_{A^{(1)}} ]_{jj} = \alpha_\mu w_j^{-k_1 + \nu_2} + \cdots + \alpha_0 w_j^{-k_1 - \nu_1} = \rho (w_j) / w_j^{k_1 + \nu_1}  $,

and

 $ [ \Lambda_{B^{(1)}} ]_{jj} = \beta_\mu w_j^{-k_1 + \nu_2} + \cdots + \beta_0 w_j^{-k_1 - \nu_1} = \sigma (w_j) / w_j^{k_1 + \nu_1}  $,

where  $  \rho(z)  $ and  $  \sigma(z)  $ are defined as in (9.10). Thus the  $  j  $th block of

 $ \Lambda_A \otimes I_n - \Lambda_{A^{(1)}} \otimes L_n - h \Lambda_B \otimes M_n - h \Lambda_{B^{(1)}} \otimes N_n $

in  $  \bar{S}  $ is given by

 $ \bar{S}_j = [ \Lambda_A ]_{jj} I_n - [ \Lambda_{A^{(1)}} ]_{jj} L_n - h [ \Lambda_B ]_{jj} M_n - h [ \Lambda_{B^{(1)}} ]_{jj} N_n $

 $  = \frac{1}{w_j^{k_1 + \nu_1}} [ w_j^{k_1} (\rho (w_j) I_n - h \sigma (w_j) M_n) - \rho (w_j) L_n - h \sigma (w_j) N_n ]  $,

for  $  j = 1,2,\ldots,r_2 - \nu_1  $. In order to prove that  $  \bar{S}  $ is invertible, we only need to show that  $  \bar{S}_j  $ ( $  j = 1,2,\ldots,r_2 - \nu_1  $) are invertible. Let

 $ \Delta \equiv e^{ik_1 \theta} (\rho (e^{i\theta}) I_n - h \sigma (e^{i\theta}) M_n) - \rho (e^{i\theta}) L_n - h \sigma (e^{i\theta}) N_n $

 $  = e^{ik_1 \theta} (I_n - e^{-ik_1 \theta} L_n) D  $,

where

 $  D \equiv \rho (e^{i\theta}) I_n - h (I_n - e^{-ik_1 \theta} L_n)^{-1} (M_n + e^{-ik_1 \theta} N_n) \sigma (e^{i\theta})  $. (9.24)

Hence, we are required to show that  $ \Delta $ is invertible for any  $  \theta \in \mathbb{R}  $ in order to prove that  $  \bar{S}_j  $ is invertible. Assume that  $  \| L_n \|_2 < 1  $, we have  $  I_n - e^{-ik_1 \theta} L_n  $ is nonsingular for any  $  \theta \in \mathbb{R}  $. Therefore, we only need to show  $  D  $ is invertible for any  $  \theta \in \mathbb{R}  $. We have the following theorem [5].

164 Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

Theorem 9.5 If the BVM with  $ (\nu_1, \nu_2) $-boundary conditions is  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable and  $ \operatorname{Re}(\lambda_i) < 0 $ where  $ \lambda_i $ ( $ i = 1, 2, \ldots, n $) are the eigenvalues of matrix

 $ (I_n - \eta L_n)^{-1} (M_n + \eta N_n) $

with  $ |\eta| \leq 1 $, then for any  $ \theta \in \mathbb{R} $, the matrix  $ D $ defined by (9.24) is invertible. It follows that the Strang-type preconditioner  $ \bar{S} $ defined as in (9.23) is also invertible.

Proof Let

 $ U \equiv (I_n - e^{-im\theta} L_n)^{-1} (M_n + e^{-im\theta} N_n) $.

Then  $ D $ can be written as

 $ D = \rho(z) I_n - h U \sigma(z) $.

Note that the eigenvalues of  $ D $ are given by

 $ \lambda_i(D) = \rho(z) - h \lambda_i(U) \sigma(z) $, \quad i = 1, ..., n,

where  $ \lambda_i(U) (i = 1, 2, \ldots, n) $ denote the eigenvalues of  $ U $. Since we know that

 $ \operatorname{Re}[\lambda_i(U)] < 0 $, \quad i = 1, ..., n,

it follows that  $ h \lambda_i(U) \in \mathbb{C}^- $. Note that the BVM is  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stable and then we have

 $ h \lambda_i(U) \in \mathbb{C}^-\subseteq \mathcal{D}_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $.

Therefore, the  $ A_{\nu_1, \nu_2} $-stability polynomial defined by (9.11)

 $ \pi[z, h \lambda_i(U)] \equiv \rho(z) - h \lambda_i(U) \sigma(z) $

has no roots on the unit circle  $ |z| = 1 $. Thus, for any  $ |z| = 1 $, we have

 $ \lambda_i(D) = \rho(z) - h \lambda_i(U) \sigma(z) = \pi[z, h \lambda_i(U)] \neq 0 $, \quad i = 1, ..., n.

It follows that  $ D $ is invertible. Therefore, the Strang-type preconditioner  $ \bar{S} $ defined as in (9.23) is also invertible.

9.5.3 Convergence rate

We have the following result for the spectra of preconditioned matrices [5].

Theorem 9.6 Let  $ H $ be given by (9.22) and  $ \bar{S} $ be given by (9.23). Then we have

 $ \bar{S}^{-1} H = I_{n(r_2 - \nu_1)} + L $,

where

rank( $ L) \leq 2(\mu + k_1 + \nu_1 + 1)n $.

9.5 Strang-type preconditioner for NDDEs

165

By Theorems 6.13 and 9.6, when the GMRES method is applied to

 $ \bar{S}^{-1}Hy = \bar{S}^{-1}b $,

the method converges in at most  $ 2(\mu + k_1 + \nu_1 + 1)n + 1 $ iterations in exact arithmetic.

We observe from Theorem 9.6 that if the step size  $ h = \tau/k_1 $ is fixed, the number of iterations for convergence of the GMRES method, when applied for solving

 $ \bar{S}^{-1}Hy = \bar{S}^{-1}b $,

is independent of  $ r_2 $, i.e., the length of the interval that we considered.

9.6 Strang-type preconditioner for SPDDEs

In this section, we study the solution of singular perturbation delay differential equations (SPDDEs).

9.6.1 Singular perturbation delay differential equations

We consider the solution of SPDDE:

 $$ 

\left\{

\begin{aligned}

& \mathbf{x}'(t) = V^{(1)}\mathbf{x}(t) + V^{(2)}\mathbf{x}(t-\tau) + C^{(1)}\mathbf{y}(t) + C^{(2)}\mathbf{y}(t-\tau), \\

& \mathbf{\epsilon}\mathbf{y}'(t) = F^{(1)}\mathbf{x}(t) + F^{(2)}\mathbf{x}(t-\tau) + G^{(1)}\mathbf{y}(t) + G^{(2)}\mathbf{y}(t-\tau), \\

& \mathbf{x}(t) = \phi(t),

\end{aligned}

\right. \quad t \geq t_0,

where

 $ \mathbf{x}(t), \phi(t) : \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^m $; \quad \mathbf{y}(t), \psi(t) : \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^n $;

 $ V^{(1)}, V^{(2)} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m} $; \quad  $ C^{(1)}, C^{(2)} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n} $;

 $ F^{(1)}, F^{(2)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times m} $; \quad  $ G^{(1)}, G^{(2)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n} $;

and \tau > 0, 0 < \epsilon \ll 1 are constants. We can rewrite SPDDE (9.25) as the following initial value problem [45]:

 $ \left\{

\begin{aligned}

\mathbf{z}'(t) &= P\mathbf{z}(t) + Q\mathbf{z}(t-\tau), \\

\mathbf{z}(t) &= \left[ \begin{array}{c}

\phi(t) \\

\psi(t)

\end{array} \right],

\end{aligned}

\right. \quad t \leq t_0,

\quad t \leq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

\quad t \geq t_0,

9.6 Strang-type preconditioner for SPDDEs 167

where  $ P $ and  $ Q \in \mathbb{R}^{(m+n) \times (m+n)} $ are defined as follows,

 $ P = \begin{bmatrix} V^{(1)} \\ \epsilon^{-1} F^{(1)} \\ \epsilon^{-1} G^{(1)} \end{bmatrix} $,  $ Q = \begin{bmatrix} V^{(2)} \\ \epsilon^{-1} F^{(2)} \end{bmatrix} $,  $ \epsilon^{-1} G^{(2)} $

Let

 $ h = \tau / k_2 $

be the step size where  $ k_2 $ is a positive integer. For (9.26), by using a BVM with  $ (\nu_1, \nu_2) $-boundary conditions over a uniform mesh

 $ t_j = t_0 + jh $,  $ j = 0, ..., v $,

on the interval  $ [t_0, t_0 + vh] $, we have

 $ \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \alpha_i \mathbf{z}_p + i - \nu_1 = h \sum_{i=0}^{\mu} \beta_i (P \mathbf{z}_{p+i - \nu_1} + Q \mathbf{z}_{p+i - \nu_1 - k_2}) $, (9.27)

for  $ p = \nu_1, ..., v - 1 $, where  $ \mu = \nu_1 + \nu_2 $. By providing the values

 $ \mathbf{z}_{-k_2}, ..., \mathbf{z}_0 $,  $ \mathbf{z}_{1}, ..., \mathbf{z}_{\nu_1-1} $,  $ \mathbf{z}_v, ..., \mathbf{z}_{v+\nu_2-1} $, (9.28)

(9.27) can be written in a matrix form as

 $ K\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{b} $, (9.29)

where

 $ K \equiv A \otimes I_{m+n} - hB \otimes P - hU \otimes Q $. (9.30)

The vector  $ \mathbf{v} $ is defined by

 $ \mathbf{v} = [\mathbf{z}_{\nu_1}^T, \mathbf{z}_{\nu_1+1}^T, ..., \mathbf{z}_{\nu-1}^T]^T \in \mathbb{R}^{(m+n)(v-\nu_1)} $.

The right-hand side  $ \mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^{(m+n)(v-\nu_1)} $ of (9.29) depends on the boundary values and the coefficients of the method. The matrices  $ A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{(v-\nu_1) \times (v-\nu_1)} $ in (9.30) are defined as in (9.4) and  $ U \in \mathbb{R}^{(v-\nu_1) \times (v-\nu_1)} $ in (9.30) is defined as the matrix  $ C^{(1)} $ in (9.17).

9.6.2 Construction of preconditioner

The Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner can be constructed for solving (9.29):

 $ \widehat{S} \equiv s(A) \otimes I_{m+n} - hs(B) \otimes P - hs(U) \otimes Q $, (9.31)

where  $ s(E) $ is Strang's circulant preconditioner of matrix  $ E $, for  $ E = A, B, U $ respectively. We have the following theorem for the invertibility of our preconditioner  $ \widehat{S} $ and for the convergence rate of our method [45].

168

Chapter 9 Applications in ODEs and DDEs

Theorem 9.7 If the BVM with (\nu_1, \nu_2)-boundary conditions is A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}-stable,

 $ \eta(P) \equiv \frac{1}{2} \lambda_{\max}(P + P^T) < 0, \quad \eta(P) + \| Q \|_2 < 0, $

then the Strang-type preconditioner \widehat{S} defined by (9.31) is invertible. Moreover, when the GMRES method is applied to

\widehat{S}^{-1} K \mathbf{v} = \widehat{S}^{-1} \mathbf{b}, $

the method converges in at most O(m + n) iterations in exact arithmetic.

 $ F = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix}_{1 \times 1} \quad \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -2 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & -2 \end{bmatrix}_{n \times n} $

and

\begin{bmatrix}

2 & \ddots & \ddots \\

Chapter 10

Applications in FDEs

In this chapter, we briefly study the Crank-Nicolson weighted and shifted Grunwald difference (CN-WSGD) scheme for solving two-dimensional space-fractional diffusion equations. This method requires the solution of one or more nonsymmetric, large and full linear systems. The GMRES method introduced in Chapter 6 together with restart strategy and preconditioning techniques is used for solving these linear systems. One of the main results is that when the restarted GMRES method is applied to the preconditioned systems, the computational cost is \mathcal{O}(N^2 \log N) at each time step, where N = N_1 = N_2 is the number of spatial partitions in x-direction and y-direction (see Section 10.2). For a literature on fractional diffusion equations (FDEs), one can refer to [7, 31, 56, 60, 68].

10.1 Introduction

We consider the following initial boundary value problem of two-dimensional space-fractional diffusion equation [62, 89]:

 $$ 

\left\{

\begin{array}{l}

\frac{\partial u(x, y, t)}{\partial t} = d_+(x, y, t)_a D_x^\alpha u(x, y, t) + d_-(x, y, t)_x D_b^\alpha u(x, y, t) \\

+ e_+(x, y, t)_c D_y^\beta u(x, y, t) + e_-(x, y, t)_y D_d^\beta u(x, y, t) + f(x, y, t),

\end{array}

\right.

 $$ 

 $ (x, y, t) \in \Omega \times (0, T], u(x, y, 0) = u_0(x, y), (x, y) \in \overline{\Omega}, u(x, y, t) = 0, (x, y, t) \in \partial \Omega \times [0, T], (10.1) $

where  $ 1 < \alpha, \beta < 2 $,  $ d_\pm(x, y, t), e_\pm(x, y, t) \geq 0 $, and  $ \Omega = (a, b) \times (c, d) $. In addition,  $ a D_x^\alpha, x D_b^\alpha, c D_y^\beta $ and  $ y D_d^\beta $ are the left-sided and right-sided Riemann-Liouville

10.1 Introduction

fractional derivatives defined by

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

a D_x^\alpha u(x, y, t) \equiv \frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)} \frac{d^2}{dx^2} \int_a^x \frac{u(\xi, y, t)}{(x-\xi)^{\alpha-1}} d\xi, \\

x D_b^\alpha u(x, y, t) \equiv \frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)} \frac{d^2}{dx^2} \int_x^b \frac{u(\xi, y, t)}{(\xi-x)^{\alpha-1}} d\xi, \\

c D_y^\beta u(x, y, t) \equiv \frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\beta)} \frac{d^2}{dy^2} \int_c^y \frac{u(x, \eta, t)}{(y-\eta)^{\beta-1}} d\eta,

\end{array}

\right. $

Here, the Gamma function  $ \Gamma(z) $ is defined by the following integral [68]:

 $ \Gamma(z) \equiv \int_0^{\infty} e^{-t} t^{z-1} dt, \quad \text{Re}(z) > 0 $,

where  $ \text{Re}(\cdot) $ denotes the real part of a complex number.

To approximate FDE with a left-sided fractional derivative and the FDE with two-sided Riemann-Liouville fractional derivatives, Meerschaert and Tadjeran proposed a shifted Grünwald discretization scheme [61,62]. Their methods were proved to be unconditionally stable. In 2012, the Crank-Nicolson weighted and shifted Grünwald difference (CN-WSGD) scheme was proposed by Tian, Zhou and Deng [79]. Using the CN-WSGD scheme to discretize (10.1), we can obtain a sequence of linear systems

 $ \underline{A}^{(m+1)} \mathbf{u}^{(m+1)} = \mathbf{b}^{(m+1)}, \quad m = 0, 1, ..., M - 1 $,

where  $ M $ is the number of temporal partitions.

The above coefficient matrices possess a special Toeplitz-like structure: it can be written as a sum of diagonal-multiply-Toeplitz matrices [46, 79, 90]. These linear systems are nonsymmetric and large, and the restarted GMRES method is used for solving these linear systems. Numerical result shows that the discretized systems may become very ill-conditioned when the diffusion coefficients are not small and the grid size is small. In order to accelerate the convergence of restarted GMRES method, a preconditioner defined by the incomplete  $ LU $ factorization of a block-banded-banded-block approximation of the coefficient matrix is constructed by extending a banded preconditioner proposed in [59]. One advantage of this preconditioned iterative method is that it requires only  $ O(N^2 \log N) $ operations at each time step by using the Toeplitz-like structure of the coefficient matrix.

172

Chapter 10 Applications in FDEs

The outline of this chapter is organized as follows. In Section 10.2, we discretize the FDE (10.1) by the CN-WSGD scheme, and the matrix form of this finite difference method is presented. Then we construct a preconditioner for the discretized linear systems of FDE (10.1) in Section 10.3. Finally, numerical result is given to show the efficiency of the preconditioner.

10.2 The CN-WSGD scheme

10.2.1 Disretization of FDEs

In this subsection, we use the CN-WSGD scheme to discrete the FDE (10.1). The time interval  $ [0, T] $ is partitioned into  $ M $ subintervals of length  $ \tau = T/M $, and the spatial domain  $ \Omega $ is partitioned uniformly with grid sizes  $ h_x = (b - a)/(N_1 + 1) $ and  $ h_y = (d - c)/(N_2 + 1) $, where  $ M $,  $ N_1 $ and  $ N_2 $ are positive integers. Let  $ x_i = a + i h_x $ for  $ i = 0, 1, \ldots, N_1 + 1 $,  $ y_j = c + j h_y $ for  $ j = 0, 1, \ldots, N_2 + 1 $, and  $ t^{(m)} = m\tau $ for  $ m = 0, 1, \ldots, M $. Let  $ t^{(m+1/2)} = (t^{(m)} + t^{(m+1)})/2 $ for  $ m = 1, 2, \ldots, M - 1 $.

In the following, we consider numerical methods for the solution of the discretized linear systems, which are obtained by using the second-order accurate CN-WSGD scheme with shifting parameters  $ (p, q) = (1, 0) $ given by [79]:

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

L \mathcal{D}_{h_x}^{\alpha} u(x_i, y_j, t^{(m)}) = \frac{1}{h_x^{\alpha}} \sum_{k=0}^{i} w_k^{(\alpha)} u(x_{i-k+1}, y_j, t^{(m)}), \\

R \mathcal{D}_{h_x}^{\alpha} u(x_i, y_j, t^{(m)}) = \frac{1}{h_x^{\alpha}} \sum_{k=0}^{N_1-i+1} w_k^{(\alpha)} u(x_{i+k-1}, y_j, t^{(m)}), \\

L \mathcal{D}_{h_y}^{\beta} u(x_i, y_j, t^{(m)}) = \frac{1}{h_y^{\beta}} \sum_{k=0}^{j} w_k^{(\beta)} u(x_i, y_{j-k+1}, t^{(m)}), \\

R \mathcal{D}_{h_y}^{\beta} u(x_i, y_j, t^{(m)}) = \frac{1}{h_y^{\beta}} \sum_{k=0}^{N_2-j+1} w_k^{(\beta)} u(x_i, y_{j+k-1}, t^{(m)}).

\end{array} \right. $

Here the coefficients  $ w_k^{(\gamma)} $ are given by

 $ w_0^{(\gamma)} = \frac{\gamma}{2} g_0^{(\gamma)} $, \quad w_k^{(\gamma)} = \frac{\gamma}{2} g_k^{(\gamma)} + \frac{2 - \gamma}{2} g_{k-1}^{(\gamma)}, \quad k \geq 1, $

where  $ g_k^{(\gamma)} = (-1)^k \binom{\gamma}{k} $ with  $ (\gamma) $ being the fractional binomial coefficients. For  $ \gamma = \alpha, \beta $, the fractional binomial coefficients  $ g_j^{(\gamma)} $ have the following properties [61,

10.2 The CN-WSGD scheme

173

62, 66, 87]:

 $ \left\{\begin{array}{l}g_0^{(\gamma)}=1,\quad g_1^{(\gamma)}=-\gamma,\quad g_2^{(\gamma)}>g_3^{(\gamma)}>\cdots>0,\\\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}g_j^{(\gamma)}=0,\quad\sum_{j=0}^{n}g_j^{(\gamma)}<0,\quad n\geqslant1,\\g_j^{(\gamma)}=\mathcal{O}(j^{-(\gamma+1)}).\end{array}\right. $

(10.2)

Similarly, the coefficients  $ w_k^{(\gamma)} $ have the following basic properties [79].

Lemma 10.1 The coefficients  $ w_k^{(\gamma)} $ satisfy the following properties for  $ 1<\gamma<2 $,

 $ \left\{\begin{array}{l}w_0^{(\gamma)}=\frac{\gamma}{2},\quad w_1^{(\gamma)}=\frac{2-\gamma-\gamma^2}{2}<0,\quad w_2^{(\gamma)}=\frac{\gamma(\gamma^2+\gamma-4)}{4},\\1\geqslant w_0^{(\gamma)}\geqslant w_3^{(\gamma)}\geqslant w_4^{(\gamma)}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant0,\end{array}\right. $

Moreover, we have

 $ w_j^{(\gamma)}=\mathcal{O}(j^{-(\gamma+1)}). $

Let

 $ \left\{\begin{array}{l}\delta_x^{\alpha,(m)}=\frac{d_{i,j}^{+(m)}\tau}{2}L\mathcal{D}_{h_x}^{\alpha}+\frac{d_{i,j}^{-(m)}\tau}{2}R\mathcal{D}_{h_x}^{\alpha},\\\delta_y^{\beta,(m)}=\frac{e_{i,j}^{+(m)}\tau}{2}L\mathcal{D}_{h_y}^{\beta}+\frac{e_{i,j}^{-(m)}\tau}{2}R\mathcal{D}_{h_y}^{\beta},\\\end{array}\right. $

where

 $ d_{i,j}^{+(m)}=d_{+}(x_i,y_j,t^{(m)}),\quad d_{i,j}^{-(m)}=d_{-}(x_i,y_j,t^{(m)}), $

 $ e_{i,j}^{+(m)}=e_{+}(x_i,y_j,t^{(m)}),\quad e_{i,j}^{-(m)}=e_{-}(x_i,y_j,t^{(m)}). $

The two-dimensional CN-WSGD finite difference method can be formulated as follows:

(10.3)

where  $ u_{i,j}^{(m)}=u(x_i,y_j,t^{(m)}) $ and  $ f_{i,j}^{(m+1/2)}=f(x_i,y_j,t^{(m+1/2)}) $.

174

Chapter 10 Applications in FDEs

10.2.2 Matrix form of the CN-WSGD scheme

To introduce the matrix form of the scheme (10.3), we define the following notations.

(1) Let  $ N = N_1 = N_2 $. The  $ N^2 $-dimensional vectors  $ \mathbf{u}^{(m)} $,  $ \mathbf{d}_+^{(m)} $,  $ \mathbf{d}_{-}^{(m)} $,  $ \mathbf{e}_+^{(m)} $,  $ \mathbf{e}_{-}^{(m)} $ and  $ \mathbf{f}^{(m+1/2)} $ are defined as follows:

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

\mathbf{u}^{(m)} = \left[ \mathbf{u}_{1,1}^{(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{u}_{N_1,1}^{(m)}, \mathbf{u}_{1,2}^{(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{u}_{N_1,2}^{(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{u}_{1,N_2}^{(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{u}_{N_1,N_2}^{(m)} \right]^T, \\

\mathbf{d}_+^{(m)} = \left[ \mathbf{d}_{1,1}^{+(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{d}_{N_1,1}^{+(m)}, \mathbf{d}_{1,2}^{+(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{d}_{N_1,2}^{+(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{d}_{1,N_2}^{+(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{d}_{N_1,N_2}^{(m)} \right]^T, \\

\mathbf{d}_{-}^{(m)} = \left[ \mathbf{d}_{1,1}^{-(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{d}_{N_1,1}^{-(m)}, \mathbf{d}_{1,2}^{-(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{d}_{N_1,2}^{-(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{d}_{1,N_2}^{-(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{d}_{N_1,N_2}^{(m)} \right]^T, \\

\mathbf{e}_+^{(m)} = \left[ \mathbf{e}_{1,1}^{+(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{e}_{N_1,1}^{+(m)}, \mathbf{e}_{1,2}^{+(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{e}_{N_1,2}^{+(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{e}_{1,N_2}^{+(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{e}_{N_1,N_2}^{(m)} \right]^T, \\

\mathbf{e}_{-}^{(m)} = \left[ \mathbf{e}_{1,1}^{-(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{e}_{N_1,1}^{-(m)}, \mathbf{e}_{1,2}^{-(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{e}_{N_1,2}^{-(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{e}_{1,N_2}^{-(m)}, \ldots, \mathbf{e}_{N_1,N_2}^{(m)} \right]^T, \\

\mathbf{f}^{(m+1/2)} = \left[ \mathbf{f}_{1,1}^{(m+1/2)}, \ldots, \mathbf{f}_{N_1,1}^{(m+1/2)}, \mathbf{f}_{1,2}^{(m+1/2)}, \ldots, \mathbf{f}_{N_1,2}^{(m+1/2)}, \ldots, \mathbf{f}_{1,N_2}^{(m+1/2)}, \ldots, \mathbf{f}_{N_1,N_2}^{(m+1/2)} \right], \\

\ldots, \mathbf{f}_{N_1,N_2}^{(m+1/2)} \right]^T.

\end{array} \right



(2) Define the following  $ N^2 \times N^2 $ diagonal matrices:

 $ D_+(m+1) = \text{diag}(r\mathbf{d}_+^{(m+1)}), \quad D_-(m+1) = \text{diag}(r\mathbf{d}_-^{(m+1)}), \quad E_+(m+1) = \text{diag}(s\mathbf{e}_+^{(m+1)}), \quad E_-(m+1) = \text{diag}(s\mathbf{e}_-^{(m+1)}), \quad E_-(m+1) = \text{diag}(s\mathbf{e}_+^{(m+1)}) $,

where  $ r = \frac{\tau}{2h_x^\alpha} $ and  $ s = \frac{\tau}{2h_y^\beta} $.

(3) Define the Toeplitz matrices  $ A_Q^\gamma, L $ and  $ A_Q^\gamma, R $ by

 $ \left[ \begin{array}{cccc} w_1^{(\gamma)} & w_0^{(\gamma)} & 0 & \cdots & 0 \\ w_2^{(\gamma)} & w_1^{(\gamma)} & w_0^{(\gamma)} & \cdots & \ddots \\ \vdots & w_2^{(\gamma)} & w_1^{(\gamma)} & \cdots & \ddots \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots \\ w_Q^{-1} & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & w_1^{(\gamma)} & w_0^{(\gamma)} \\ w_Q^{(\gamma)} & w_Q^{-1} & \cdots & \cdots & w_2^{(\gamma)} & w_1^{(\gamma)} \end{array} \right], \quad A_Q^\gamma, R = (A_Q^\gamma, L)^T. $

(4) Define the  $ N^2 \times N^2 $ matrices  $ A_x^{(m+1)} $ and  $ A_y^{(m+1)} $ by

 $ \left\{

\begin{array}{l}

A_x^{(m+1)} = D_+(m+1)(I_{N_2} \otimes A_{N_1}^\alpha, L) + D_-(m+1)(I_{N_2} \otimes A_{N_1}^\alpha, R), \\

A_y^{(m+1)} = E_+(m+1)(A_{N_2}^\beta, L \otimes I_{N_1}) + E_-(m+1)(A_{N_2}^\beta, R \otimes I_{N_1}).

\end{array} \right. $

10.3 Sparse banded preconditioner for FDEs

175

(5) Define  $  A^{(m+1)} = A_x^{(m+1)} + A_y^{(m+1)}  $.

Then the CN-WSGD finite difference method (10.3) can be written in the following matrix form

 $  (I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)}) \mathbf{u}^{(m+1)} = (I_{N^2} + A^{(m)}) \mathbf{u}^{(m)} + \tau \mathbf{f}^{(m+1/2)},  $

where  $  I_Q  $ denotes the  $  Q \times Q  $ identity matrix. Let

 $  A^{(m+1)} = I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)}, \quad \mathbf{b}^{(m+1)} = (I_{N^2} + A^{(m)}) \mathbf{u}^{(m)} + \tau \mathbf{f}^{(m+1/2)}  $.

The above linear system can be written as

 $  A^{(m+1)} \mathbf{u}^{(m+1)} = \mathbf{b}^{(m+1)}  $.

(10.4)

Let  $  \gamma_0 = (-1 + \sqrt{17})/2 \approx 1.562  $. It can be seen from Lemma 10.1 that if  $  \gamma \in [\gamma_0, 2)  $, then  $  w_2^{(\gamma)} \geqslant 0  $, and the coefficient matrix  $  A^{(m+1)}  $ is a strictly diagonally dominant M-matrix. In addition, the spectral radius of the matrix  $  (I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)})^{-1} (I_{N^2} + A^{(m+1)})  $ is less than 1. When the diffusion coefficients are time independent, the CN-WSGD scheme [79] is unconditionally stable and the truncation error is  $  \mathcal{O}(h_x^2 + h_y^2 + \tau^2)  $. Compared with the method given in [78], the CN-WSGD scheme is second-order accurate without the Richardson extrapolation scheme.

10.3 Sparse banded preconditioner for FDEs

The discretized linear systems (10.4) are usually large and possess a special Toeplitz-like structure, solving these structured systems as central problems in the applications of the CN-WSGD scheme. The GMRES method introduced in Chapter 6 with restart technique is used for solving (10.4). To speed up the convergence rate of the restarted GMRES method, a preconditioner defined by the incomplete  $  L U  $ factorization of a block-banded-banded-block approximation of the coefficient matrix is used.

10.3.1 Construction of preconditioner

Notice that the Toeplitz matrices  $  A_{N_1}^{\alpha,L}, A_{N_1}^{\alpha,R}, A_{N_2}^{\beta,L}  $ and  $  A_{N_2}^{\beta,R}  $ have a special diagonal decay property: the  $ (i, j) $th entry of each of the above-mentioned matrices is of order  $  \mathcal{O}(|i - j|^{-\gamma})  $, where  $  \gamma = \alpha, \beta  $; refer to (10.2). Thus, we consider constructing preconditioners based on banded approximation of the coefficient matrix. As diagonal compensation is a useful technique in designing preconditioners [2,59], we also consider using this technique.

176

Chapter 10 Applications in FDEs

First we consider banded preconditioners for the discretized systems (10.4). Let

 $ \left[\begin{array}{c} w_1^{(\gamma)} w_0^{(\gamma)} \\ \vdots w_1^{(\gamma)} w_0^{(\gamma)} \\ w_k^{(\gamma)} \end{array}\right]_{Q \times Q} = \left( \begin{array}{c} A_{Q,k}^{\gamma,L} = \\ A_{Q,k}^{(\gamma,R)} = \left( A_{Q,k}^{\gamma,L} \right)^{T}, \\ A_{Q,k}^{(\gamma,R)} = \left( A_{Q,k}^{\gamma,L} \right)^{T} \end{array} \right) \begin{array}{c} w_0^{(\gamma)} \\ w_0^{(\gamma)} \\ w_k^{(\gamma)} \end{array} \cdots \begin{array}{c} w_0^{(\alpha)} \\ w_1^{(\gamma)} \\ w_1^{(\gamma)} \end{array} \bigg]_{Q \times Q} \quad  $

Let  $  C_{Q}^{(\gamma)}  $ be the diagonal matrices defined by

 $ \left[ \begin{array}{c} 0 \\ 0 \\ C_{Q}^{\gamma} = \\ \sum_{j=k+1}^{Q} w_{j}^{(\gamma)} \end{array} \right]_{Q \times Q} \quad \left( \begin{array}{c} A_{Q,k}^{\gamma,R} = \left( A_{Q,k}^{\gamma,L} \right)^{T}, \\ A_{Q,k}^{(\gamma,R)} = \left( A_{Q,k}^{\gamma,L} \right)^{T} \end{array} \right) $

The matrices  $  A_{x,k}^{(m+1)}  $ and  $  A_{y,k}^{(m+1)}  $ are defined by

 $ \left\{ \begin{array}{c} A_{x,k}^{(m+1)} = D_{+}^{(m+1)} \left( I_{N_2} \otimes \left( A_{N_1,k}^{\alpha,L} + C_{N_1}^{\alpha} \right) \right) + D_{-}^{(m+1)} \left( I_{N_2} \otimes \left( A_{N_1,k}^{\alpha,R} + C_{N_1}^{\alpha} \right) \right), \\ A_{y,k}^{(m+1)} = E_{+}^{(m+1)} \left( \left( A_{N_2,k}^{\beta,L} + C_{N_2}^{\beta} \right) \otimes I_{N_1} \right) + E_{-}^{(m+1)} \left( \left( A_{N_2,k}^{\beta,R} + C_{N_2}^{\beta} \right) \otimes I_{N_1} \right). \end{array} \right. $

Then the matrix  $  A^{(m+1)}  $ can be split into two parts

 $  A^{(m+1)} = A_k^{(m+1)} + B_k^{(m+1)},  $

where

 $  A_k^{(m+1)} = A_{x,k}^{(m+1)} + A_{y,k}^{(m+1)}, \quad B_k^{(m+1)} = A^{(m+1)} - A_k^{(m+1)}.  $

The matrix  $  A_k^{(m+1)}  $ is a sparse banded matrix containing 4k-3 nonzero diagonals of  $  A^{(m+1)}  $ only, while the bandwidth of  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $ is  $  2N_1(k-1) + 1  $. Since  $  w_k^{(\gamma)} = \mathcal{O}(k^{-(\gamma+1)})  $ for  $  \gamma = \alpha, \beta  $, it follows that

 $ \frac{\|(I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)}) - (I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)})\|_\infty}{\|I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)}\|_\infty} = \frac{\|B_k^{(m+1)}\|_\infty}{\|I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)}\|_\infty} = \mathcal{O}(k^{-\varsigma}), $

where  $  \varsigma = \min\{\alpha, \beta\}  $. The relative distance between  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $ and  $  I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)}  $ is very small if k is sufficiently large. Thus the sparse banded matrix  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $ is a good approximation of the coefficient matrix  $  A^{(m+1)}  $. Therefore, we can consider

10.3 Sparse banded preconditioner for FDEs 177

constructing a preconditioner for  $  \underline{A}^{(m+1)}  $ by using the incomplete LU factorization of the sparse banded matrix  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $.

Since the banded matrix  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $ has  $  4k - 3  $ nonzero diagonals only, it is a very sparse matrix. However, the bandwidth of  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $ is  $  2N_1(k-1) + 1  $. It is very expensive to calculate its inverse or LU factorization since the sparsity of the matrix is destroyed. To overcome this difficulty, we calculate incomplete LU factorization with no fill-in (ILU(0)) of the banded matrix  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $. Let L and U be the factors of its incomplete ILU(0) factorization, then both of them have  $  2k - 1  $ nonzero diagonals only. Thus,  $  P_k^{(m+1)} = LU  $ is also a sparse matrix. Since the matrix  $  P_k^{(m+1)}  $ can be a good approximation of  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $, it can also be a good approximation of the coefficient matrix  $  \underline{A}^{(m+1)}  $. For the operation of the matrix  $  P_k^{(m+1)}  $ and its inverse  $  (P_k^{(m+1)})^{-1}  $ on a vector  $  \mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{N^2}  $, we have

 $  P_k^{(m+1)} \mathbf{x} = LU \mathbf{x}, \quad (P_k^{(m+1)})^{-1} \mathbf{x} = U^{-1} L^{-1} \mathbf{x}.  $

Thus, the preconditioner  $  P_k^{(m+1)}  $ is implicitly constructed by using the incomplete LU factorization of the spare banded matrix  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $.

10.3.2 Preconditioned GMRES( $ \rho $) method

Now the preconditioner  $  P_k^{(m+1)}  $ is constructed for  $  \underline{A}^{(m+1)}  $. Instead of solving the linear system (10.4), we try to solve the following preconditioned linear system

 $  (P_k^{(m+1)})^{-1} \underline{A}^{(m+1)} \mathbf{u}^{(m+1)} = (P_k^{(m+1)})^{-1} \mathbf{b}^{(m+1)}  $. (10.5)

We combine the GMRES method introduced in Chapter 6 with restart strategy to solve the above preconditioned linear system. Here we use GMRES( $ \rho $) to denote the restarted GMRES method which is restarted after  $ \rho $ iterations. For an introduction to the restarted GMRES method, one can refer to [70]. For the sake of completeness, we state the preconditioned GMRES( $ \rho $) method [59] with residual checking at each iteration for the preconditioned linear system (10.5). Here we use the following notations: the symbols  $  \mathbf{r}_t  $ and  $  \mathbf{r}_p  $ denote the current residual of the original linear system and that of the preconditioned linear system, respectively. Let IterMax stand for the maximal number of iteration, and  $ \epsilon > 0 $ denote the given relative accuracy of the residual.

At each time step  $  t^{(m+1)}  $, we choose a  $  \mathbf{u}_0  $ as an initial guess for  $  \mathbf{u}^{(m+1)}  $. The ILU(0) factorization of  $  I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}  $:  $  P^{(m+1)} = LU  $ is computed. Then we calculate the residual  $  \mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b}^{(m+1)} - \underline{A}^{(m+1)} \mathbf{u}^{(m+1)}  $ and assign  $  \mathbf{r}_t = \mathbf{r}  $. Initially,  $  \mu = 0  $. In the iteration step, we have

178

Chapter 10 Applications in FDEs

Algorithm 10.1 (Preconditioned GMRES( $ \rho $) method)

While  $ \mu \leq IterMax $ and  $ \|\mathbf{r}_t\|_2 / \|\mathbf{b}^{(m+1)}\|_2 > \epsilon $

 $ \mu = \mu + 1 $

 $ \mathbf{r}_p = U^{-1}L^{-1}\mathbf{r}; \quad \beta = \|\mathbf{r}_p\|; \quad \mathbf{v}_1 = \mathbf{r}_p / \beta $

 $ j = 0 $

While  $ j \leq \rho $ and  $ \|\mathbf{r}_t\|_2 / \|\mathbf{b}^{(m+1)}\|_2 > \epsilon $

 $ j = j + 1 $

 $ \mathbf{w}_j = U^{-1}L^{-1} \underline{A}^{(m+1)} \mathbf{v}_j $

for  $ i = 1 : j $

 $ h_{ij} = \mathbf{w}_j^T \mathbf{v}_i $

 $ \mathbf{w}_j = \mathbf{w}_j - h_{ij} \mathbf{v}_i $

end

 $ h_{j+1,j} = \|\mathbf{w}_j\|_2 $

 $ \mathbf{v}_{j+1} = \mathbf{w}_j / h_{j+1,j} $

compute  $ \mathbf{y}_j $ the minimizer of  $ \|\beta \mathbf{e}_1 - \bar{H}_j \mathbf{y}\|_2 $

 $ \mathbf{r}_t = \mathbf{r} - LUV_{j+1} \bar{H}_j \mathbf{y}_j $

end

where  $ \bar{H}_j = (h_{ik})_{1 \leq i \leq j+1, 1 \leq k \leq j} $ and  $ \mathbf{e}_1 = [1, 0, \ldots, 0]^T \in \mathbb{R}^{j+1} $. The matrix  $ V_j = [\mathbf{v}_1 \mid \mathbf{v}_2 \mid \ldots \mid \mathbf{v}_j] \in \mathbb{R}^{N^2 \times j} $ with  $ j \leq N^2 $ in the algorithm is a matrix with orthonormal columns.

In the preconditioned GMRES( $ \rho $) method, the main computational works at each iteration are as follows:

(1) Compute  $ \mathbf{w} = U^{-1}L^{-1} \underline{A}^{(m+1)} \mathbf{v}_j $.

(2) Compute  $ \mathbf{r}_t = \mathbf{r} - LUV_{j+1} \bar{H}_j \mathbf{y}_j $.

Since  $ \underline{A}^{(m+1)} $ has a special Toeplitz-like structure, the matrix-vector multiplication  $ \underline{A}^{(m+1)} \mathbf{x} $ for  $ \mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{N^2} $ can be calculated very efficiently [16, 43]. Notice that

 $ \underline{A}^{(m+1)} \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x} - D_{+}^{(m+1)} \left( I_{N_2} \otimes A_L^{\alpha,N_1} \right) \mathbf{x} - D_{-}^{(m+1)} \left( I_{N_2} \otimes A_R^{\alpha,N_1} \right) \mathbf{x} $

 $ -E_{+}^{(m+1)} \left( A_L^{\beta,N_2} \otimes I_{N_1} \right) \mathbf{x} - E_{-}^{(m+1)} \left( A_R^{\beta,N_2} \otimes I_{N_1} \right) \mathbf{x} $

and

 $ I_{N_2} \otimes A_R^{\alpha,N_1} = (I_{N_2} \otimes A_L^{\alpha,N_1})^T $, \quad  $ A_R^{\beta,N_2} \otimes I_{N_1} = (A_L^{\beta,N_2} \otimes I_{N_1})^T $.

10.3 Sparse banded preconditioner for FDEs

179

The main costs of computing  $ \underline{A}^{(m+1)} $x are

 $ (I_{N_2} \otimes A_L^{\alpha,N_1})\mathbf{x}, \quad (I_{N_2} \otimes A_R^{\alpha,N_1})\mathbf{x}, \quad (A_L^{\beta,N_2} \otimes I_{N_1})\mathbf{x}, \quad (A_R^{\beta,N_2} \otimes I_{N_1})\mathbf{x}. $

The multiplications  $ (I_{N_2} \otimes A_L^{\alpha,N_1})\mathbf{x} $ and  $ (I_{N_2} \otimes A_R^{\alpha,N_1})\mathbf{x} $ can be calculated simultane-ously by computing  $ (I_{N_2} \otimes A_L^{\alpha,N_1}) $  $ (\mathbf{x} + \sqrt{-1}\mathbf{x}) $, where  $ \mathbf{x} = [x_N, x_{N-1}, \ldots, x_1]^T $ [66]. Similarly, we can also calculate  $ (A_L^{\beta,N_2} \otimes I_{N_1})\mathbf{x} $ and  $ (A_R^{\beta,N_2} \otimes I_{N_1})\mathbf{x} $ simultaneously. By using the FFT, the matrix-vector multiplication  $ \underline{A}^{(m+1)}\mathbf{x} $ needs  $ \mathcal{O}(N^2 \log N) $ operations only.

In addition, since both  $ L $ and  $ U $ are sparse triangular matrices with  $ 2k-1 $ nonzero diagonals only, the computations of  $ L\mathbf{x}, U\mathbf{x}, L^{-1}\mathbf{x} $ and  $ U^{-1}\mathbf{x} $ require  $ \mathcal{O}(N^2k) $ operations only. Therefore, computing  $ \mathbf{w} = U^{-1}L^{-1}\underline{A}^{(m+1)}\mathbf{v}_j $ requires  $ \mathcal{O}(N^2 \log N) $ operations. As for computation of  $ \mathbf{r}_t $, it can be obtained in  $ \mathcal{O}(N^2(j+k)) $ operations since  $ V_{j+1} $ and  $ \bar{H}_j $ are matrices of sizes  $ N^2 \times (j+1) $ and  $ (j+1) \times j $. Thus the computational cost of the preconditioned GMRES method is  $ \mathcal{O}(N^2 \log N) $ at each time step for large  $ N $.

10.3.3 Numerical result

In this subsection, we present a numerical example to illustrate the performance of preconditioned GMRES $ (\rho) $ method versus GMRES $ (\rho) $ method without precondi- tioners. For the CN-WSGD scheme (10.4), we choose the initial guess as

 $ \mathbf{u}_0 = \begin{cases} \mathbf{u}^{(0)}, & m = 0, \\ \tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{(m+1)} \equiv 2\mathbf{u}^{(m)} - \mathbf{u}^{(m-1)}, & m > 0. \end{cases} $

Since  $ \tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{(m+1)} $ is a good approximation of  $ \mathbf{u}^{(m+1)} $ [90], we set it as an initial guess. The stopping criterion is

 $ \frac{\|\mathbf{r}_j\|_2}{\|\mathbf{b}^{(m+1)}\|_2} < 10^{-7} $,

where  $ \mathbf{r}_j $ is the residual vector after the  $ j $th iteration. In our numerical experiment, we set the parameter  $ k = 4 $. Moreover, we choose  $ \rho = 20 $ for the GMRES $ (\rho) $ method. In the following table, we use  $ N_1 $ and  $ N_2 $ to denote the numbers of spatial parti- tions in  $ x $-direction and  $ y $-direction respectively, and use  $ M $ to denote the number of temporal partition. The maximal error between the true solution and the numerical solution at the last time step is denoted by Error. In addition, the preconditioned GMRES $ (\rho) $ method is denoted as  $ PGMRES(\rho) $.

Example 10.1 Consider the equation (10.1) with  $ \alpha = 1.8 $,  $ \beta = 1.9 $. The left-sided

180

Chapter 10 Applications in FDEs

and right-sided diffusion coefficients are given by

 $ d_{+}(x,y,t)=4(1+t)x^{1.8}(1+y),\qquad d_{-}(x,y,t)=4(1+t)(1-x)^{1.8}(1+y), $

 $ e_{+}(x,y,t)=4(1+t)(1+x)y^{1.9},\qquad e_{-}(x,y,t)=4(1+t)(1+x)(1-y)^{1.9}. $

The spatial interval is  $ \Omega=(0,1)\times(0,1) $ and the time interval is  $ [0,T]=[0,1] $. The initial condition is  $ u_{0}(x,y)=x^{3}y^{3}(1-x)^{3}(1-y)^{3} $. The source term is given by

 $ f(x,y,t)=-e^{-t}\left[(x^{3}(1-x)^{3}y^{3}(1-y)^{3})\right] $

 $ +4(1+t)\left(\frac{\Gamma(4)}{\Gamma(2.2)}(x^{3}+(1-x)^{3})-\frac{3\Gamma(5)}{\Gamma(3.2)}(x^{4}+(1-x)^{4})\right) $

 $ + \frac{3\Gamma(6)}{\Gamma(4.2)}(x^{5}+(1-x)^{5}) - \frac{\Gamma(7)}{\Gamma(5.2)}(x^{6}+(1-x)^{6})\right)y^{3}(1-y)^{3}(1+y) $

 $ +4(1+t)\left(\frac{\Gamma(4)}{\Gamma(2.1)}(y^{3}+(1-y)^{3}) - \frac{3\Gamma(5)}{\Gamma(3.1)}(y^{4}+(1-y)^{4})\right) $

 $ + \frac{3\Gamma(6)}{\Gamma(4.1)}(y^{5}+(1-y)^{5}) - \frac{\Gamma(7)}{\Gamma(5.1)}(y^{6}+(1-y)^{6})\right)x^{3}(1-x)^{3}(1+x)\Big]. $

The solution to the fractional diffusion equation is

 $ u(x,y,t)=e^{-t}x^{3}(1-x)^{3}y^{3}(1-y)^{3}. $

Numerical result for Example 10.1 is shown in Table 10.1. From Table 10.1, we can see that the GMRES(20) method requires much more iterations than the PGMRES(20) method. The number of iterations required by the PGMRES(20) method increases slowly as  $ N_{1} $ and  $ N_{2} $ increase.
